#3032. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Global stock markets remain volatile amid persistent geopolitical tensions and concerns about the sustainability of AI trade. Given this backdrop, investors seeking steady income can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios.

Tracking recommendations from top Wall Street analysts can help investors pick attractive stocks, as these experts assess a company's ability to pay dividends consistently.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is a dividend king. The company has increased its dividend for 39 consecutive years. At a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share (annualized dividend of $7.12 per share), CVX offers a yield of 3.14%. Chevron will announce its third-quarter results on October 30.

In an earnings preview note on U.S. oil majors, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Chevron with a price target of $241. The analyst is focused on the company's international growth prospects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa, and the Eastern Mediterranean.

Specifically, Mehta highlighted management's commentary on solid execution in Venezuela, with gross production expected to more than double to nearly 600 kbd (kilo barrels per day) by 2031 via three joint ventures. He also noted a recent oil and gas condensate discovery in offshore Angola and is awaiting commercial updates on the West Qurna 2 and Nasiriyah projects in Iraq.

Furthermore, Mehta expects CVX to report improved productivity and capital efficiency across domestic shale assets. He expects the improvement mainly in the Permian Basin, where Chevron projects advanced chemical treatments to drive about a 10% recovery uplift in new wells and bring down capital spend per barrel by about 25% year-over-year. Overall, Mehta expects Chevron to report Q3 2026 EPS of $4.95, above the consensus of $4.89.

"While unfavorable timing effects and volatile commodity prices may present quarterly earnings headwinds, we highlight healthy U.S. refining margins and strong asset utilization as partial offsets," said Mehta.

Mehta ranks No. 381 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Chevron KPIs on TipRanks.

Kinetik Holdings (KNTK) is a fully integrated, pure-play, midstream company operating in the Delaware Basin. Earlier this month, the company declared a dividend of $0.81 per share, payable on October 30, 2026. With an annualized dividend of $3.24 per share, KNTK offers a yield of 5.82%. Kinetik will announce its Q3 earnings on November 4, with a conference call scheduled the next day.

Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, Goldman Sachs analyst John Mackay reiterated a buy rating on Kinetik stock and slightly raised his price target to $58 from $57. He expects the company to report Q3 2026 EBITDA of $279 million, higher than the Street's expectation of $270 million but lower than the second quarter's EBITDA of $281 million.

The 5-star analyst expects Kinetik's Q3 performance to gain from higher gas volumes after notable curtailments in the second quarter. Mackay also expects the quarter to benefit from higher production and steady commodity margins, even though some lower-margin contracts and weaker Waha spread marketing gains could weigh on results.

Overall, Mackay increased his Q3 2026 estimates to reflect higher volumes, driven by rapid Permian egress pipeline commissioning, stronger throughput at Kings Landing I, and higher M2M commodity prices, offset by increased costs.

"As natural gas takeaway constraints ease across the Permian, investor focus centers on KNTK's YE26 volume trajectory and durability of its multi-year growth," said Mackay.

Mackay ranks No. 569 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.9%. See Kinetik's Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Moving to Crescent Energy (CRGY), an exploration and production company with activities focused in the Eagle Ford, Permian, and Uinta Basins. With a quarterly dividend of $0.12 per share (annualized dividend of $0.48 per share), CRGY's dividend yield stands at 3.56%. Earlier this month, Crescent announced an agreement to acquire Eagle Ford assets from Devon Energy (DVN).

Heading into Q3 earnings on Nov. 2, Evercore analyst Stephen Richardson reiterated a buy rating on Crescent stock and slightly lowered the price target to $17 from $18, citing dilution from new shares.

"CRGY's recent acquisition of DVN's Eagle Ford assets marks a strategic, growth-oriented move, potentially enhancing cash flow performance," said Richardson.

The 5-star analyst noted that the acquisition of the Eagle Ford assets at a net purchase price of $3.85 billion will add about 68 MBoe/d of net production and over 600 net Tier 1 locations. Richardson added that a solid track record of synergies from past deals in the Eagle Ford region increases confidence about Crescent achieving efficiencies with this deal.

Additionally, Richardson noted that the financial implications of the acquisition seem impressive, with a projected increase in cash flow per share of about 9% for 2027 to 2029.

The analyst believes that Crescent is not just expanding its business through acquisitions but positioning itself as a more durable company, regardless of market conditions.

Richardson ranks No. 478 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Crescent Energy Statistics on TipRanks.

#3030. The ERP Migration Blind Spots That Technology Alone Can't Fix

Bruno Billy, President & CEO of APGAR North America, advises organizations on the operational reality of data and enterprise transformation.

Enterprise resource planning (ERP) transformations remain high on the agenda for many large organizations. Mergers and acquisitions, consolidation and modernization are driving significant investment. For SAP customers, the approaching end of mainstream ERP Central Component (ECC) support adds further urgency.

But replacing an ERP is much more than replacing technology. It affects nearly everything about how a company does business, from processing customer orders to managing its supply chain and reporting financial results. The scope makes it notoriously difficult and expensive to execute.

The expense creates enormous pressure to get moving. Teams responsible for different workstreams often start before the organization agrees on how the new ERP should represent the business. Often, those differences start with the most basic assumptions about how the business operates.

Here’s a simple question that comes up surprisingly often during ERP transformations: what is a customer?

The obvious answer is the person or organization purchasing your products or services. Simple enough, right? But consider a manufacturer that sells through distributors. For finance, the customer might be the distributor that places the order and pays the invoice. For service, it might be the business actually using the product. Neither is wrong. Both definitions serve legitimate business needs.

An ERP needs to accommodate these nuances through its business objects, relationships and hierarchies. If implementation teams decide how to represent customers independently, they risk embedding conflicting assumptions in data mappings, system configurations and business processes.

Those conflicts may not become apparent until integration testing or financial reconciliation. By then, resolving them can require substantial rework, increase project costs, delay go-live and potentially disrupt business operations.

You won’t resolve every business requirement before an ERP migration begins, but there are three decisions I’d recommend making before the teams get too far into configuration and data migration.

Start by agreeing on how the business will be represented in the new ERP. For each major data domain, define the business entities, their relationships and the hierarchies needed to support operations and reporting.

This is particularly important when consolidating systems following an acquisition. In one organization I worked with, acquisitions had left the business operating six different ERP environments, each with its own representations of customers, suppliers and products. Supplier records in separate systems, for instance, may refer to subsidiaries of the same parent company. The future-state model should preserve those relationships so procurement can understand total spending across the supplier organization and negotiate accordingly.

Before approving the model, test it against actual legacy records. Can the migration team determine what each record becomes, which hierarchy it belongs to and how it relates to other entities? If the answers differ depending on who you ask, more business-design work is needed.

Not every difference needs to disappear. Some reflect legitimate regional or business-unit requirements. Not every local variation is inefficiency. Some may actually reflect a competitive advantage.

A manufacturer may sell the same product in several countries while using different packaging or meeting different regulatory requirements in each market. I find it useful to distinguish between what should be written in ink and what can be written in pencil.

• Write in ink the definitions, identities and relationships that need to remain consistent across the enterprise.

• Write in pencil the attributes and classifications that must accommodate local requirements.

Make these decisions with future rollouts in mind. The first implementation often establishes conventions that subsequent regions are expected to follow. A model designed exclusively around the first region’s needs can become an expensive constraint on everyone else.

Decide which systems are responsible for maintaining master data and, equally important, who has the authority to make decisions about it.

A customer record might originate in the CRM, while finance maintains credit information and payment terms. Authority may therefore need to be defined at the attribute or business-process level rather than assigned fully to one system. For each major data domain, identify who can create records, approve changes, resolve conflicts and authorize exceptions.

Consider a customer changing its legal name. Who determines whether that requires updating an existing record or creating a new legal entity? If CRM and finance reach different conclusions, the ERP can end up with conflicting information even when both systems are functioning correctly.

Establish these governance processes before go-live and sustain them afterward. Customers merge, suppliers reorganize and businesses expand. Without ownership and change management, the fragmentation you worked to eliminate will quickly return.

It is understandable to worry that business-model discussions will slow an already demanding program, but these decisions must be made eventually. The longer they are deferred, the more configuration, mappings and processes are built on assumptions that may need to be revisited.

Making the decisions early reduces rework and gives implementation teams a common foundation. It also creates value beyond the migration itself. The same business definitions and hierarchies can support reporting, analytics, future acquisitions and even AI applications.

An ERP doesn’t merely store your business’s data. It encodes a model of your business. If you haven’t agreed on that model before implementation starts, the implementation will make those decisions for you. ​

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

#3029. Zebra Technologies’ Record Quarter Came With a 2027 Margin Warning

Zebra Technologies beat estimates and raised its full-year sales outlook. So why did investors hit the sell button?

Zebra Technologies (ZBRA -7.82%) turned in its third-quarter homework this morning, and at first glance, there wasn't much to complain about.

Revenue rose 22.3% year over year to a record $1.61 billion, and adjusted earnings of $4.95 per share topped the high end of management's guidance. Both figures beat Wall Street's consensus estimates. The maker of barcode scanners, rugged mobile computers, and RFID equipment also raised its full-year sales growth target to about 17%, up from 14% to 16% in the Q2 update. Its bottom-line projections for the fourth quarter landed in line with current analyst expectations.

And the stock fell as much as 17% in pre-market trading. Whatever bullish mojo was left over from August's second-quarter report is gone.

Short version: the market skipped past the headline-inspiring numbers at the top of the paper and went straight to the profit margins. Specifically, traders focused on next year's margin trends.

On the earnings call, CEO Bill Burns said fourth-quarter profitability should serve as a "baseline" for 2027. Zebra expects a fourth-quarter adjusted EBITDA margin of about 22%, while the full-year 2026 figure should land around 23.5%. So 2027 starts out roughly 1.5 percentage points thinner than this year. Burns noted that the 2026 figure includes tariff refunds, and that Zebra held margins flat in Q3 and expects the same in Q4 despite memory costs. Footnotes on your asterisks, you know.

Lower margins on deals with hefty customers played a part, but the main culprit is memory chips. Zebra's mobile computers need plenty of them, and the company is a price-taker here. It doesn't set memory prices; it pays them. The full-year memory cost headwind grew to about $145 million, up from $120 million in August. Zebra sold more mobile computers than expected, which sounds great until you remember each one now costs more to build and relies on scarce memory supply. Price increases covered only about half of the roughly $50 million increase in memory costs in the third quarter.

Zebra raised mobile computer prices by about 15% in October to close the gap. Offsetting memory costs dollar-for-dollar keeps profits whole but shrinks the margin percentage. That's why the margin rate dips even when the pricing works.

In a follow-up interview, Burns admitted that there's customer friction over Zebra's price increases. "They don't like it, nor do we."

But it's an industrywide thing, and arguably the right move. Zebra's customers expected price talks in this economy, because the painful component costs are everywhere.

"They said things like, 'Oh, we were waiting for you to come to us,' because others were already out there," Burns told me. Higher prices didn't surprise anybody.

And the headline growth number deserves a footnote. Roughly half of the third quarter's sales growth came from the Elo Touch acquisition and currency effects; organic growth was still healthy at 11.3%. Elo just passed its first anniversary under Zebra, so growth from here will be mostly organic. And according to the CFO, the third-quarter beat largely came from securing additional memory supply. The demand was already there.

There's a bit of irony in all this. Zebra pitches itself as the plumbing between AI and the physical world: the scanners, trackers, and handheld computers that feed real-world data into AI systems. Meanwhile, the AI boom is widely blamed for driving up memory price, squeezing Zebra's margins.

AI is mostly a reason to buy new Zebra hardware today. Software accounts for a low-single-digit percentage of revenue. Still, management sees a multiyear refresh cycle among transportation and logistics customers starting in 2027 and expects next year's growth to hit the high end of its 5% to 7% long-term target.

I asked Burns whether AI is good or bad for Zebra overall. He laughed and called AI "a net positive" for Zebra, though "it's still early days."

At roughly $334 per share on Tuesday afternoon, Zebra trades at about 15 times forward earnings estimates, though those estimates may well come down after this report. If Zebra's price increases catch up with chip costs by late 2027 without undermining demand, this sell-off may look like an overreaction. If memory keeps climbing higher than projected, these bearish investors were simply early.

Anders Bylund has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Zebra Technologies. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3027. 2 High-Yield Dividend Stocks That Just Raised Their Guidance

VICI Properties and EPR Properties both raised their full-year outlooks, and both could be worth a closer look for patient investors.

Real estate investment trusts (REITs) aren't known for generating much excitement during earnings season. They collect a predictable stream of rent, making their earnings easier for analysts to gauge than, say, AI chipmakers. That predictability is a big part of their appeal for income investors, and it's also why a guidance increase from a REIT is worth paying attention to.

With that in mind, two high-yield REITs, Vici Properties (VICI -1.90%) and EPR Properties (EPR +0.00%), recently reported third-quarter results, and both raised their full-year outlooks. Here's why I own both of these high-yield stocks, and why I plan to add even more shares to my portfolio over time.

Let's start with Vici Properties. The company owns casino and entertainment properties, including Caesars Palace and MGM Grand in Las Vegas, just to name a couple. It then leases them to operators under very long-term agreements with built-in rent increases. Its adjusted funds from operations (AFFO), which is a REIT's version of free cash flow, came in at $0.62 per share, up 4.3% from a year ago. And as mentioned earlier, full-year guidance for AFFO per share. What's more, the board authorized a new $2 billion share buyback.

It's tough to overstate how rare a large buyback is for a real estate investment trust. Typically, REITs use all of the cash flow they generate to pay dividends and invest in growth. A buyback is a REIT's management team saying that its own stock is the most attractive investment opportunity it can find.

Vici's quarterly dividend works out to a 7.4% yield at its share price as of this writing, and it uses about 74% of the AFFO management expects this year. That is generally considered a safe, if somewhat conservative, payout ratio for a REIT.

Of course, Vici isn't without risk. Tenant concentration is a big one. Caesars accounts for about 38% of its rent, and MGM Resorts (MGM +1.91%) is also a massive tenant. But this is an extremely solid real estate investment trust with a well-covered dividend, and management says shares trade at an attractive price. To put it mildly, I agree.

EPR owns what it refers to as experiential properties, such as movie theaters, eat-and-play venues (TopGolf is a major tenant), ski resorts, and other entertainment destinations. Its adjusted FFO rose 8.8% to $1.49 per share in the third quarter, and management raised the low end of its 2026 guidance to $5.49-$5.57 per share. The company also increased its investment spending forecast, which is impressive, given that the high-rate environment often makes it tougher to find acceptable investment opportunities.

EPR is a rare example of a stock that pays its dividend monthly, with an annualized yield of about 6.2%. Its payout ratio is only about 60% of AFFO (most REITs are in the 70%-80% range, such as Vici), leaving plenty of room for future increases.

To be sure, EPR has its own risk: movie theaters. Theaters still account for about 34% of its annualized base revenue, and the company is working to reduce that share and focus on its highest-value properties. To be sure, box office performance has been quite strong in recent years. But if theater attendance weakens, some of EPR's tenants could come under pressure.

In a nutshell, these two REITs offer similar yields with different trade-offs. VICI's tenants are among the largest casino operators in the country, and its leases run for decades, but most of its rent comes from two companies. EPR's portfolio is more varied, its payout ratio is lower, and it pays every month, but its properties depend on consumer spending on experiences, which can be cyclical.

Having said all that, both reports gave income investors reasons for confidence rather than concern. I'm happy to keep collecting VICI's dividend, and EPR looks like a solid option for investors seeking monthly income and comfortable with its theater exposure.

Matt Frankel, CFP® has positions in EPR Properties and Vici Properties. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends EPR Properties. The Motley Fool recommends Vici Properties. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3025. Where Will Boeing Stock Be by This Time Next Year?

Investors shouldn't underestimate the stock's potential given the execution of its plans for 2027 and beyond.

Boeing (BA -0.46%) is a fascinating company and an attractive stock for value-oriented investors. Its mammoth $745 billion backlog (including $612 billion in commercial airplanes) gives investors confidence in a long-term stream of income, and this time next year the company could have delivered on two major catalysts that could propel the stock significantly higher.

The two things to look out for next year are:

Management guided toward both events on its recent earnings call.

The 737 MAX production and delivery ramp is critical, and although management recently walked back expectations on the ramp, it did so in a manner that suggests the bar may be set relatively low. Management expects to hit 52 a month in the back half of 2027 and then "closer to the mid- to-high 50s on deliveries in '29."

While noting that deliveries lag production, Boeing will still have roughly two years to hit the "mid-to-high 50s" from 52 a month in 2027. If management can stabilize at a rate of 52 a month next year and then guide toward, say, 57 a month in 2028, then it will represent an improvement on the standing guidance.

The wide-body 777X was supposed to enter service in 2020, and its delay has caused significant issues, not least the cash tied up in inventory and in program work before any revenue from deliveries kicks in. As such, certification and first delivery (on the larger 777-9 variant) next year will be a major de-risking event for Boeing, potentially leading to upgrades to expectations for the program.

There's no guarantee these events will happen, but they look like reasonable assumptions. As a result, I think Boeing's stock could be materially higher next year. Something to consider.

#3023. Is Sandisk's Incredible Run About to End?

Until news and sentiment shift again, don't fight the trend.

Shares of NAND memory chip company Sandisk (SNDK +3.67%) are up nearly 385% since January, but since July, they have tumbled by more than 43%.

For the latest double-digit percentage drop in the aftermath of earnings, blame disappointment in both the magnitude of Sandisk's earnings beat and concerns about a further growth slowdown.

For its fiscal 2027 first quarter, which ended Oct. 2, Sandisk reported around $11 billion in revenue, up 23% quarter over quarter, and up 378% year over year. Earnings came in at $52.60 per share, up 20% quarter over quarter and significantly higher than the 75 cents in earnings reported for the prior-year period. These results also handily beat Wall Street forecasts, which called for revenue of $10.7 billion and earnings per share of $46.67, respectively.

With strong AI data center demand driving up NAND prices, it's no surprise that Sandisk reported $4.8 billion in data center revenue, a nearly 1,700% year-over-year increase. Yet despite these strong numbers, the market reacted negatively. As can often happen with growth stocks, failing to beat estimates sufficiently can create angst among investors. Moreover, while results beat expectations, Sandisk's Edge commercial unit reported revenue of $5.8 billion. That was a 320% year-over-year increase, but it fell short of estimates calling for around $6.1 billion in revenue.

Sandisk's latest sell-off may not be over just yet. Earlier this year, its sales were nearly doubling on a quarter-over-quarter basis, but management is guiding for slower growth next quarter, with revenue forecast to land in the $12.2 billion to $12.6 billion range. That would amount to just an 11% to 14.5% quarter-over-quarter increase in sales.

Sandisk may trade at only 5 times forward earnings, but its low valuation reflects the highly cyclical nature of the memory chip business. Yes, Sandisk's upcoming 8-for-1 stock split could trigger a relief rally, even though stock splits have no bearing on company fundamentals. However, until the current narrative surrounding Sandisk shares shifts toward a more favorable one, I consider it best to not fight the trend.

#3020. Investors are doing just as well this year by shunning AI. How to invest if the tech trade keeps fizzling

A version of the S&P 500 without AI briefly pulled ahead of the traditional index this year and is now neck-and-neck as investors rotate into other areas of the market.

A proprietary Goldman Sachs index tracking the S&P 500 without stocks classified as "AI enablers" was up 12.4% through Oct. 22, edging ahead of the standard S&P 500's 12% gain. The Goldman index is now neck-and-neck with the S&P 500 as tech rallied in Monday trading.

Tech and AI-related companies make up about 45% of the S&P 500. That heavy weighting has helped power the index for years, but it's turned into a liability lately; the S&P 500 tech sector is down about 7% this month while other sectors have held up.

Investors have soured on debt and equity issued by companies like CoreWeave and Oracle. Shares of Meta Platforms sank almost 7% Thursday after it outlined more plans to spend heavily on capex and issue additional shares.

Companies supplying power and equipment for the buildout have been hit hard — names like GE Vernova, Bloom Energy, Vistra and Quanta Services all suffered steep declines during the recent selloff.

Goldman, which launched the measure earlier this year with S&P, uses the S&P 500 as its starting universe and excludes companies perceived to be enablers of AI before applying S&P 500 Index weighting methodology to the remaining universe of companies.

The ex-AI index lagged the S&P 500 for most of the year as investors continued to favor chipmakers, cloud-computing companies and other firms benefiting from heavy AI spending. However, that advantage has been narrowing and it disappeared last week.

Meanwhile, investors are rotating into defensive areas. ETFs like State Street Consumer Staples Select Sector SPDR ETF, State Street Health Select SPDR ETF and State Street Utilities Select Sector SPDR ETF are up 7% to 10% this month. Energy is ahead 10% too, though that's more related to the war in Iran.

Some analysts recommend sticking with non-AI companies for diversification and protection against more pain in tech. The "AI pendulum has swung back in favor of non-AI exposure over the last two weeks," Oppenheimer technical analyst Ari Wald said in a note on Oct. 24.

Two industries that Oppenheimer favors now are commercial services and insurance. The firm upgraded those industries to overweight last week, recommending Republic Services, Cintas, Verisk Analytics, Broadridge Financial Solutions, MetLife and Arthur J. Gallagher.

Berkshire Hathaway is also capitalizing on the rotation out of tech. Its Class B shares are up 8% this month, though it's still lagging behind the S&P 500 this year.

Berkshire owns a wide swath of operating companies, including insurance giant Geico and Burlington Northern Santa Fe railroad. It also has a $364 billion stock portfolio including companies like Apple, Coca-Cola, Alphabet and Bank of America. That diversification may help Berkshire continue to outperform if the tech sector drags down the S&P 500.

Trivector Research sees potential in "non-tech compounders." Healthcare giant Eli Lilly is one idea. The stock is up 22% on the strength of Lilly's weight-loss drugs. The company has more in the pipeline, including a next-generation obesity drug that Lilly plans to file for FDA approval early in 2027.

Viking Holdings, a cruise operator, is also proving durable. The company said on its August earnings call that 40% of its river capacity and 60% of its ocean capacity was already booked for 2027. "Based on recent events, we've seen our guests have proved to be resilient and are continuing to book 2027 and future seasons," the company told investors.

Viking stock is up 15% this year but it may still have upside. The average Wall Street target is $109, above recent prices around $82. Barclays recently upgraded the cruise operator to overweight with a $102 target, citing the company's "compelling risk/reward."

#3016. Alphabet Stock Tanks After Earnings: Should Investors Buy the Dip?

The tech giant delivered underwhelming numbers for its most recent quarter, and its stock is now trading close to its 52-week low.

Shares of Alphabet (GOOG +0.03%)(GOOGL +0.13%) fell sharply last week, after the company released its latest quarterly results. The tech stock didn't quite hit a new 52-week low, but it is trading close to it.

Has the market overreacted, and could this be a great time to buy Alphabet, or could there be more of a decline to come for this top tech stock?

There wasn't a whole lot of positive news around Alphabet's stock when it reported earnings last week. While its revenue rose by 25% year over year for the September quarter, the company missed expectations on both its top and bottom lines, which is something it hasn't done for multiple years.

Revenue of $127.6 billion was well below the $128.2 billion that analysts were expecting, and the gap between adjusted earnings per share and expectations was even wider -- $2.67 vs $3.02. Meanwhile, the company anticipates capital expenditures to hit $200 billion this year, and rise to $300 billion next year.

An earnings miss combined with elevated spending effectively gives investors multiple reasons to be down on the stock, and thus a big drop-off after earnings shouldn't have come as too much of a surprise.

The tech stock is now down more than 10% since the start of the year, trading roughly around where it was this time last year.

Alphabet has an excellent business model that commands high margins and possesses plentiful growth opportunities in search, video streaming, artificial intelligence, and robotaxis.

How it performs during a single quarter is irrelevant in the grand scheme of things. The business itself is successful and highly profitable. It's the type of stock that investors can comfortably hold on to for the long haul.

Today, it's trading at a forward price-to-earnings multiple of around 18, which is based on analyst expectations. That's a great price, given that the average stock on the S&P 500 trades at about 19 times its future profit estimates. Alphabet is a growth beast and an iconic tech giant -- it's worthy of trading at a premium, not a discount.

Alphabet has a fantastic business, and with it trading at a low valuation, it makes for a terrific buy. It's hard to go wrong with owning one of the top tech companies in the world, especially with it looking undervalued right now.

David Jagielski, CPA has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Alphabet. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3013. Coca-Cola vs. Monster Beverage: Measuring Quarter-Over-Quarter Revenue Trends

Monster's revenue has grown 39% over eight quarters while Coca-Cola's expanded just 17%—a widening gap that reshapes the competitive picture.

Coca-Cola Co. (KO -0.42%) primarily earns revenue by manufacturing, marketing, and selling nonalcoholic beverage concentrates and syrups to worldwide bottling partners.

It recently entered a holiday-themed partnership with Gaylord Hotels, while facing a legal investigation regarding a potential class action claim related to a reported customer data breach.

Monster Beverage Corp. (MNST -0.45%) generates revenue mainly by innovating, marketing, and distributing various energy drinks and concentrates globally.

It executed a two-for-one stock split in August, and it unveiled an upcoming product innovation pipeline featuring new energy and high-protein beverage lines during an October trade show.

Revenue here refers to the data provider's standardized income-statement revenue line item, which shows investors the total amount of incoming money a company brings in from selling its goods and services.

Coca-Cola vs. Monster Beverage Revenue over the last 8 quarters.

The first thing to check for when examining a company's revenue trend is seasonality: some times of the year have stronger buying patterns than others. There usually is some seasonality for every business. In the case of Coca-Cola, the warmer summer months tend to generate more sales of its core sodas. Similarly for Monster, the summer months see slightly stronger sales, though attributed less to a refreshment cause and more to people wanting the stronger caffeine hit of Monster for outdoor activities during longer daylight periods.

As the chart shows, comparing comparable quarters makes Coke's sales less volatile, given that there is modest growth quarter over quarter compared to the prior year, such as Q1 2026 and Q1 2025. Monster's is more interesting in that, even with seasonality, the company is posting steady sequential quarterly growth. That suggests stronger growth prospects for Monster than Coke.

It's worth noting, too, that while Coke owns a large minority stake in Monster, it doesn't incorporate Monster revenues into its own accounting, which it would likely have to do if it owned a majority stake.

Revenue is perhaps the simplest metric investors have to judge a business by. As the chart shows, look for rising revenue, especially over similar periods of each year, and take comparable-quarter stagnation, or worse, declines, as a red flag.

Data source: Financial Modeling Prep. Data as of Nov. 1, 2026.

#3005. Knicks Notes: Mike Brown not hesitating to sit Mikal Bridges; Jordan Clarkson providing spark

Mikal Bridges was 1-for-7 in the third quarter on Monday and the Knicks were struggling. Some NBA head coaches would stick with their veteran in that spot. It's easier to keep a player like Bridges -- one who is signed to a large contract and was obtained for significant trade capital -- on the court.

But in this respect, Mike Brown isn't most coaches. He didn't hesitate to bench Bridges in the second half of the Knicks' win over the Nets.

We're seven games into the season. Brown has pulled Bridges in the third quarter of three of those seven games.

"Yeah, he's just got to keep playing. Everybody goes through those ups and downs throughout the course of the season, and obviously, it's the beginning of the season right now, so he's got to find his way," Brown said after New York's win over Brooklyn. "Not just with him but with everybody, when you're in a funk you gotta find one or two things to hold your hat on. Mikal is one of the best runners in the NBA.

"In both directions -- offensively and defensively. So he's just got to try to hang his hat on that as well as his cutting and trying to do the other little things -- rebounding and driving and touching the paint and spraying it. Then this shot will come eventually."

Bridges is shooting 30 percent from the floor so far this year. If you've watched the Knicks during Bridges' tenure, you know that he can be up and down over the course of the regular season. And you also know he can deliver in big playoff situations. Brown believes Bridges will ultimately be fine.

"It's a matter of time in my opinion, before Mikal gets going," Brown said.

But if the Knicks head coach thinks someone else gives the team a better chance to win on a given night, he won't hesitate to sit Bridges.

After a sluggish three quarters, Brown turned to Jordan Clarkson and Tyler Kolek to start the fourth quarter. Clarkson, Kolek, Josh Hart, OG Anunoby and Karl-Anthony Towns sparked the Knicks early in the fourth.

It was the second time in the past three games that Clarkson helped turn things around. The veteran had 18 points in New York's comeback win in Chicago. But he has also basically been out of the rotation in three of New York's seven games.

"I think it shows his character more than anything else. He's someone that if he was on a different team, he could play 20, 25 minutes every night. He could score double digits. He could be that spark, that microwave off the bench. And he's here," Hart said. "He was willing to sacrifice and take a smaller role, be a part of something bigger than himself. So it shows its character.

"It shows his professionalism. To be able to go out and do those things. And when he's not doing it, he's out there coaching, not on the court, he's coaching guys up, talking to them, letting them know what he sees. So he's the ultimate professional. He's someone that you want on your team. And whenever he's given an opportunity, he'll go out and play well."

Brown also had effusive praise for Clarkson.

"You have a veteran that's been there, done that. and he's willing to accept the role that most guys wouldn't accept - even guys younger that haven't proved as much as he's proven in this league. And he's willing to accept it," Brown said. "So, in my opinion, if he's willing to accept it, which is what he did last year, and still try to uplift everybody, connect everybody while he's going through what he's going through, then everybody else should. That's that's the ultimate team player, I will say this about JC. I've been around some good teammates. He's got to be the best. He is the definition of ultimate pro."

Additionally, Landry Shamet led the team in scoring with 22 points off of the bench in 29 minutes. The veteran shot 4-for-6 from three, 6-for-8 from the field, and was a perfect 6-for-6 on free throws.

#3003. Clorox Now Expects Earnings Near the Low End of Its Forecast as Costs Rise. Should Dividend Investors Be Worried?

Earlier this year, it closed a pricey acquisition.

Clorox (CLX +3.00%) published its first-quarter fiscal 2027 results after market close on Monday. At first glance, the company's growth rates looked enormously impressive. A closer read of the numbers reveals its performance wasn't so sparkling.

This dynamic raises a big question, particularly for income investors of this high-yield dividend payer: is Clorox a buy, given how it did in the quarter?

Clorox's quarterly net sales leaped 33% higher year over year to nearly $1.91 billion. Much of this was due to its acquisition of Gojo Industries, the maker of Purell sanitizing products. The Gojo deal was announced in January of this year and closed in April. As Gojo began to be included in Clorox's results in the fourth quarter of 2026, this skewed the year-over-year comparison in the most recently reported frame.

Another factor was the timing of Clorox's latest enterprise resource planning (ERP) software upgrade, implemented in the first quarter of fiscal 2026. Cutting a long story short, this reduced sales that quarter, resulting in a low basis for year-over-year comparisons in the latest set of results.

Those two factors alone accounted for almost the entire 33% growth rate.

Clorox's attributable non-GAAP (adjusted) net income was $190 million. That shook out to $1.55 per share, which, again, was quite the improvement over the year-ago figure ($0.85). That "lapping" of the ERP transition and the Gojo absorption were major factors here too.

The consensus analyst estimates were $1.88 billion for net sales and $1.07 for adjusted earnings per share (EPS). But the company's beats didn't impress investors who had done their homework, as they traded the shares down slightly in after-hours action following the earnings release.

Lapping's impact was apparent in the performance of three of Clorox's four reporting segments.

That applies to health and wellness' 62% surge to $918 million. This is the unit where Gojo has been placed, and its presence contributed 39 percentage points to growth. The remainder came from the ERP transition.

Gojo didn't have any impact on the household unit, which saw a 17% rise in net sales to $423 million. This was "primarily" driven by lapping, as was the 16% jump for the lifestyle division (to $283 million).

Neither lapping nor Gojo (basically a product for the North American market) had much of an effect on the international segment, which posted an 11% increase to $281 million.

The company reiterated most of its annual guidance for fiscal 2027. Its net sales growth forecast remains at 13% to 14% (once more, much of this should be due to Gojo and lapping). The adjusted EPS forecast of $5.70 to $6 per share was maintained; however, the company now expects the actual figure to land closer to the bottom end of that range. It also slightly reduced its expectation for gross margin to 41.5% from 42%.

Clorox's long-term debt rose by 60% year over year to almost $4 billion. No prizes for guessing why; yes, it was the Gojo acquisition, an all-cash transaction with a price tag of $2.25 billion, or roughly $1.92 billion when factoring in anticipated tax benefits. This was funded through new debt instruments, raising interest expense to $52 million from $23 million.

I feel the Gojo deal is an attempt by Clorox to buy a way out of its recent slump. Several negative factors, such as higher commodity costs and increased transportation expenses stemming largely from the Middle East conflict, have affected the company. Meanwhile, consumer budgets are being stretched by higher prices across a wide range of goods.

This has sapped Clorox's share price, with one positive effect being that its dividend has been lifted well into high-yield dividend territory, at 5.6%. And this is a payout that's been raised for 49 years in a row, putting the company at the brink of achieving the rare Dividend King status -- which a stock reaches when the count hits 50.

If Clorox fell short, it would be devastating for investor morale. I don't think that'll happen, and not only because the stakes are high. In the first-quarter earnings release, Clorox revealed that operating cash flow zoomed 54% higher and, while much of that was due to lapping (of course), that shows it can still generate plenty of greenbacks to fund continued dividend raises. Its payout ratio remains high, though, so all investors should keep a watchful eye on that figure. The lofty payout ratio is likely why Clorox's hikes over the last few years have been incremental and thus easy on its cash balance.

Given all that, I predict the company will earn Dividend King status when raise time comes around in 2027. Clorox has its challenges for sure, but if I were a dividend investor, I wouldn't be overly worried.

Eric Volkman has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3000. Nearly Two-Thirds of NextEra's New Backlog Last Quarter Was Batteries. Here's Why That Matters.

NextEra's clean energy business is proving it can adapt to demand.

NextEra Energy (NEE -0.81%) is leaning into the huge growth in electricity demand it expects over the next 20 years or so. To put a number on that, demand grew by 10% between 2005 and 2025, but is expected to increase by 60% between 2025 and 2045. That's a step change in demand, and the giant U.S. utility is looking to benefit in more than one way. Here's why batteries are an important example of the company's strong execution toward that goal.

NextEra Energy is one of the world's largest utilities, but it is looking to get even bigger with its proposed acquisition of peer Dominion Energy (D -0.69%). Dominion has a monopoly in one of the world's most important data center markets, and buying the company would push NextEra Energy into the number two spot in nuclear power. It is a very big deal and could position NextEra Energy well on the growth front if it is consummated as expected (it is facing some pushback from regulators and utility customers).

But for a long time, NextEra Energy's growth was mostly driven by its clean energy business. This division builds solar and wind power assets and sells the power produced under long-term contracts. It isn't regulated, but the long-term nature of the contracts allows the division to produce reliable cash flows. That said, the clean energy industry is evolving, and batteries are an increasingly important part of the picture.

NextEra Energy doesn't plan on being left behind. And proof of that came in the company's second-quarter earnings. The utility added 3.7 gigawatts of clean energy to its backlog, expanding its overall backlog to 36.7 gigawatts. However, of that 3.7 gigawatts of new power, 2.4 gigawatts were batteries. That's roughly two-thirds of what was added during the quarter, proving that NextEra Energy can pivot as needed to keep growing this business.

For years, NextEra Energy grew its dividend at around 10% a year. It disappointed Wall Street when it announced that future dividend growth would be closer to 6% a year for the foreseeable future. A big part of that change related to lowered expectations for the company's clean energy business. But the battery pivot shows that investors shouldn't be too alarmed: that division's growth may not be as fast, but it hasn't suddenly ended. Indeed, the company's battery success suggests that long-term dividend growth investors should still find NextEra Energy of interest.

Reuben Gregg Brewer has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends NextEra Energy. The Motley Fool recommends Dominion Energy. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

Golden State will host the Miami Heat on Thursday, Nov. 5, beginning at 7 p.m. PT (10 p.m. ET).

#2990. As AI becomes more fractured, one name stands out: AMD

As Anthropic rushes to takes its shares public, the AI trade is showing cracks, and they are not confined to fringe players.

We recently wrote about CoreWeave, which fell 15.5% on Oct. 14 as yields on its 2032 notes touched 15%, and its default-swap spreads nearly doubled in October. IREN is down more than 20% this year. Last Thursday, the contagion reached chipmakers, which makes sense since much of CRWV and IREN's spending is going there. AMD dropped 8.1% as Meta's financing plans and a supply warning from Lam Research exacerbated data-center fears about the pace of AI spending.

Because when you screen for factors that demonstrably generate outperformance over time, the list narrows quickly. My screen: price above the 200-day moving average; revenue growing at least as fast as the S&P 500 (5%+ per annum); EPS growing at least as fast (7%+); rating upgrades; a PEG below 3; and ROE and ROIC of at least 12%. Of the names reporting in the next two weeks, AMD is effectively the only one that makes the list. Yes, you can (and arguably should, as our staples article described) position your portfolio with slightly less tech exposure than the broad indices provide, but we can't ignore the market's biggest and most transformative sector entirely.

Especially because the growth in this case doesn't just meet my criteria; it blows past them. Consensus for Q3 calls for EPS of $1.94 on revenue of $13.06 billion, versus $1.20 and $9.25 billion a year ago. That's YoY growth of nearly 62% and 41%, respectively. The recent drawdown came from a sector selloff, not anything AMD reported, but it shows that if some names in the space get hit, even the larger players are likely to feel it. On Oct. 6, Lisa Su said AMD would substantially increase supply in 2027 because AI orders keep outrunning capacity.

So how do you balance factors we know work for a fast-growing company against a backdrop of cracks in the AI trade? Options.

Owning the stock outright means absorbing a roughly 9% to 11% implied earnings-related move (day of). Earnings are about a week out, and over the past four quarters the smallest move over a similar window was more than 9%, and the largest move, after the company reported its Q1 26 results, was 38%! Of course, that kind of volatility means options aren't cheap, and that rich premium is also a reason not to simply buy calls.

A call spread risk reversal does both jobs: it reduces risk if the cracks widen, and the short options offset the elevated volatility we would otherwise pay for. The bottom chart shows that, by sacrificing about $1,700 of upside for $5,000 in outperformance during a severe downside move, and given how jittery this trade has been lately, this is a way to stay in it with considerably less risk. $5,000 less, specifically. Of course, selling that out will tie up some cash, but less so than simply buying the stock. And selling both the out and the call reduces long call purchase by 66%

#2988. Federal Reserve Board issues enforcement actions with former employee of Northstar Bank, former employee of American Express Travel Related Services Company, Inc., and former employee of Regions Bank

#2987. Scouting New York Jets quarterback Will Levis

The Jets recently signed former Titans quarterback Will Levis to their practice squad. Today, we break him down in detail.

The 27-year old Levis is listed at 6'4" and 229 pounds and was a second round pick out of Kentucky in 2023. He has started 21 games in his NFL career, winning just five, and did not play last season due to injury.

Levis was a three-star high school recruit, whose college career began at Penn State, where he redshirted his first season in 2018.

He got a chance to start his first game in the following season but after two years with the Nittany Lions, he had attempted just 102 passes, completing 61 for 644 yards with three touchdowns. He had also rushed for 473 yards and six touchdowns.

Levis decided to transfer to Kentucky in 2021 and passed for 367 yards in his first start – a sign of how far he had come since his first start with Penn State where he carried the ball 17 times and only passed in 15 times.

In 13 starts, Levis completed 66 percent of his passes for 2,827 yards and 24 touchdowns while also rushing for 376 yards and nine scores. However, he led the SEC with 13 interceptions.

Levis' second season with the Wildcats saw him start 11 games and he passed for over 2,400 yards and 19 touchdowns with another two touchdowns on the ground. However, he threw another 10 interceptions.

Heading into the 2023 NFL draft, Levis' stock seemed to be rising, with some reports indicating he was under consideration to be selected first overall. However, he ended up dropping out of the first round altogether with the Titans moving up to select him with the first pick of day two.

Levis struggled in camp and preseason, and began the year as the number three quarterback. However, he got a chance to start his first game in late October and threw four touchdown passes in a win over the Falcons. Several weeks later he passed for 327 yards and two late touchdowns in a comeback win over the Dolphins.

Overall, though, he was just 3-6 as a starter, completing 58 percent of his passes for 1,808 yards and eight touchdowns with four interceptions. He did also rush for one touchdown.

Levis began his second season as the starter but got off to a bad start as the Titans lost four of their first five with Levis leading the league in turnovers. He would miss time due to injury in the middle part of the season and then after a couple of good performances, his play took a downturn and he was benched down the stretch. He ended the season with just two wins and 12 interceptions in 12 starts. He had just over 2,000 passing yards and 13 touchdowns.

Ahead of the 2025 season, the Titans drafted Cam Ward first overall and then it was announced that Levis needed shoulder surgery and would miss the entire year.

After an underwhelming camp this year, indications are that Levis asked to be released at the end of preseason and the Jets picked him up after he cleared waivers.

Let's move onto some more in-depth analysis of what Levis brings to the table, based on in-depth research and film study.

Levis has good size and big hands, displaying athleticism, toughness and power as a runner. He posted a 34-inch vertical and 124-inch broad jump during his combine workout.

Levis' arm strength is regarded as one of his best attributes and he is not afraid to rip passes into a tight window.

He can also be effective on deep throws, and puts a lot of air under the ball, throwing both from the pocket and on the move.

Levis' completion percentage in college was in the mid-60's which is acceptable, but largely a product of the fact he threw a lot of dump-offs. To his credit, he improved his completion percentage from 58 to 63 in his second NFL season.

His ball placement has been inconsistent throughout his college and pro career, but the bigger issue seems to be that he's often late on throws.

With that said, from time to time he will throw a bullseye on a tight window throw.

Levis' pocket presence is another area that holds him back. He doesn't seem to have a good feel for pressure and will often try to escape rather than throw the ball away. He has been sacked 69 times in his 21 career starts but some of them came as he held the ball too long or ran himself into trouble.

The bigger issue for Levis is that when pressure comes, he will often panic and try to make something happen rather than cutting his losses, which can lead to some catastrophic mistakes.

He has had nine passes batted down or deflected at the line and has fumbled 17 times in his career, losing 10.

He does at least show some toughness in terms of being prepared to stay in the pocket and get a throw off under heavy pressure.

Levis has a quick, compact release and throws a tight spiral, but he can struggle to put touch on the ball and needs to tighten up his mechanics.

He doesn't seem to trust his footwork, instead being over-reliant on off-platform throws. While he is capable of making these at times, he can sometimes be erratic as a result.

With 23 interceptions in his two years as a full-time starter in college, and 16 in 21 starts at the pro level, it's evident that Levis isn't afraid to take risks.

However, this can lead to him being burned when he tries to force throws or is inaccurate. Moreover, he can make some really reckless decisions with the ball.

In assessing his potential to become a more reliable quarterback, you have to be concerned over Levis' inability to read the field at times. On this play, he threw to a receiver who was covered on fourth down even though another receiver was all alone on the same side of the field.

His overall mindset in terms of a refusal to take sacks or throw the ball away rather than take a risk of a fumble, interception or injury can be problematic.

Levis was really a run-first quarterback when he started his college career and even had a game where he rushed for 113 yards and four touchdowns after transferring to Kentucky.

However, he was obviously keen to establish himself as more of a traditional passer ahead of the 2023 draft, because he hardly ran the ball at all in his final season.

Levis is capable of moving around the pocket to get throws off, but ultimately lacks feel and poise in these situations.

At the pro level, he's used his legs at times but has just one touchdown and a longest gain of 21.

He does display toughness and will battle for yardage as a ball carrier. However, there should also be concerns about ball security and keeping himself healthy because he can be reckless at times.

Levis has not played a role on special teams at any level and would not be expected to in the NFL.

As noted, Levis ran the ball a lot when he was with Penn State, but did get a chance to operate in a pro style system when Liam Coen was his offensive coordinator during his first season with the Wildcats.

He has been a teammate of current Jets Trevin Wallace, T'Vondre Sweat, Brant Banks, Samuel Womack, Nate Lynn and Jarvis Brownlee Jr. during his career.

Levis missed the entire 2025 season after requiring shoulder surgery. He had originally suffered an injury to his AC joint in week four of the 2024 season and then aggravated that injury in the following game, causing him to be out for three weeks.

In his final season in college, Levis missed a few games when he suffered a dislocated finger and an ankle injury that left him in a walking boot in the same game.

He also had a hamstring issue during his rookie preseason.

Former Kentucky head coach Mark Stoops praised Levis' toughness and competitiveness, calling him a "great leader" and saying he made the players around him better.

He had impressed the Kentucky staff by learning their system quickly to snatch the starting job, and was voted as a team captain for both of his seasons with the Wildcats.

Levis did a lot of community work in college, and his academics were excellent as he graduated at Penn State and then did a masters degree at Kentucky. He was a two-time all-academic Big Ten selection and a two-time member of the SEC academic honor roll.

There are no character flags other than some strange eating habits and a hacking scandal involving a viral sex tape, which doesn't seem to have been his fault.

As a college prospect, Levis was regarded as a toolsy quarterback with arm talent, but with rawness to his game and a lot to learn. Unfortunately, three years later not much has changed in terms of that prognosis.

Levis has had some great highlights during his pro career to date, and even put together a handful of good performances. In fact, if you look at his highlight reel, you would likely come away impressed. However, his lowlights are just as bad, and you could equally put together a reel of his worst moments that would immediately sum up why he hasn't been an NFL success and has a long way to go to reach that level.

You might consider the early success that JJ McCarthy has had with the Giants and be hopeful that Levis could enjoy similar success with a change of scenery and good weapons around him. However, while the time it took to give up on each of them was similar, McCarthy's body of work was better, and he has proved more capable of winning games, albeit on a stronger team.

For the Jets, Geno Smith is obviously not a long-term solution, but it may be too early to throw Cade Klubnik to the wolves. We may therefore see Levis get his shot at some point. Perhaps he has the talent and determination to turn his career around but realistically it would be surprising if this Jets staff was the one he achieved that under.

#2983. Chicago Bears Stock Watch: Offense making plays; secondary struggles

The Monday Night Football game between two of the NFC's best lived up to the hype.

The Chicago Bears got the best of the Seattle Seahawks in a 34-17 win.

It was a statement game for the Bears. On the road, against the defending Super Bowl champs, and against a defense widely seen as one of the best in the league, they put up 34 points.

The Bears are solidly one of the best teams in the league, with one of the best quarterbacks in the league. It's indisputable at this point. It's not a fluke when a QB can put up the numbers Caleb has against top defensive units like Seattle and New England.

Caleb Williams, QB – I try not to put Williams here often. Partly because I think we expect him to be excellent. And partly because it often feels easy. But this game warrants it. Williams put up numbers — 247 yards, 1 TD, 0 INT, 70.5 percent completion — against the best pass defense in the league. Seattle hasn't allowed a 200-yard passer this season. What he did is special.

Jeffery Simmons, DT – He's been on the team for five days, four of those, he's actually been able to really be in the building working. Yet, he registered a sack, a PBU, two TFL and six total tackles. Great early returns on the massive trade.

Montez Sweat, DE – Sweat benefited from the arrival of his college teammate, as many expected. He had one sack officially; two others were called off by penalties nowhere near him. He also had three QB hits and one TFL.

Tyrique Stevenson, CB – Maybe this is too harsh, considering his worse moments came against Jaxson Smith-Njigba. But it wasn't a great game for the secondary. He had a nice PBU, but overall was beat more than he won.

Colston Loveland, TE – This is hardly a down, but when the team is winning by three scores, any mistake is amplified. Loveland's fumble came on a third-down screen. A play where the offense is setting up a punt. It set the Seahawks up inside the red zone and created the only real momentum they had. Loveland is still more good than bad, but his one mistake came when the team could least afford it.

Jonah Jackson, G – Jackson hasn't quite matched the performance he had in 2025. That would be fine if it wasn't so noticeable. Seattle is a great defense and Jackson was often facing Leonard Williams, but he got beat a few times.

Who was on your up-and-down list this week? Who did I miss?

#2981. Utah Jazz vs San Antonio Spurs: Recap and final score

At the start of the season, Jazz fans were hyped. With the #2 pick in Darryn Peterson, Keyonte George on a new contract, and Lauri Markkanen and Jaren Jackson Jr. healthy and ready to play, the season was extremely promising.

After tonight's 119-97 loss to the San Antonio Spurs, the Jazz are now 1-6 and are in last place in the Western Conference.

Tonight was easily the worst showing from the Jazz all season, with the Jazz looking disjointed and chaotic on both ends of the floor for most of the game. As a team, Utah shot 35.4% from the field and 35.3% from three. Yes, the Jazz are young, and they're missing Jaren Jackson Jr. to injury, but nothing looks easy with this offense. Credit goes to the Spurs for their excellent perimeter defense, but Utah never looked like it had an offensive rhythm. There are a lot of actions with the Jazz offense that come from a lot of movement, but those ended up in some pretty odd endings. Jaxson Hayes, for example, had 9 shots in this one but was just 4/9. Svi Mykhailiuk was 1/7 in this one as well. That's not the worst thing in the world, but it feels like there are too many times when Utah's offense doesn't flow through the most important players: Keyonte George, Darryn Peterson, Lauri Markkanen, and Ace Bailey. That's not to say Utah didn't get shots for those players. Markkanen led the team with 16 shots, Peterson was next with 13, Keyonte George 12, and Ace Bailey 11, but at times the offense feels like it has no clear order, and a lot of role players hijack possessions that turn into bad plays.

Now, you could argue those shots being spread around were justified, given the bad shooting from those core players on the Jazz. Keyonte George has now had two bad shooting games in a row (3/12), and Ace Bailey is still finding his stroke from three (1/5), for example. But something has to be done to improve shot quality. Are there offensive tweaks that could give them more opportunities for easier shots? Can they simplify things somehow? The reality is something has to improve because Utah is not a very good team right now.

Tonight's game had some really weird energy. Could it have had something to do with the news that Isaiah Collier had the team option declined on his contract?

Maybe it was that? Or maybe it was a residual effect of Will Hardy's decision to bench Darryn Peterson for Brice Sensabaugh and Isaiah Collier? Speaking of that, it's not great that Peterson was benched for a player who won't even be on the team next year. It's not a great sign. There's no way to know as an outside observer, but the team did not look happy. Now, winning cures all, and Utah could really stand to get some wins soon to turn the season around, but if they play like they did tonight, it'll take some time.

That's not to say there weren't some bright spots. Utah apparently made it clear they were not playing Collier and had Darryn Peterson running the show with the second unit. Things weren't perfect, but Peterson and the second unit clawed their way back into the game. Peterson was just a -4 in a blowout loss, and those minutes with the bench were a big reason why. It's a great sign for the future and confirmation that Will Hardy force-feeding Isaiah Collier minutes wasn't the best play.

The takeaway from this game, besides learning to compartmentalize the difficult things in life, is to remember the team is only going to get better. The Jazz are evolving game by game in how they are running, and eventually those moves will lead to winning. It also looks like this season will turn around as soon as Darryn Peterson takes off as a player. That may take time though, so Jazz fans might need to find some patience.

#2979. Ripple strikes deal with Robinhood to expand its RLUSD digital dollar

Ripple has struck a deal with Robinhood to expand the reach of its digital dollar, RLUSD, strengthening trading and financing links between their institutional businesses and improving Robinhood's internal money management.

The partnership involves using RLUSD to settle institutional trades, automate Robinhood's treasury operations and expand access to digital asset trading and financing through Ripple Prime and Bitstamp, the global crypto exchange acquired by Robinhood last year.

It also helps meet Robinhood's ambition of becoming a global financial superapp, centralizing its customers' financial lives while expanding the underlying infrastructure that can serve both retail and institutional clients.

"Utility is what moves stablecoins forward. Working with Ripple, Bitstamp and Robinhood will have access to RLUSD for institutional settlement, Ripple Treasury for our operations and Ripple Prime for financing," Robinhood Crypto General Manager Johann Kerbrat said in a statement shared with CNBC. "That is what a connected, global financial system looks like."

Stablecoins have long been a workhorse of the crypto world, giving traders a way to move money among exchanges, buy and sell crypto assets, and park funds between trades without having to convert back into traditional currency. But as the crypto industry evolves beyond trading and markets move toward 24/7 liquidity, stablecoins are becoming essential financial infrastructure because of their ability to enable near-instant settlement and move dollar-denominated value at any time, regardless of traditional banking hours.

As firms look to do more with stablecoins beyond simply listing them on exchanges, stablecoin issuers are betting they'll favor a one-stop-shop approach that bundles the technology and services needed to move money, settle trades and manage cash.

"Stablecoins have evolved from a crypto trading tool into a critical piece of financial infrastructure," Ripple President Monica Long told CNBC. "The clearest evidence of this is where they're now being embedded — from corporate payments to capital markets. Our partnership with Robinhood reflects that evolution."

"This is about much more than trading," she added. "It's about fundamentally changing how money moves and is managed — giving institutions the ability to operate 24/7/365, unlocking liquidity and putting capital to work more efficiently."

Circle Internet Group, a Ripple competitor, is positioning itself for a similar future, where winning platforms, in addition to issuing stablecoins, also provide the infrastructure to put them to work. Earlier this year, for example, it launched the Circle Payments Network, connecting financial institutions and payment providers using its flagship USDC stablecoin and other digital assets for cross-border payments.

The network is Circle's answer to the 8-year-old Ripple Payments business, a service that helps businesses move money across borders, using any of several assets moving through that system, including USDC, Ripple-created XRP and, as of its debut in December 2024, RLUSD.

RLUSD's market cap has grown to more than $2.5 billion. Tether's USDT and Circle's USDC still dominate the total stablecoin market, which now exceeds $300 billion.

RLUSD will also be listed on Robinhood's trading platform, and is currently available to traders on Bitstamp as well as Robinhood Chain, the company's home-grown blockchain for tokenized stocks and other assets.

Robinhood, which reports third-quarter results for the three months ended in September after the market closes, rose about 2.5% in early trading Tuesday, snapping a three-day decline.

Correction: A previous headline misspelled RLUSD.

#2976. Federal Reserve Board requests public comment on two proposals related to establishing a regulatory framework for Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers under the GENIUS Act

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 2:30 p.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Thursday requested public comment on two proposals related to establishing a regulatory framework for Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers under the GENIUS Act.

The first proposal would require that Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers fully back their stablecoins with certain permissible reserve assets, such as short-term Treasury bills and certain other high-quality, liquid assets. The proposal would also establish standardized capital requirements to address certain credit and operational risks of payment stablecoin activities, as well as risk management standards, in accordance with the law. Separately, the proposal would introduce rules for Board-supervised firms that safekeep the assets backing payment stablecoins. It would also clarify the permissibility of stablecoin and related activities for Board-supervised banks.

The second proposal would establish a tailored application process for Board-supervised banks applying to issue payment stablecoins. These applicants would be required to submit a business plan and financial information, among other documents. The proposal would also create a process governing appeals, hearings, and final determinations for applications.

The comment period will close 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call (202) 452-2955.

#2974. Rockets keep rolling with 109-93 win over Celtics

Well, this is pretty fun. The Rockets are not only winning, they're rolling over most teams. Yes, they lost a winnable game in Dallas, with perhaps the wrong players missing, but they followed that up with a win over a depleted yet still dangerous OKC the next night. Now they've taken down Boston, admittedly on a back to back of their own, dropping their record to 5-2. That is to say, Boston had been very good so far, and beating them, even with a schedule advantage is no easy feat.

And yet, the Rockets are making it look pretty easy in most of their wins. Boston wasn't depleted tonight, and the Rockets were fairly close to full strength as well, with recent absentees Reed Sheppard, Fred VanVleet and Marcus Smart playing tonight. Clint Capela was out, and that proved surprisingly troublesome at points in the game, as Boston is pretty big and physical at center, if generally fairly small elsewhere. Adams or Clint might have helped in that regard, but alas. Boston was without Classic Celtic Paul George, who was sitting on the second night of a back to back.

This game started out disjointed, and it more or less stayed that way. Lots of odd calls, anticipatory calls, and an illegible standard for fouls. Not terrible, but it did kill any hope of free flowing basketball. Until fairly late in the 3rd quarter the Rockets would step out to a small lead, of say 6-8 points, but couldn't build on it. Boston would close the gap to a few points. The Rockets would build a small lead again. And repeat. In the fourth quarters the Rockets pulled away, and with the lead pushing 20 points, a bad shooting Boston could never catch up.

Boston lives or dies by three point shooting, and tonight, they died by it, going 12-42 from deep. Some of this was surely tired Celtic legs, but some of it has to be credited to the Rockets. It was a dismal shooting night from pretty much all the Boston stalwarts and quondam Rocket killers. Peyton "I cut my own hair." Pritchard was 0-5 from deep. Jason "MSFT" Tatum was 1-7, Derek "Beardo" White was 1-5, and Sam"Snuggy" Hauser was 0-5. Only Baylor "Nebraksa!" Scheierman and Luka "The Other Luka" Garza had good shooting nights with any volume.

If you'd asked who'd be the high scorer tonight, Jason "Microsoft" Tatum, Kevin Durant or Alperen Sengun, would you have automatically picked Sengun? Possibly not, but Sengun has played not like an All Star, but an All NBA player this season. Tonight he went for 26pts on a very tidy 12-15 shooting, grabbed 7 boards, dished 10 assists to 1 TO, and made 1 of 2 of his 3pt shots, maintaining his great, though low volume, 3pt% percentage.

Tatum only scored 19, went 5-16, with nearly half his output coming from the free throw line. He had 6 boards, and 9 assists to 5 turnovers. Some in TDS land aren't happy with Amen Thompson lately, but a lot of Amen's value so far, in my view, has come from shutting down opposing stars fairly well. He's still posting stats, he still is having trouble scoring inside, and also having any sort of effective mid range game. He seems to be struggling to find his place in a new order where even Kevin Durant isn't notching high volume attempts.

We saw another great game from Jabari Smith, who is somehow turning into the "go to" guy for the Rockets when they simply have to have 3pt shot. Jabs was 5-10 shooting tonight, and 4-5 from three point range. He added 10 rebounds, and I think played some excellent defense, especially on the perimeter.

We also saw the Rockets Dr. Jeckyll and Mr Hyde player, Marcus Smart, in Mr. Hyde form tonight. He could not shoot it much at all, unlike last game, going 2-10 and 2-8 from three point range (though the makes came late to help seal the win). Smart did however open almost the entire Dark Arts Grimoire that so annoyed me when he played for Boston. I've never seen a player pick up not one, but two, fouls in a sequence with the ball not yet inbounded, but Smart did that tonight. I'm fairly sure both of those fouls were on Boston, but whatever the case, he definitely annoyed the hell out of Boston's players. Even "Cap" himself, Jason Tatum showed human emotions at times. Did playing Boston mean something to Ime Udoka and Marcus Smart? Yes, I'd say it did.

Fred VanVleet continues his good playing, maculate shooting, and immaculate distribution. We also saw another quiet night, at a lowish 33 minutes from Kevin Durant, who took only 6 shots. He did add two blocks and a steal, and seems perfectly content. Opponents now have a tough choice with Sengun, Durant, and Smith. It's really impossible to do what opponents did last season, and utterly pack the paint and dare the Rockets to make shots. It seems almost impossible to grab and blanket Durant as is the usual scheme against the Rockets and win. That message hasn't permeated the NBA yet, and it will be interesting to see what the adjustment to the Rockets will be.

We saw a late return to shooting form from Reed Sheppard who played a pretty worthless stint in the first half, but contributed some good passing, defense, and two valuable three pointers as part of a Rockets run that buried Boston for good. Hopefully this will continue in his role off the bench. Tari Eason continues to be a force from the bench, mostly a force for good. Tonight he had 13pts on 5-8 shooting and a very strong 3-6 from three, and added 4 rebounds, 3 assists (2TO) and 2 blocks in his 27 minutes.

It's all going very well, and I think we should not let the perfect (which the Rockets nearly are) be the enemy of the very good indeed.

Anyway, the Rockets are alone at number one in the West, as Memphis was knocked off in OT by the Pelicans. I will admit that I didn't think the Rockets would be #1 in the West at any point before the season started.

The Rockets play Antless Minnesota on Wednesday in Houston.
