#2967. Player Grades: Lakers vs. Heat

This is far from a finished product. Head coach JJ Redick has repeatedly mentioned across the first two weeks of the season that patience is needed as a host of new faces get acclimated to the Lakers' system.

What they've already proven, though, is that this is a potent offensive team. Led by Luka Dončić, this team is everything everyone imagined it would be when it comes to scoring the ball. And that comes with, again, kinks still needing to be worked out.

But through their first eight games of the season, the Lakers' 121.9 offensive rating leads the league. They lead the league in field goal percentage, are third in 3-point percentage and second in points per game.

It's been a light schedule, yes, but they've come sprinting out of the gate as one of the league's best offenses. And that's pretty encouraging with work still to be done.

So, let's dive into the win. As always, grades are based on expectations for each player. A "B" grade represents the average performance for that player.

19 minutes, 6 points, 1 rebound, 2 assists, 1 block, 3 fouls, 1 turnover, 2-6 FG, 2-6 3PT, +13

Thybulle's ability to be a point-of-attack defender slots so many other players on the team into better-suited roles defensively. He's proven to be such a valuable asset already, so long as he stays healthy.

25 minutes, 7 points, 4 rebounds, 2 assists, 1 steal, 2 blocks, 5 fouls, 4 turnovers, 3-4 FG, 1-1 3PT, +20

It was certainly notable that, even with Williams back, Jake LaRavia kept his starting spot. And he has more than earned it.

32 minutes, 15 points, 15 rebounds, 2 assists, 2 steals, 3 fouls, 1 turnover, 7-13 FG, 1-3 FT, +16

How can a team that has both Giannis Antetokounmpo and Bam Adebayo be so bad at the rim defensively? Kessler was gifted so many easy baskets on Monday. Wasn't defense supposed to be Miami's calling card?

34 minutes, 27 points, 2 rebounds, 5 assists, 3 fouls, 5 turnovers, 10-15 FG, 4-5 3PT, 3-4 FT, +21

After a couple of poor showings, Austin bounced back. The turnovers were still a bit too high, but if he just simply shoots 66% from the field the rest of the season, we can overlook that.

35 minutes, 42 points, 7 rebounds, 13 assists, 2 steals, 2 fouls, 2 turnovers, 14-24 FG, 6-15 3PT, 8-10 FT, +26

Yes, it's November. Yes, the Lakers haven't even played 10 games yet. Yes, the sample size is small. But Luka is the best player on the planet right now and well on course for an MVP award.

22 minutes, 7 points, 4 rebounds, 4 assists, 2 fouls, 2-4 FG, 1-3 3PT, 2-3 FT, +5

After being indirectly called out by Redick after Sunday's game for his indecisiveness, this was Grimes' best non-Clippers game of the season.

27 minutes, 13 points, 3 rebounds, 4 assists, 2 steals, 3 fouls, 2 turnovers, 5-10 FG, 2-5 3PT, 1-2 FT, +10

Mamu playing as the five was particularly dangerous against the Heat as his ability to pop out to the 3-point line allowed him to knock down some long-range buckets as well as take advantage of a rotating defense.

15 minutes, 9 points, 2 rebounds, 2 assists, 1 steal, 3 fouls, 3-6 FG, 3-6 3PT, -6

Welcome back Ziaire. He jumped back in seamlessly even as he came off the bench. The Lakers having so many interchangeable wings is such a great luxury.

15 minutes, 6 points, 1 rebound, 5 assists, 1 foul, 3-4 FG, 0-1 3PT, -2

Dare I say a much more controlled game from Sexton? That's still relative as he is still a bit too wild at times. But, still, only one missed shot, no turnovers and five assists? Yes please.

Interestingly, Vando got his normal first half shift but did not see the floor in the second half. With the Heat not really having traditional big men and smaller lineups on the floor, the Lakers could get away with Mamu at the five surrounded by wings and guards.

As for the bench group, that JJ waited until there were 82 seconds left in a game they led by more than 20 to put them in felt like a point being made.

Keeping LaRavia in the starting lineup was the right call. Redick is also still experimenting a bit with the rotations, including having Kessler on the floor with Reaves and Sexton to start the second quarter. This is all a work in progress to find the right combinations and he's still doing some experimenting, too.

Using a challenge less than 30 seconds into a game is hilarious. He technically lost the second challenge but I'm not going to dock him for that as Giannis bowling over two defenders while giving them a shove and getting away with it was bonkers.

#2963. Federal Reserve Board finalizes changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 9:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Wednesday finalized changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements. The two final rules are largely similar to the proposed rules from 2025. The Board also requested comment on a proposal to better capture differences in banks' business models to generate fee income.

The Board conducts stress tests to ensure that large banks are sufficiently capitalized and able to lend to households and businesses even in a severe recession. In December 2024, the Board announced it would modify the test to improve its resilience.

"The stress test is an essential component of our regulatory capital framework," said Vice Chair for Supervision Michelle W. Bowman. "Today's changes preserve its resilience by ensuring that it is transparent, granular, and risk-sensitive. The public will now have greater assurance that the risks banks take will be reflected appropriately in their stress test losses and their capital requirements."

The first final rule requires the Board to invite public input annually on the stress test scenarios and any material model changes. It also updates the framework that guides the design of the hypothetical scenarios and adopts the models that will be used for the 2027 stress test. In addition, the final rule makes several adjustments to the stress test calendar. The final rule also updates the global market shock component, which is a set of shocks to specific market components applied to banks with large trading books. These banks will now be tested against two global market shock components each year, and the Board will use the shock that produces the largest losses for each firm to calculate the firm's stress test results.

The second final rule requires the Board, when calculating stress capital buffer requirements, to average the results from the two most recent annual supervisory stress tests for firms subject to the stress test in both years. The Board will begin averaging stress capital buffer requirements in 2028 to ensure that only models incorporating public input are used in the calculation.

The proposal would request public comment on a revision to the Board's noninterest income model that would improve the stress test's ability to capture business model diversity across firms. If adopted, these adjustments would replace the current noninterest income model that projects each bank's fee income under stress. Comments are due 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

Together, the changes are likely to reduce year-over-year volatility in capital requirements by approximately 50 percent and are not expected to materially affect aggregate capital requirements.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call 202-452-2955.

Federal Register notice: Modifications to the Capital Plan Rule and Stress Capital Buffer Requirement

Federal Register notice: Enhanced Transparency and Public Accountability of the Supervisory Stress Test Models and Scenarios; Modifications to the Capital Planning and Stress Capital Buffer Requirement Rule, Enhanced Prudential Standards Rule, and Regulation LL

Federal Register notice: Request for Comment on Model Changes for the Boardâs 2027 Supervisory Stress Test

Stress Test Changes and Effective Dates (PDF)

Review of Comments and Summary of Changes to the Proposed Stress Test Models (PDF)

#2961. AI debt fears ease ahead of Oracle, Alphabet and Amazon updates. Here's what to watch.

Fears over artificial intelligence debt have eased after a bout of volatility. But more tests for AI debt are coming.

Oracle holds its Investor Day on Wednesday. And hyperscalers Alphabet and Amazon.com are scheduled to report quarterly results after the market close on Wednesday and Thursday, respectively.

Credit markets are on edge over a flood of AI debt, though fears have eased up a little lately. Meta Platforms' plans for a $95 billion stock offering, announced on Monday, helped bring down its credit-default swap spreads. Those spreads had risen last week when the company reported negative free cash flow in the third quarter. Other CDS spreads had moved higher too, though they have since backed off.

Investors had expected Meta to bring a large bond deal to market shortly after its earnings announcement, said Richard Cheng, head of Nuveen's investment-grade corporate sector team. The fact that it wasn't announced came as a relief.

"Just the absence of the bond deal itself is a direct catalyst for AI-related spread tightening," he said.

A credit spread is the difference in the yield between the corporate bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. When spreads tighten, it means investors are becoming more confident about the bond issuer.

The market action over the last few days shows that investors are recognizing that "credit fears may have been overblown," said CNBC contributor Paul Meeks, head of technology research at Freedom Capital Markets.

"I expect that these better feelings, maybe not good but better, may continue as companies announce their quarters over the next few weeks."

Still, spreads on CDS remain wider over the past year due to a surge of debt issuance and free cash flows turning negative across the AI space. Meta's CDS spread, for instance, is still up 177% year to date, while Amazon's spread has gained 129%.

"It's not necessarily fundamentals," said CreditSights analyst Jordan Chalfin. "It's more, I'd say, 80% driven by technicals and sentiment."

Alphabet and Amazon pose the next big hurdle for tech bonds as they update their capex plans and free cash flow forecasts. Microsoft's earnings are also due Wednesday, but the company has yet to issue any bonds to pay for its AI spending.

Both Amazon and Alphabet reported negative free cash flow in the second quarter and analysts expect that trend to continue.

Analysts expect Alphabet to report free cash flow of -$2.3 billion while spending $55.2 billion on capital expenditures in the third quarter, according to FactSet consensus estimates. Amazon is expected to report -$9.9 billion in free cash flow against capex of $58.7 billion.

S&P Global Ratings estimated in an August report that the hyperscalers will have negative free operating cash flow in 2026 and 2027.

More critical will be their outlooks, Cheng says. "The singular focus on the investors' minds is really what the capex guidance trajectory looks like across the hyperscaler space."

Meeks believes elevated capex is a sign of strong AI demand. The hyperscalers have "decided that with all the evidence they have — and they have more than you and me — that this is worthwhile spending, and not just worthwhile; it's required," he said.

CreditSights' Chalfin doesn't expect the hyperscalers to announce major new bond issuance around their earnings reports. Oracle's updates may be more critical for credit markets. The company has issued $117 billion in long-term debt and is clinging to an investment-grade credit rating of BBB-, the lowest level of investment grade above junk, according to S&P.

"It's the most important IG tech credit, just given how it's a bit more stressed relative to the A-rated names," he said. "It's trading more like a high-yield credit, even though it's IG, and so there's just a lot of investor concern on that one."

One wrinkle to the AI debt story: balance sheets don't reveal the whole picture. The companies are using complicated off-balance sheet arrangements for data centers and chip financing, and they aren't fully transparent, S&P Global said in a September report.

"There are commitments they are entering into, for example, into data center leases or purchase commitments on chips that aren't necessarily treated as debt," Mark Habib, digital infrastructure sector lead at S&P Global Ratings, said on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" Monday.

S&P is trying to provide the market with more visibility on those commitments "to give a fuller picture of what kinds of obligations they're actually entering into," he added.

Oracle aside, hyperscaler credit ratings are all comfortably above A levels — reflecting the fact that they have broadly diversified revenue streams, ample coverage for interest payments, and capacity to add leverage if they choose.

Nuveen's Cheng isn't concerned, pointing out that while there has been some credit repricing, there is no credit crisis.

"They continue to have exceptional credit profiles, strong cash generation liquidity," he said of the largest hyperscalers.

"The market is really not concerned about credit quality at this point," Cheng added. "It's really just the trajectory of the capex needs and the supply of debt to fund that. Both of those are pointing in one direction — and that's up."

Correction: This story has been revised to reflect that Oracle's investment-grade credit rating is BBB-, according to S&P. A previous version mischaracterized the company's credit rating.

#2960. Spurs down Jazz for second time in a week, win fifth straight

The San Antonio Spurs continued their three-game homestand on Monday night and used two strong quarters to put away the Utah Jazz, earning the 119-97 win at the Frost Bank Center. The win is the fifth straight for San Antonio, who defeated the Jazz for the second time in nearly a week.

San Antonio set the tone from the opening tip, being aggressive by slicing into the paint at every chance and using their signature ball movement to find the open man for a shot. Defensively, the Spurs put together their best quarter of the season so far, holding Utah to 16 points, the lowest they've allowed in the first quarter this season, and 27 percent shooting.

The second quarter saw Utah make a run, cutting the Spurs' lead to 36-35. Spurs head coach Mitch Johnson was furious and called a timeout, pounding his fist into his hands in frustration as his team walked over to the bench. After that timeout, San Antonio responded with a 14-5 run to take a 50-40 lead and would eventually go into the locker room with some motivation to put the game away in the second half.

San Antonio opened the second half by outscoring Utah 21-18, but another timeout from Coach Johnson saw him not happy with his team's transition defense as Keyonte George hit a three in transition. The Spurs responded by closing the quarter on a 23-11 run to take full control going into the final 12 minutes.

"Just not getting bored on what was working," San Antonio guard Stephon Castle said after the game when asked about the second half surge. "I think we came back out in the 2nd half and brought that same kind of energy."

The Spurs were led by a balanced attack as seven players were in double figures by the final horn. Dylan Harper led San Antonio with 17 points, eight assists, and seven rebounds. Castle rebounded from his poor outing with a migraine on Saturday to score 16 points to along with five rebounds and four assists.

Victor Wembanyama and De'Aaron Fox each chipped in 12, while Wemby pulled down 11 rebounds. Keldon Johnson led the Spurs' second unit with 15 points and seven rebounds.

"We have so many weapons," Castle said of the Spurs' balanced attack after the game. "We have so many guys who can make a play out of nothing, and we'll be fine either way."

The injury-depleted Jazz were led by Lauri Markkanen's 22 points as Utah lost its fifth straight.

With the Spurs' homestand closing on Wednesday night with a visit from Cooper Flagg and the Dallas Mavericks, Keldon Johnson said the team is not satisfied with a five-game winning streak after climbing out of the 0-2 hole to start the season.

"We've yet to play our best brand of basketball," he said after the game. "We're getting better each and every game. If we felt like the best version of ourselves at this point of the season, we'd be in trouble. We're improving and will continue to improve."

Harrison Barnes came off the bench to score 10 points and left the internet with a pretty funny moment that will surely turn into a meme by the time this article goes live.

Luke Kornet continued his strong play from Saturday night, scoring eight points and pulling down five rebounds in 16 minutes. If this is the version of the backup big the Spurs can consistently rely on, it will be huge for the non-Wemby minutes.

On the other side, Carter Bryant continues to have growing pains as the enters his second season in the NBA. At one point in the game, Mitch Johnson subbed Bryant out for a quick conversation, sent him to the bench for a few moments and then put him back into the game. That was shades of Gregg Popovich from Coach Johnson, using these games as teaching moments that will benefit Bryant later in the season and in his career.

#2948. Utah Jazz decline team option for Isaiah Collier

According to Michael Scotto, the Utah Jazz declined the team option for Isaiah Collier.

Like Scotto mentioned, Collier has averaged 9.9 points, 6.6 assists, 3 rebounds, and 1.0 steals in three seasons. This season, Collier is averaging 7.5 points, 4 rebounds, and 5.7 assists while shooting 36.8% from the field. It's been a dissapointing start to the season for the Jazz, and a dissapointing one for Collier, who has been the focal point of Will Hardy's bench unit all season. Collier's assist numbers have been impressive, but he's never been able to find a playstyle that is cohesive with the players around him, and Utah has rarely won the bench minutes with him leading the team. It's a bummer because Collier has a lot of promise and brings a lot of energy and competitiveness, but he just hasn't improved his game enough for the Jazz to keep investing in him.

Not everything comes down to shooting, but Collier hasn't made a single three this season, and it allows opposing defenses to sag off him, making things difficult for everyone around him.

This isn't the end for Collier, he'll definitely have some interested teams looking to give him a chance, but he'll have to find his shooting stroke to stay in the league long term.

#2940. Trump is going to get 'trounced' in the midterms — and knows it, Anthony Scaramucci tells CNBC

U.S. President Donald Trump will get "trounced" in Tuesday's midterm elections, Anthony Scaramucci, former White House communications director and founder of SkyBridge Capital, told CNBC.

Speaking to CNBC's "Squawk Box Europe" on Tuesday, Scaramucci said America is in the throes of a cultural and economic struggle, with the two main parties fighting to deliver "two different visions of where the country should go."

"I think the president's going to get trounced, by the way, and I think he knows he's going to get trounced," he said.

Follow CNBC's live coverage of the 2026 midterm elections

Trump's economic approval rating hit an all-time low last month, according to CNBC's All-America Economic Survey, with 65% of respondents disapproving of the president's handling of the economy, while 80% rated the economy as fair or poor.

"You have one vote. You have one customer relations survey that you can do on the American president — that's at the midterms, and people are not happy," Scaramucci added.

"He promised affordability, he promised no more forever wars, he promised transparency on the Epstein files," Scaramucci said Tuesday. "He didn't deliver on any of that stuff, and so there's a lot of people inside MAGA that are now also breaking from him."

The White House did not immediately respond to a CNBC request to comment. The White House previously told CNBC that Scaramucci's "relevance ended almost a decade ago." Scaramucci was White House communications director for 10 days in 2017, during Trump's first term.

Prediction markets are now pricing in around a 3-in-4 chance of the Democrats winning control of the Senate in Tuesday's election. Odds and gambling platforms do not use methodologies used by traditional political polling, and therefore are not substitutes for political polls.

On the Iran war, Scaramucci said he was "bearish" on a potential diplomatic breakthrough after the ballot.

"I don't see any alignment between the Iranians, the Israelis, and the Americans for a potential negotiated solution right now," he said. "If the Americans are going to ramp up the military strikes alongside of the Israelis ... I don't really understand how that's going to necessarily help the region either."

The U.S. launched "defensive" strikes on Iran's Qeshm Island overnight, a U.S. official told MS NOW. Trump previously said American forces would not attack Iran before the midterms.

"Wars are very bad for business," Scaramucci told CNBC on Tuesday. "They're not great for the stock market. So I would like to have a negotiated settlement, but there's nothing on the matrix where you see the concentric circles lining up, where these three groups would say, 'OK, that's a deal that all three of us are willing to accept.'"

Scaramucci also said "disarray" within the Democratic Party had been "the best thing that has happened for Donald Trump."

"And so, if he gets trounced here, there will be bicameral investigations that will be the main show because there's a lot of things that's going on in the administration that the Democrats are going to want to uncover," he said.

"The Republicans, in my mind, I think there'll be a great intellectual civil war. The Republicans are going to have to reconcile where do they want to go. It's no longer the party of Ronald Reagan, certainly not the party of George W. Bush, but is it the party of Donald Trump after he leaves the stage?"

Scaramucci added that it was going to take a long while to get away from the so-called MAGA movement.

"There'll be DNA elements of MAGA in the next presidential candidate for sure, because looming large over that presidential candidate will be Donald Trump and several hundred million dollars of his PAC money, and so they're not going to want to get too far away from him in 2028. So there will be elements of MAGA, but I also think there'll be a civil war in that party," he said.

#2939. Robinhood shares fall as crypto slump weighs on profits, offsetting gains in new businesses

Robinhood shares were down around 8% in pre-market trading on Wednesday as investors digested weaker-than-expected third-quarter earnings despite growth in options trading and prediction markets.

Here's how the brokerage performed in its quarter ended Sept. 30, compared with Wall Street estimates, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Robinhood's net income fell to $468 million, or 51 cents per share, from $556 million, or 61 cents per share, a year ago. Revenue represented a 12% increase from $1.27 billion in the same quarter last year.

Despite that gain, transaction-based revenue of $867 million came in below Wall Street estimates from StreetAccount of $879 million, putting focus on whether Robinhood can sustain momentum across its trading businesses and reduce its reliance on volatile market activity.

Robinhood has been diversifying its business as it tries to expand its brokerage app into a broader financial services platform. Options trading and event contracts have become increasingly important sources of transaction revenue, while the company has continued to invest in wealth management, subscriptions and other offerings.

Transaction-based revenue grew 19% from the same period last year to $867 million, driven primarily by a more than 500% increase in event contracts revenue. Additionally, options revenue grew 31% to $399 million and equities revenue rose 41% to $121 million.

Those gains were partially offset by cryptocurrency revenue, which fell 55% to $120 million.

Chief Financial Officer Shiv Verma told CNBC event contracts on crypto movements did well in the quarter, offering investors another way to express a view on crypto despite the slump in prices and spot trading.

Net deposits on the Robinhood platform totaled $15.7 billion for the quarter, equal to a 17% annualized growth rate based on total platform assets at the end of Q2 2026. Over the past year, net deposits totaled $71 billion, representing 21% growth compared with total platform assets at the end of Q3 2025.

—With reporting from CNBC's Robert Hum and Kate Rooney

Week 9 is the official midway point of the 2026 NFL season. So by now you know what you have on your fantasy football roster. You also know who has been healthy the majority of the year and which players have missed time. It feels like the injury report is thinning out a bit as we get into the middle of the campaign. Lamar Jackson, BAL (thumb) — Headed for injured reserve

Tyler Huntley, BAL (toe) — Could miss extended time

Ravens QBs: Jackson left in the first half of Sunday's loss to the Bills. He needs surgery on his thumb and is headed for IR. He'll miss at least four games, if not the rest of the season. #2937. A little-known biotech’s high-stakes cancer bet just paid off and its shares doubled

Danish biotech Genmab has seen its shares double in three months after a string of major cancer drug trial successes, giving fresh momentum to its push to take greater control of the medicines it develops and sells.

Shares in the roughly $35 billion company surged 48% last week, including a 29% jump in a single session after strong results for petosemtamab, its experimental head and neck cancer treatment. The announcement marked Genmab's third successful Phase 3 trial in October, following positive results for treatments targeting ovarian cancer and lymphoma.

After nearly three decades spent building Genmab into one of Europe's prominent biotechnology companies, CEO Jan van de Winkel told CNBC on Friday the breakthroughs marked "a completely new era."

Historically, Genmab made money from royalties from drugs it helped develop, including Johnson & Johnson's multiple myeloma treatment Darzalex and Novartis' Kesimpta. Van de Winkel said Johnson & Johnson is expected to generate $16.5 billion to $16.9 billion in Darzalex sales this year, with Genmab receiving royalties of up to 20%.

Genmab is now using that financial firepower to build a business that keeps a much larger share of the revenue from its own treatments.

The biggest market reaction followed Genmab's Oct. 28 announcement that petosemtamab, combined with Merck's Keytruda, reduced the risk of death by 50% compared with Keytruda alone in a Phase 3 trial of patients with recurrent or metastatic head and neck cancer.

The combination also significantly improved response rates and showed no new safety signals, according to Genmab.

Van de Winkel told CNBC the combination "could potentially set a completely new standard" of care although he declined to say whether the survival data might hold up as the data mature, citing still unpublished full data.

Days earlier, Genmab reported that its medicine Rina-S reduced the risk of disease progression or death by 71% and the risk of death by 57% versus chemotherapy in a Phase 3 ovarian cancer trial.

Barclays analysts said the results exceeded investor expectations and suggested Rina-S could offer an alternative for patients ineligible for AbbVie's competing treatment Elahere, or whose cancer had progressed after receiving it. They cautioned, however, that cross-trial comparisons are limited.

A third late-stage October win came from Epkinly, co-developed with AbbVie, which reduced the risk of disease progression or death by 51% when combined with standard chemotherapy in previously untreated diffuse large B-cell lymphoma.

Analysts see significant commercial potential for petosemtamab, including beyond its initial target market.

Jefferies analysts said the drug could generate $3.5 billion in peak annual global sales treating recurrent or metastatic head and neck cancer, and estimated an expansion into locally advanced disease could add another $3.5 billion, an opportunity they believe investors have yet to fully appreciate.

Declining to give specific numbers, van de Winkel said he expects petosemtamab to become a "multi-billion dollar drug."

Genmab is due to report third-quarter earnings on Nov. 5.

Genmab has built its business around antibody-based medicines, including so-called bispecific antibodies such as petosemtamab, which can bind to two different targets on cancer cells simultaneously.

Petosemtamab targets the proteins EGFR and LGR5, and is designed to both disrupt signals that help cancer cells grow and to encourage the immune system to attack the tumor.

Van de Winkel said Genmab's expertise in antibodies and their interaction with the immune system has also helped it spot promising medicines developed by others. It acquired petosemtamab through an $8 billion purchase of Merus in 2025 and Rina-S through a $1.8 billion acquisition of ProfoundBio in 2024.

Those deals are also central to Genmab's changing business model.

It fully owns petosemtamab and Rina-S, while splitting the commercial economics of Epkinly equally with AbbVie, much larger shares than with Johnson & Johnson and Novartis.

"From now on I'm going to increasingly hold on to ownership of these new medicines," Van de Winkel said.

Genmab now has 15 Phase 3 trials across its three lead compounds.

"The model is beginning to be validated and click together," he said. "I think the market gets it at this point finally."

The Merus deal underscored the scale of the bet. Genmab borrowed $5.5 billion to help fund the purchase, relying on its royalty income as a financial backstop if petosemtamab failed.

Owning more of its medicines also means taking on more of the work of selling them. Van de Winkel said the company already has much of the necessary infrastructure in place, having built up commercial capabilities around existing treatments including Epkinly and Tivdak, co-developed with Pfizer.

The company is expanding its sales presence in Europe and seeking to reach more cancer patients through community healthcare, beyond specialist hospitals, in the U.S.

Van de Winkel said he wants Genmab's majority-owned medicines to reach around 500,000 patients by the 2030s, up from fewer than 30,000 today.

He also acknowledged potential interest from larger drugmakers, saying Genmab was likely "high up on the radar screen," but stressed that his ambition remained to build an independent European biotech.

Greater ownership, however, also means bearing more of the cost and risk of clinical trials, regulatory approvals and commercialization.

Van de Winkel acknowledged that geopolitical shifts and drug-pricing pressures could complicate expansion plans and change the order in which Genmab launches medicines in different markets.

Participants in the study are chosen at random from 119 geographic areas, including metropolitan areas and rural counties across the United States, using scientific sampling procedures that aim to produce a sample representative of American families.

#2934. As Draymond Green comes apart, motivated Warriors come together in win vs. Suns

As Draymond Green comes apart, motivated Warriors come together in win vs. Suns originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

SAN FRANCISCO – For all that Draymond Green has done, and still does, for the Warriors, they did not come together Monday night until after he came apart.

The Warriors spent their first 26 minutes in futile pursuit of the Phoenix Suns and trailed by 11 when Green was assessed with two technical fouls, receiving an automatic disqualification two minutes into the second half.

Golden State's response was 22 minutes of grit and determination – and defense – to overtake Phoenix for a 116-113 victory that allowed the Warriors to walk off the floor with their first five-game win streak since April 2025.

With Stephen Curry pouring in 25 of his game-high 34 points in the second half, the Warriors outscored the Suns by 14 (59-45) in Green's absence.

"He lit a fire in all of us and we had to respond," Curry said. "I'm glad we did.

"It was a combination of that moment and having our brother's back when he defends us. But it was also understanding this is one of those games where most of (our) guys were on a back-to-back and Phoenix had been off for a couple of days. They were 1-3 and we knew they were going to come out playing desperate."

The Suns owned the first quarter, puncturing Golden State's defense for 31 points on 60-percent shooting from the field and leading by as much as 17 before the Warriors shook off their lethargy in the second quarter.

Green was assessed his first technical foul with 10 minutes left in the third quarter when he vigorously retaliated to a shove from Phoenix center Khaman Maluach. That sent combative Suns forward Dillon Brooks charging in to physically confront Green for a brief scuffle and some mutual jersey grabbing. After officials reviewed the activity, both players received technical fouls, with Draymond departing because it was his second.

The Warriors composed themselves and went on a 16-5 run, pulling into a 73-73 tie on a Curry midrange jumper with 6:47 remaining. From that point on, the momentum generally rode with them.

"It fueled our team," Brandin Podziemski said. "We didn't have that edge to us to start the game. Found it a little bit in the second quarter and then they came out in the third quarter pretty well, and then (the ejection) kind of changed the game for us. Everybody locked in a little bit more and came together for Dray. I think it was needed. Maybe he sensed it, maybe he didn't. But it helped us."

The weirdest part of all is that Golden State played its best defense without its best defender. The Suns shot 43.2 percent from the field, including 26.7 percent beyond the arc, after Green was banished.

"It's rare when that happens," coach Steve Kerr said of the oddity. "But I think it was more just about us catching up to the energy that that phoenix was showing that whole first half. We got engaged; the game was tight the whole second half and our guys summoned the energy necessary to win a game on a back-to-back."

Podziemski scored 11 of his 20 points during the post-Draymond comeback, while backup center Charles Bassey filled in wonderfully for Green. The much-traveled – five NBA teams in six years – 26-year-old produced 10 points and six rebounds. He finished with a career-high 19 points and eight rebounds over 23 minutes.

The more Bassey plays, the more his value becomes apparent. He's 6-foot-10, an active presence in the paint with a soft shooting touch. His performance was by any metric superior to the work Green submitted during his 17 minutes (four points, for rebounds, two assists).

Which isn't to suggest it's time to bench Green. He still brings immense value, especially when the stakes rise, whether it's the significance of the game or a personal matchup against an opposing star.

But Golden State's center rotation looks better than it did when training camp opened without Kristaps Porzingis. Green and Bassey, along with rookie Graham Ike, bring different attributes, and all are needed.The five-game win streak, which followed three straight losses to open the season, is slowly making a believer of the coach.

"Winning those two games on the road in Denver and Minnesota kind of righted the ship," Kerr said. "And now we've carried the momentum forward, so we turned a bad start into a good start. Now we've got to keep it going."

That's always the biggest challenge, and there is no doubt Draymond will be an asset to this roster. Usually by his presence, but on this night by his absence.

#2931. The Lakers’ starting lineup is crushing teams to start the season

LOS ANGELES — If an 82-game season can guarantee a team one thing, it's disruption. And the Lakers have already had their fair share in just eight games.

Los Angeles forward Ziaire Williams missed the last three games with left foot soreness before being made active ahead of their 138-117 win against the Miami Heat on Monday. Williams, who impressed in the starting lineup to open the season, came off the bench for Jake LaRavia.

It could have been injury management. It could have also been matchup-dependent against the ever-so-powerful Giannis Antetokounmpo. LaRavia started in Williams's place during the three games he missed. Not only did the starting lineup with LaRavia hold up, it outperformed the opening night lineup.

Head coach JJ Redick is aware of what the numbers say, but assures that it's more about the team's overall balance rather than LaRavia simply being a better fit with the lineup than Williams.

"I just feel like there's balance with those lineups," Redick said pregame on Monday. "With Luka [Dončić] and [Austin Reaves] being the primary creators and [Walker Kessler]'s rim rolling and running. The other guys being able to guard multiple positions. … It's not surprising to me that Jake being in that lineup with those guys is good for us, because there's intrinsic knowledge with what we're trying to do. Just having another guy out there who knows our stuff has been helpful."

It's the balance Redick has been trying to manage since the Lakers turned over their roster this offseason: meshing the new with the old. It's a process that will take patience and time, as he's been lamenting so often this season.

But the results have been good nonetheless. In 51 minutes over four games when Williams shares the floor with the starters (Dončić, Reaves, Kessler and Matisse Thybulle), the Lakers have a 135.5 offensive rating and a 115.1 defensive rating for a 20.4 net rating. When LaRavia has been in Williams' spot, those numbers surge to a 144.3 offensive rating, a 90.0 defensive rating and a 54.3 net rating in 37 minutes.

For all the talk of balance and lineup optionality, it really all just boils down to Dončić continuing to be the best player in the world. The "supernova" — as Redick called him on Sunday — added another 42 points and 13 assists to his season total on Monday, dicing up every look Miami gave him on offense.

Dončić is now averaging 38.8 points, 8.1 assists and 21.3 assist points created in seven games, meaning Dončić is responsible for 60.1 points per game.

Redick added on Dončić postgame, "It's a really good offense that we're playing right now, and a lot of that, of course, is Dončić and what he's able to do. Bend the defense and his shot making creates a lot of problems, and then he can make every pass."

Williams, who scored nine points in fifteen minutes against Miami, is one of the biggest beneficiaries of Dončić's galatctic playamking. In Brooklyn last season, without a player of Dončić's shot-creating ilk, Williams shot 34.3% from deep with an overall 3-point shot quality that ranked in the league's 49th percentile, according to BBall Index.

In five games this season, Williams is shooting 47.6% from beyond the arc.

"Very easy looks. Way easier than in the past," Williams said Monday. "They're sending two to Luka, two to AR every time. So, it's just swing, swing, one more, and I get to set my feet, get to square up the ball. And I put a lot of work in this summer. I trust my work. I'm confident in my shot, my shot feels great."

It's not just Williams. Kessler is averaging 14.4 points on 71.4% from the field, the best percentage since his rookie season. Thybulle is on pace for a career-best season from on 3-pointers (45.9%). Reaves is putting together the best shooting splits of his career (53.9/44.4/86.5), taking advantage of the pressure Dončić attracts.

Los Angeles's offense is humming at a rate that rivals any other in the league. It's spearheaded by perhaps the best player in the world and bolstered by a bevy of guards, wings and bigs that can shoot, pass and dribble.

Rob Pelinka and Redick's had a clear plan this offseason, but even they may not have imagined it working this quickly and this effectively.

You can follow Connor on Twitter at @cmorenoNBA.

#2930. Supermarket fridges hit with cyberattacks across Europe — here's what's happening

European supermarkets have become the center of another cyberattack, with hackers remotely heating fridges and spoiling food.

The attack caused temperatures in fridges and freezers to rise sharply, with at least one store reporting temperatures of up to 50°C (122°F) and other retailers discarding food. It's sparked concerns across the retail industry.

Dutch supermarket group Ahold Delhaize had dozens of Delhaize stores affected in Belgium and Luxembourg.

The Dutch supermarket giant told CNBC that there was an incident with refrigeration systems in dozens of its stores on Monday, which has resulted in food having to be discarded. It said the situation has since returned to normal with stores fully operational.

Meanwhile, Colruyt Group said five of its Spar stores in Belgium were targeted, according to Reuters. French supermarket Carrefour told the BBC that 15 of its Belgian-based stores were attacked too. France's Intermarché also said 15 stores out of 153 in total were impacted in Belgium.

"In most cases, the security measures in place made it possible to quickly contain the attack," an Intermarché spokesperson told CNBC. "However, a few stores did suffer some stock losses, although nothing comparable to the attack in mid-October.

"At this stage, the information available to us indicates that the bulk of the attack affected other players in the sector, notably Carrefour and Delhaize," they added.

IKEA told CNBC that a cyber incident affected refrigerators and freezers at its Mons store in Belgium between Sunday and Monday.

Although the store is still open to customers, the company is inspecting the affected equipment and products as well as checking installations across its other stores in Belgium.

"The incident has a limited impact on the offering in our restaurant and Swedish Food Market," an IKEA spokesperson said over email. "We are continuing our investigation to determine exactly what happened."

ICA, Sweden's biggest grocery retailer, also confirmed that it had been informed of an incident at a "limited number of stores," which were on networks outside ICA's central systems. "The incident has affected the stores' refrigeration and freezer systems. ICA is currently supporting the affected stores in managing the incident," the company said over email.

Elsewhere, Finnish Kesko supermarket, Irish grocery chain Supervalu and Estonian grocer One Selver also reported disruptions to their refrigeration systems.

Carrefour did not immediately respond to CNBC's request for comment.

The EU Commission is in direct contact with national authorities to investigate the incident and convened a meeting this morning of the e EU interinstitutional Task Force, together with EU agencies (ENISA and Europol) and the relevant Commission services, a spokesperson confirmed to CNBC.

Hundreds of French supermarkets were targeted in October in a cyberattack that caused fires, fridges to overheat and food to spoil.

Intermarché owner Les Mousquetaires told CNBC at the time that it was expecting damages of several million euros.

"Food security requires more attention at all levels because the weaponization of food has become a reality," European Commissioner for Agriculture and Food Christophe Hansen said on X in October.

Commissaries at six U.S. military bases also suffered freezer malfunctions in late August.

The Pentagon is now treating those as a suspected hack and coordinating with French authorities, four people familiar with the matter told Reuters in October.

In one case, the refrigerator's temperature was raised overnight, leaving masses of food melted and wilted by morning.

The attacks have yet to be claimed by any group or bad actor, and investigations are still underway, but it's raised concerns about the vulnerability of critical infrastructure.

"I think the jury is still out on whether the attackers are cyber criminals or have other intents behind the attack," Pilling explained. "Empty supermarket fridges are a powerful psychological image. They can make a relatively small cyberattack feel like a much bigger societal disruption, because people immediately associate empty stores with shortages and crisis."

For attackers, the visibility can be just as important as the financial damage, according to Pilling.

Fredrik Blix, strategic cybersecurity advisor for Visente AB, said that the hack itself isn't sophisticated, as the control units for the refrigerators "sit directly on the public internet," with built-in support logins where the password can be obtained on the same day, according to information from researchers published in August.

"The vendor patched it in August," Blix said in a LinkedIn post. "Thousands of units never got the update, and the oldest models can't be updated at all."

Blix noted that the silence behind the attack is the most "interesting clue" as it rules out ransomware crews, leaving two options. Someone either carried out the attack simply because they can, or because someone wants to create "unease in Ukraine-supporting countries without leaving fingerprints," Blix said.

Sophos' Pilling noted that banks, government organizations, manufacturers, utilities, and retailers all rely on connected systems that are managed remotely.

"The lesson from these incidents is that an attacker doesn't necessarily need an extraordinarily sophisticated capability to create disruption if they can find an exposed system that controls something important in the physical world," Pilling added.

While there's no evidence AI has played a role in these attacks, elsewhere criminals have been using AI to speed up the scale and efficiency of attacks, said Rafe Pilling, senior director of threat intelligence at Sophos Counter Threat Unit.

"Even if AI is not being used today in these waves of attacks against refrigeration units, we should be alive to the fact that in future AI is likely to play a significant role in orchestration and execution of attacks like this," Pilling said. "It will become the norm and not an outlier."

Meanwhile, the U.K.'s National Cyber Security Centre warned Tuesday in a new report that the region's critical national infrastructure needs to be prepared for ransomware attacks involving AI-powered malware, which will become increasingly likely over the next two years.

"For decades, we have collectively got away with doing tech on the cheap," NCSC CEO Richard Horne said. "We can be a nation where leaders of all organisations actively choose to invest with urgency... and where all organisations make security and resilience a fundamental requirement, not an afterthought."

#2929. Coinbase net income surges 66% in third quarter, benefiting from bitcoin investment gains

Coinbase Global earned third-quarter adjusted net income of $719 million, up 66% from $433 million a year ago, largely due to a recovery in the cryptocurrency market.

Coinbase said it earned $2.51 a share in the latest quarter, although it wasn't immediately clear if that compared with the 6-cent-per-share profit that Wall Street analysts had estimated. Revenue of $1.20 billion trailed the $1.233 billion expected by analysts polled by LSEG.

Net income at the San Francisco-based platform surged thanks to the value of its volatile cryptocurrency investment portfolio, which can swing wildly even when the composition of the underlying holdings are little changed.

The third quarter value of Coinbase's crypto investment portfolio climbed due to a resurgence in the digital asset market. The price of bitcoin — often used as a gauge for all crypto assets — rose 39% in the three-month period ended Sept. 30 and the flagship cryptocurrency recently changed hands at roughly $80,809.

In the third quarter, Coinbase deepened its push into non-crypto products and services, including artificial intelligence integrations for traders, tokenized real-world assets and prediction markets.

In July, VP of product Max Branzburg told CNBC that Coinbase would debut an "everything exchange" spotlighting tokenized stocks and other real-world assets, derivatives trading, prediction markets and early-stage token sales. The trading platform also introduced Coinbase for Agents in early October, supporting users that want to use ChatGPT, Muse or other AI agents to manage their portfolios or research and trade various assets.

Coinbase also expanded its prediction market offerings, making a wider range of event contracts available to users.

In the third quarter, prediction markets revenue grew to more than $200 million on an annual basis, while the number of traders on those markets increased 60% compared with the second quarter.

#2927. Trump midterm election lawsuits, state pushback come to a head Tuesday

The Trump administration's monthslong campaign to challenge how states conduct elections and pushback against those efforts come to a head as Election Day arrives Tuesday.

Those efforts have included lawsuits, an attempt to implement a new U.S. Postal Service rule for mail-in ballots this year, threatening letters to state election officials and a plan to increase the number of poll monitors.

The administration could continue such efforts beyond Election Day.

Democratic lawmakers, attorneys general and advocacy groups are poised for battle if the administration tries to interfere with voting sites or challenge ballot counts, particularly in the states that could determine whether President Donald Trump's fellow Republicans lose their narrow Senate majority.

"Got to be prepared for anything, and we are," California Attorney General Rob Bonta told CNBC on Monday evening.

"We've been, you know, blocking and tackling and protecting and defending our elections for months and through the last few weeks of the voting window, and it'll all culminate tomorrow. And each of Trump's attempts has failed," Bonta said.

After a series of legal losses by Trump's Department of Justice in election cases, "I'm not sure what he has up his sleeves," the attorney general said.

But he also said that he and other California officials, along with the 23 other Democratic state attorneys general for months have been preparing for Election Day and potential actions by the Trump administration "to disenfranchise and suppress votes and voters."

"We game-planned, and you know, war-gamed and tabletop- and scenario planned all the possibilities," Bonta said.

"We're ready for National Guard or ICE or Marines at polling stations near polling stations, armed people near polling stations," he said. "We are ready for ballot seizures tomorrow, and I think we're ready for the monitors that are coming."

Bonta said that if those extreme events happened, California officials would quickly seek relief from the court system.

He noted, "We have laws in place right now where it is a criminal offense" to remove ballots from the custody and control of an election official.

On Monday, multiple federal courts were dealing with urgent motions in election-related lawsuits and appeals by and against the Trump administration.

The cases include an appeal by the DOJ of the dismissal Sunday of its lawsuit seeking a delay in the certification of Alaska's Senate election if incumbent Republican Sen. Dan Sullivan loses by a margin that could be attributable to voter confusion from having a man with the same name on the ballot.

In another case, the DOJ on Monday asked the Supreme Court to block California from counting about 20,000 mail-in ballots from voters whom the DOJ suggests might not be U.S. citizens. Hours later, the Supreme Court rejected that request without any noted dissents.

In U.S. District Court in Washington, a DOJ lawyer told Judge Reggie Walton on Monday that the department expected to deploy 900 people to monitor polling places at sites in all 50 states.

"In 2024, there were about 100 monitors," the lawyer, Kathryn Barragan, said.

The DOJ separately has said it will deploy approximately 1,000 election monitors and observers.

Her statement came at a hearing where Walton was considering an emergency request by the NAACP to bar the DOJ from using election monitors who are law enforcement personnel from federal agencies, including the Bureau of Alcohol, Tobacco, Firearms and Explosives, the Drug Enforcement Administration and the Bureau of Prisons.

John Cusick, assistant counsel at the Legal Defense Fund, a civil rights group, said the administration has used the DOJ, Department of Homeland Security and other federal agencies to target election procedures this election cycle for three main reasons.

"First and foremost, it's an attempt to intimidate voters and election officials," Cusick said in an interview.

"The second component is confusion" about election rules, he said. "And the third, the unfortunate part, is trying to lay the groundwork for challenging the post-election counting and certification of ballots."

He said there has been a "constant barrage" by the administration to target election workers and election officials with unlawful actions.

That included, he said, the DOJ sending state election officials letters warning them of potential criminal prosecution if they fail to retain election records.

"That has created such a chilling effect," Cusick said. "There's no doubt that the threats are threats to our democracy."

The DOJ, which did not respond to a request for comment by CNBC for this article, has previously defended its election-related efforts.

"Voting by noncitizens and other ineligible voters is a federal crime, and the department will enforce the law whether a case involves one illegal ballot or hundreds," the DOJ's rapid response account on X said on Oct. 12.

Attorney General Todd Blanche, when asked on Oct. 22 if there was any scenario in which the DOJ would try to block states from certifying their election results, conceded that was possible.

"I mean, is there any scenario?" Blanche told reporters in Virginia. "Yes, of course. There's scenarios that we could all come up with, hypothetically, you know, some doomsday scenario, for example."

"It is not DOJ's intention to, and it's never our intention to improperly interfere with elections and the state confirmation process or certification process," the attorney general said.

"But, you know, to say that there's no circumstances that we would ever do that? Of course I'm not going to say that."

Cusick and other experts note the president and executive branch of the federal government have no official role in administering elections, which are handled by the states as designated under the Constitution.

In her ruling Sunday dismissing the DOJ's Alaska Senate lawsuit, District Court Judge Sharon Gleason said the Trump administration was making requests that involve "the time, place, and manner of Alaska's election, which are the responsibilities of the State, not the executive branch of the federal government."

On Monday, a bipartisan group of former election officials in a filing told the 9th Circuit U.S. Court of Appeals that "increased federal pressure and threats on election officials ... are part of a pattern by the administration of attempting to assert authority over elections and questioning the integrity of state and local officials."

The filing came in a case where the DOJ is appealing an order issued Friday by a federal judge in California barring the Trump administration "from carrying out a series of efforts to actually or attempting to intimidate, threaten, or coerce election workers who aid others in exercising their right to vote."

The judge cited threats by the DOJ to prosecute state and local election officials if they process and count ballots using procedures that are legally required.

The ruling noted that on Oct. 26, the administration sent a letter to the state of Washington identifying potential noncitizens and "demanding that the Washington Secretary of State halt the processing of ballots."

Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer, D-N.Y., on Sunday night convened the entire Senate Democratic Caucus for a briefing by him and election attorney Marc Elias about what a Democratic aide said was the "current landscape of election threats from Trump and his allies." The aide spoke on condition of anonymity to discuss the private briefing.

"Senate Democrats are not waiting until Election Day to confront these threats," the aide said. "If Trump and his allies attempt to interfere with the will of the voters, Senate Democrats are prepared to fight back immediately and aggressively, on Election Day and for as long as it takes to ensure the results are respected."

Bonta, California's attorney general, said he expected Trump and the DOJ To continue targeting states over their election procedures through the next cycle in 2028, when voters will choose a new president.

"We can't be naive about it. We have to expect it to get worse, prepared for it to get worse. I don't want to be alarmist," Bonta said. "I am a realist though, and I see the way he thinks and acts and what he's done, and maybe we'll be prepared for the worst, and it'll never happen."

"But we can't not be prepared for it. And I expect him to escalate as he gets more desperate."

#2925. Warriors vs. Suns player grades: Steph Curry saves the day, Charles Bassey has career night

The Golden State Warriors won an odd game on Monday, beating the Phoenix Suns 116-113. They once again dug themselves a first-quarter hole, and this time it took them a bit longer to climb out of it. But a win is a win is a win, and the Warriors have now won five straight … something they didn't do at any point last season.

It was a game that featured some great performances and some awful ones, so let's grade them! As always, grades are based on my expectations for each player, with a "B" grade representing the average performance for that individual.

Note: True-shooting percentage (TS) is a scoring efficiency metric that accounts for threes and free throws. Entering Monday's games, league-average TS was 57.2%.

28 minutes, 6 points, 5 rebounds, 1 block, 1 turnover, 3 fouls, 2-for-7 shooting, 2-for-5 threes, 42.9% TS, -17

Richard got the start in this one, for the second time this season. It was a play to get more perimeter defense on a team starting Jalen Green, Devin Booker, and Miles Bridges, and Richard took turns defending all three. As we've grown accustomed to, he played good defense, though it wasn't the best he's been on that side of the ball. There were definitely plenty of moments where he got beat, or was a little over eager and out of position.

He did some good things on offense, but not too much. He's there for defense, though, and he played it.

Grade: BPost-game bonus: Worst plus/minus on the team.

29 minutes, 16 points, 5 rebounds, 5 assists, 1 steal, 1 block, 2 turnovers, 4 fouls, 3-for-10 shooting, 2-for-6 threes, 8-for-9 free throws, 57.3% TS, -11

Poor Yaxel. Sunday was the first time this season that I didn't write a grades article and, fittingly, he had his best game of the season. Obviously that's his biggest concern, so I'm sorry, Yaxel. Please know that you would have gotten an A with two plus signs had I penned some grades.

For this game the grade has to be a little bit lower, though. Lendeborg got off to a slow start (as did the entire team), and picked up two quick fouls while struggling to get anything going on offense.

As the game went on, though, the rookie picked things up considerably, and he ended up having a very strong second half. I remain in awe of his restraint: you just don't see a lot of rookies who are so comfortable picking and choosing their shots, and not hoisting an unnecessary number of jumpers. Critically, even after his tough start, the team turned to him on multiple possessions down the stretch, and he very often delivered, while also having a glass-check block late in the game that was probably the defensive highlight of the contest.

I'm still struggling to grade him, though, because I'm struggling to figure out what my expectations should be. This guy genuinely might be the first rookie All-Star since Blake Griffin, so how do I grade him? With all respect to Draymond Green and Brandin Podziemski, Lendeborg is far and away the second-best player on the team (until Jimmy Butler III returns, at least, and maybe after, too), and that makes grading difficult. But who cares, the show is a hell of a lot of fun to watch.

17 minutes, 4 points, 4 rebounds, 2 assists, 1 steal, 1 turnover, 1 foul, 1-for-2 shooting, 2-for-2 free throws, 69.4% TS, -6

The Warriors over the last week have had the best vibes they've possessed since the first round of the 2025 playoffs. And Green put that in jeapoardy. Two minutes into the third quarter, he got into it with Phoenix center Khaman Maluach. After being pushed to the floor, Green got up, committed a hard foul, and kind of threw Maluach to the floor. What followed was an altercation with Dillon Brooks, in which Green shoved Brooks around the neck area. He received a technical foul for each act, and was subsequently ejected for the first time this year.

Ejections come with the territory with Green, and I'm usually one to defend him more quickly than others. After all, these are grown adults playing a high-stakes contact sport … some pushing and shoving and yelling never hurt anyone. But this was too far, and, while not as bad as his incidents a few years back with Rudy Gobert, Domantas Sabonis, and Jusuf Nurkić, these started to cross over the line from "aggressive basketball" to "violence."

Thankfully for Green, the team responded to his absence well, but it didn't need to go that way. His absence didn't end up costing the Warriors the win, but it could hurt them down the road, as it's possible he'll get a one-game suspension for his actions.

I've giving Green a harsh grade for taking himself out of the game and possibly the next one, and not playing particularly well before then, either. But it's worth noting that the Warriors players spoke after the game about how Green's ejection fired them up, so … take that for what it's worth.

33 minutes, 20 points, 3 rebounds, 2 assists, 1 steal, 3 turnovers, 2 fouls, 7-for-15 shooting, 3-for-8 threes, 3-for-4 free throws, 59.7% TS, -4

The Warriors, over the course of this nice winning streak, have developed a habit of starting slow and finishing strong. And Podziemski completely embodies that. Podz has really been struggling out of the gates lately, but as soon as he emerges from the locker room at halftime, he's a completely different player.

That was again the case in this game. He couldn't get anything going in the first half, and then he was bailing the Dubs out time and time again in the second. If he had paced his performance over the course of the game, I'd probably give him a "B" for a very solid, if not noteworthy performance. But because he had so many big plays on both ends of the court with the game on the line, we'll bump him up a half grade.

33 minutes, 34 points, 6 rebounds, 7 assists, 1 steal, 3 turnovers, 2 fouls, 9-for-21 shooting, 5-for-13 threes, 11-for-12 free throws, 64.7% TS, +21

I'm sure that when the game ended and the Dubs retreated to the locker rooms, Green was waiting with a big hug for Curry. Golden State was losing when Green was ejected, and had the score held, the biggest story — maybe the only story — would have been Draymond's transgressions. Instead, Curry went ballistic as soon as Green left the court, and scored 18 points in the third quarter alone as the Dubs rattled off 42 in the frame to tie things up.

Curry looks better this year than in the past few years, and this was his third consecutive game with at least 30 points and five made threes. He seems energized, and that's a joy to behold, and a harbinger of how good this team can be.

Grade: APost-game bonus: Led the team in points and assists; best plus/minus on the team.

20 minutes, 2 points, 2 rebounds, 1 assist, 1 steal, 2 turnovers, 4 fouls, 1-for-3 shooting, 0-for-2 threes, 33.3% TS, +3

Not surprisingly, GPII got a lot of run against a team with Booker, Green, and Bridges. And also not surprisingly, he handled those assignments really well. He hasn't had one of his bursts of offense yet this season, and he had a few sloppy plays in this one, but overall, solid performance.

22 minutes, 13 points, 4 rebounds, 3 assists, 1 foul, 5-for-6 shooting, 1-for-2 threes, 2-for-3 free throws, 88.8% TS, +20

Well that was a damn fine performance. Niang showed everything the Warriors are looking for from him: he drained open shots, passed to the open man, played strong defense while guarding a variety of positions, crashed tho glass, made the smart play, and hustled. He's very quickly building a strong rapport with Curry, which is huge; the result is that he's a very trustworthy player to have on the court down the stretch.

It's not always going to work this well, but when it does? Yeesh.

28 minutes, 2 points, 7 rebounds, 2 assists, 2 steals, 2 fouls, 1-for-9 shooting, 0-for-5 threes, 0-for-1 free throws, 10.6% TS, 0 +/-

Remember when I said, just a few words ago, that it isn't always going to work this well with Niang? Well, that's the case for all players in that sort of role … and Melton is living proof. Melton has had some really good games lately but this one was a bit of a clunker … at least scoring the ball, as he could not get any shots to fall.

He did some other good things though. He took care of the rock, rebounded really well, and played decent enough defense. But it's hard to impact the game when you shoot 1-for-9 from the field, and he just looked a step behind on a lot of plays.

23 minutes, 19 points, 8 rebounds, 1 assist, 1 turnover, 4 fouls, 7-for-10 shooting, 5-for-6 free throws, 75.2% TS, +10

What a performance by Bassey! His role has been very undefined this year, which makes sense. He's still trying to carve out a role in the NBA, he's recovering from a preseason injury, and the Warriors needs fluctuate based on who they're playing, how Green and Lendeborg are performing, and whether or not Al Horford is playing.

But Bassey got a decent-sized role in this one, and then that role expanded because he was playing so damn well. He was crafty around the rim, finding ways to contort his body to score around and through contact, en route to a career-high 19 points. He not only grabbed a bunch of boards, but did so emphatically, patrolling the paint every time a shot went up.

He even had a great memeable moment, when the ball went out of bounds and he hugged a fan.

Re-signing him is just one of many fantastic moves that the Warriors made this offseason. Hindsight is 20/20, as no one was saying they made good moves a few months ago!

Grade: A+Post-game bonus: Led the team in rebounds.

Ike somewhat surprisingly got some run in this game, especially after Green was ejected. He didn't do anything statistically — he didn't log a single stat — but I liked what we saw from him on defense.

39 seconds, 0 points, 0-for-1 shooting, 0-for-1 threes, 0.0% TS, 0 +/-

Cryer briefly got onto the court for an end-of-quarter offense situation, but that was all we saw of him.

Monday's inactives: Jimmy Butler III, Al Horford, Moses Moody, Kristaps Porziņģis, Gui Santos, Brandon Williams

#2924. From the Bills to 49ers, 1 NFL trade deadline target for each division leader

The 2026 NFL season is nearing its midpoint and the playoff race is shaping up to be one of the most exciting in recent memory.

Almost every division race remains competitive as the NFL turns its calendar to Week 9. The Jacksonville Jaguars have a three-game lead in the AFC South, but most are separated by just one or two games.

With that in mind, division leaders aren't going to want to stand pat ahead of the league's Nov. 10 trade deadline. Many will need to stay aggressive to keep themselves on top of their power structures, especially in divisions like the AFC North and NFC North, where no teams are below .500 for the season.

What types of trades could these division leaders consider? Here's a look at one trade that each of the division leaders entering Week 9 should consider with the NFL trade deadline drawing nearer.

Week 3: Denver Broncos cheerleaders perform during the "Sunday Night Football" game against the Los Angeles Rams at Empower Field at Mile High.

Week 3: The Maracanã Stadium gets set to host the International Series game between the Baltimore Ravens and Dallas Cowboys in Rio de Janeiro, Brazil.

Week 1: Fans arrive ahead of the NFL game between the Los Angeles Rams and San Francisco 49ers at the Melbourne Cricket Ground (in Melbourne, Australia. The 49ers won the first NFL game played in Australia, 27-7.

Week 1: Kim Thayil of Soundgarden and Mike McCready of Pearl Jam perform during halftime of the 2026 NFL Kickoff Game between the Seattle Seahawks and New England Patriots at Lumen Field.

Week 3: Denver Broncos cheerleaders perform during the "Sunday Night Football" game against the Los Angeles Rams at Empower Field at Mile High.

Week 3: The Maracanã Stadium gets set to host the International Series game between the Baltimore Ravens and Dallas Cowboys in Rio de Janeiro, Brazil.

Week 3: Corgi dog competitions take place during halftime of the game between the Detroit Lions and New York Jets at Ford Field.

Week 3: A fan looks on during warmups prior to the game between the Cleveland Browns and Carolina Panthers at Huntington Bank Field in Cleveland, Ohio.

Week 2: Singer Taylor Swift watches the game between the Indianapolis Colts and Kansas City Chiefs at Arrowhead Stadium.

Week 1: Fans arrive ahead of the NFL game between the Los Angeles Rams and San Francisco 49ers at the Melbourne Cricket Ground (in Melbourne, Australia. The 49ers won the first NFL game played in Australia, 27-7.

Week 1: Kim Thayil of Soundgarden and Mike McCready of Pearl Jam perform during halftime of the 2026 NFL Kickoff Game between the Seattle Seahawks and New England Patriots at Lumen Field.

Gregory Rousseau has been a revelation for the Bills this year, racking up seven sacks and 37 pressures – good for the fifth-most league-wide entering Week 9, per the NFL's Next Gen Stats – across just seven games.

Despite that, the Bills rank just 24th league-wide in pressure rate, even after bolstering their numbers in a favorable Week 8 matchup against Baltimore Ravens third-string quarterback Joe Fagnano. That's largely because they have struggled to generate pressure across from Rousseau, with No. 2 pass rusher Bradley Chubb ranking second on the team with just 1.5 sacks and 19 pressures.

Sweat would go a long way toward improving those numbers. He has three sacks and 19 pressures while playing for a lowly Cardinals defense, and he would get a big-time boost playing across from Rousseau. Sweat had a career-best 12 sacks last season and won Super Bowl 58 with the Philadelphia Eagles, so the 29-year-old would be an ideal organizational fit for the win-now Bills.

The Steelers have been better than expected on offense this season, but they could use extra depth at receiver. Both Michael Pittman Jr. and Roman Wilson are on IR, which has left the Steelers thin behind DK Metcalf and Germie Bernard.

Downs would be a natural fit for the Steelers given his skills as a slot separator. He is averaging 3.2 yards of separation per route run, according to the NFL's Next Gen Stats, which ranks 19th among 73 qualified receivers this season. The 5-9, 171-pounder's ability to get open quickly should mesh well with Aaron Rodgers' current playing style, which relies heavily on the short passing game to keep Pittsburgh's offense on schedule.

The Steelers and Colts already swung a trade involving Pittman during the offseason, so it might not be too difficult for them to agree on compensation to complete a Downs trade.

The Jaguars have sported the NFL's best run defense during the 2026 season, but they have struggled to consistently contain opposing passing attacks. They enter Week 9 having allowed the fourth-most passing yards per game (253.9) and could stand to add some talent to their secondary for that reason.

Baker would be a massive upgrade for Jacksonville's safety room. The perennial Pro Bowler is undersized at 5-10, 195 pounds, but he has long been a savvy playmaker with excellent instincts and versatility. This season, he has generated 51 tackles, a career-high 3.5 sacks and an interception despite playing for a leaky Cardinals stop unit.

The only question is about whether the Cardinals will trade Baker, who has been with the team for a decade. But given that Baker is 30 and the Cardinals don't appear to be close to contention, the franchise could part with him if they get a significant enough offer.

The Broncos have one of the NFL's deepest rosters, so they don't have many glaring needs. They could swing big for an upgrade at running back, like Jonathan Taylor, but if they can't get him, adding a higher-end tight end to their room would be a solid move.

Evan Engram hasn't developed into the threat many expected he would when he signed with the Broncos before the 2025 season. He, Nate Adkins and Adam Trautman have combined for just 23 catches and 220 yards this season – albeit with four touchdowns – so adding a player like Goedert, who has 26 catches for 258 yards and two touchdowns in six games on his own, could go a long way toward giving Bo Nix another security blanket.

The Eagles have long been rumored to be considering trading Goedert, so offloading the 31-year-old in the midst of what looks like a lost season would be sensible. It would also give them a chance to see how second-round rookie Eli Stowers looks in an expanded role.

The Giants are thin at the running back position after releasing Najee Harris and losing Tyrone Tracy Jr. to a hamstring injury. Backfield leader Cam Skattebo came out of the team's Week 7 game with a mild hamstring injury of his own, and John Harbaugh also benched the second-year back earlier in the season as he dealt with fumbling issues.

All of that could lead the Giants to target a reliable veteran at running back. Could that be Jonathan Taylor? Surely, but if the team is confident in Skattebo being its longer-term starter, general manager Joe Schoen may pursue more of a short-term stopgap who can split the workload with the Arizona State product.

Pollard is on pace to log his fifth consecutive 1,000-yard season and has posted a combined 236 rushing yards and five rushing touchdowns over his last two games. The 29-year-old will be a free agent after the season, so it wouldn't likely cost too much to pry him away from the 1-6 Titans.

Taylor will have many suitors on the open market, but no team could use him more than the Vikings.

Minnesota's offense has sputtered this season despite the team's 6-1 record. Notably, the Vikings' Aaron Jones and Jordan Mason-led running game ranks 27th in rushing EPA per play this season, per the NFL's Next Gen Stats. Neither looked particularly sharp against the Detroit Lions in Week 8, and with Jones nursing a nagging knee injury, it would behoove the Vikings to add a high-caliber weapon to their backfield.

Taylor wouldn't just be an instant upgrade for the Vikings. He would also help give Kevin O'Connell's offense a much-needed identity. Minnesota could focus on becoming a ground-and-pound offense that would pair very well with the team's aggressive, attacking-style defense.

JONATHAN TAYLOR TRADE RUMORS: Ranking RB's best fits if Colts deal him

The Panthers have unsurprisingly struggled to defend the pass since their top two cornerbacks, Jaycee Horn and Mike Jackson, were placed on IR due to injuries. Carolina will need to shore up its depth at the position to have a chance to win the wide-open NFC South, so targeting Woolen would be sensible.

Woolen's first season with the Eagles hasn't gone as either party would have hoped, as the 27-year-old has surrendered a career-worst 116.1 passer rating, per Pro Football Focus. Still, the 6-4, 205-pound cornerback is a freak athlete and spent four years as part of a productive Seattle Seahawks secondary, so there's hope a change of scenery could allow him to bounce back.

The Panthers could also look to target an offensive tackle on the trade market should a starting-caliber player end up on the block. However, that seems unlikely to happen given the dearth of high-end talent at the position league-wide.

The 49ers have dealt with a plethora of injuries at receiver this season with players like Deebo Samuel, Ricky Pearsall and De'Zhaun Stribling all missing extended time. Mike Evans has largely been up to the task of carrying San Francisco's receiver room, but he was unable to play in the 49ers' lone loss of the season, and it showed in the passing game.

The 49ers need to add healthy depth to their receiver room if only to ensure their offense isn't overly reliant on Evans to carry it. Targeting someone like Josh Downs could make sense, but if John Lynch wants to swing for the fences, Thomas Jr. could be worth chasing.

Thomas has seemingly fallen out of favor in Jacksonville and hasn't caught more than three passes in a game this season. Still, he's just two years removed from a rookie campaign where he posted 87 catches, 1,282 yards and 10 touchdowns, so maybe a change of scenery would serve him well.
