#2923. Steph Curry, Charles Bassey help Warriors to fifth straight win

A Golden State Warriors season doesn't truly begin until Draymond Green gets kicked out of a game. This year, they have Charles Bassey to pick up the slack.

Bassey scored a career-high 19 points and collected eight rebounds, holding down the fort inside as the Warriors erased a 17-point deficit to beat the Phoenix Suns, 116-113. Golden State put up 42 points in the third quarter, Steph Curry exceeded 30 points for the third straight game, and De'Anthony Melton made two huge defensive plays late to lock down the victory.

Let's get the ejection out of the way early. It's become exhausting to write about Green losing control on the court, which coincidentally tends to happen when he's playing badly or the Warriors are losing. Early in the third quarter, Green took exception to contact from second-year Suns center Khaman Maluach, which left him on the ground. To be honest, it looked like Green was flopping for a call.

Green jumped up, grabbed Maluach around the waist, and threw him to the ground. When his longtime nemesis Dillon Brooks ran in to defend his teammate, Green grabbed Brooks by the jersey and jammed a fist into the side of his neck. The result? One technical foul for Brooks, and two for Green, which meant his 23rd regular-season ejection (he's been kicked out of two playoff games and suspended for two more). He finished with four points, four rebounds, and two assists.

Brooks claimed he was running in to de-escalate the situation, something Brooks is known for never, ever doing, but he really wasn't in the wrong there. I've watched the replay multiple times and I strongly support anyone who would grab Draymond and yell, "What are you doing?" at him. There's no word that Maluach was injured in the altercation, but he also didn't really play afterward.

After the game, Curry said Green's ejection "lit a fire beneath us," but what lit up the Warriors right away was Bassey's energy. They missed their first two shots after Green's ejection, but Bassey tipped in a miss and drew a foul, then found Curry for one of his four third-quarter triples.

Bassey scored six points in the quarter and 10 after halftime, getting to the line for five free throws and making four. He was opportunistic, setting screens and rolling hard to the rim while also taking advantage of mismatches with smaller Suns defenders.

Of course, Steph Curry scoring 18 points in the quarter was a pretty big factor as well. He shot 6-for-9 in nine minutes, putting up double-digit points in the third quarter for the third time in his last four games (he had 8 in the other one). In the four games Curry played during the Warriors' five-game winning streak, he's scored 55 points in the third quarter, perhaps because he's gone back to his old habit of reading critical tweets about his first-half performance during halftime.

Graham Ike played seven minutes and twenty-three seconds Monday night and did not record a single stat. That's officially known as a "7 trillion," with the seven referring to Ike's minutes, followed by 12 consecutive zeroes in the other categories. He was -1 in those seven minutes, which means that while Ike did not have an individual moment statistically regarded for posterity, at least he didn't foul, turn the ball over, or get in the way of better players. Plus, he had a sweet hockey assist on Curry's corner three.

Curry finally missed a free throw in the final seconds of the game, one that could have iced the game, but it's hard to judge him too harshly when he started the season 51-for-51. Curry is also averaging 8.7 free throw attempts per game, which is 37% more than his previous single-season high. Maybe the referees are finally showing some respect to the baby-faced assassin Dangerfield.

The Warriors got Green and Curry back after they sat out Sunday's win over the L.A. Clippers, while Will Richard made his first start of the season in place of the injured Gui Santos and the too-slow-to-guard-Devin-Booker Georges Niang. Or Steve Kerr simply wanted The Minivan to run all over the Suns' reserves, as he did by shooting 5-for-6 and putting up 13 points, finishing +20 in his 23 minutes.

It was a rough start, mainly because the Warriors kept settling for, and missing, three-pointers, finishing 2-for-15 from behind the arc in the quarter. A typical slow Warriors start involves a lot of turnovers, reckless fouling, or indifferent defense, but this one was simple: They could not buy a bucket, even with just two turnovers. It could have been worse, but Bassey drew some fouls and hustled his way to seven points.

Phoenix blocked the Warriors' first three-point attempts of the second quarter, meaning they started the game going 2-for-17 from deep. After a Miles Bridges three, Phoenix led by 17 points with 8:29 to play. Even after Yaxel Lendeborg made two threes, Phoenix was still up by 16 points with 3:45 to go in the quarter. After Devin Booker (26 points) hit a short jumper, Kerr called timeout, Curry and Green came back in the game, and ESPN declared the Suns had a win probability of 88.6%. Do they do election predictions?

The Suns wouldn't make a field goal the rest of the half, as Brandin Podziemski (20 points) led a 12-0 run that got the Warriors firmly back into the game (Suns win probability down to 62.3%). Kerr also won a challenge after Curry forced a turnover and the referees awarded Phoenix the ball, which was reversed and turned into a Curry bucket on the ensuing inbounds play.

Golden State finally tied the game in the third quarter on a Curry bucket, but didn't take the lead until early in the 4th, on a bucket from (who else?) Bassey.

The game went back and forth throughout the final quarter. The Warriors led by as many as seven points, then lost their lead, and finally went ahead for good on a Niang three-pointer with under four minutes to go. The rest of the way, they did it with defense.

Curry stole the ball from Booker. Melton stole the ball from Jalen Green. Then, with the Warriors clinging to a five-point lead, Melton drew an offensive foul on Booker, his sixth. Not a great closing effort for Booker, who had the same number of turnovers as points in the 4th: Two.

The Warriors made it interesting, of course. After Booker's ejection, Lendeborg blocked a Jalen Green layup out of bounds, but the Suns got a dunk after inbounding the ball. With 20 seconds left, Curry traveled, and the Suns, who were out of timeouts, cut the lead to a single point on Green's dunk. But Curry made two free throws, Green missed the rim entirely trying to miss on purpose, and Collin Gillespie's last-gasp 37-footer missed at the buzzer.

Curry is always the MVP of this team, but honorable mentions for Monday go to Bassey, Podz, and the security guy who dragged Draymond to the bench before he could start throttling Brooks' windpipe.

Brooks had 18 points, with four turnovers. Jalen Green had eight assists, with four turnovers. Miles Bridges had 21 points and four steals and remains a domestic abuser who has no place in the NBA. However, he did only turn the ball over once.

It was a big win for the Warriors, who didn't win five straight for all of last season. It was also a big win for Warriors assistant coach Frank Vogel, who has seen the Dubs defeat the two most recent NBA teams to fire him within four nights. You're next, Orlando Magic and Indiana Pacers! They didn't want you, Frank!

More accurately, the Miami Heat are next, coming to town on Thursday. I have a feeling Draymond Green won't be in uniform for that one.

#2915. Fantasy Basketball Trends: Key Warriors, old and new, showing out in the early going

The first three weeks of the 2026-27 NBA season have reshaped fantasy basketball rankings. Warriors rookie Yaxel Lendeborg is a must-add breakout, Stephen Curry is scoring 30.0 points per game at age 38 and Victor Wembanyama bounced back with a 43-point outing. Walker Kessler is stacking rebounds and blocks in a new offense, while Jalen Johnson's shooting slump raises buy-low questions.

Lendeborg's 113 points through five games and Kessler's 2.5 blocks per game drive waiver wire demand. Our start-or-sit breakdown covers who to buy low, who to sell high and which hot streaks are real for your 2026-27 fantasy basketball lineup.

Lendeborg is off to an incredible start and putting critics to rest with impactful performances that are translating to wins for his squad. He kicked off his NBA career by amassing the third-most points (113) by a player through their first five games since 2000, behind Kevin Durant and Paolo Banchero, and the first player since Shaquille O'Neal (1992) to have 113 points and 46 rebounds through his first five games. He is also the first Warriors rookie to log 25 points in three straight games (his third, fourth, and fifth career games) since Stephen Curry (2009).

Lendeborg has already topped the 30-point mark twice in his eight outings, helping his team to five straight wins. He is getting the job done with efficiency, shooting 50.0% from the field, including a blistering 40.0% from deep (6.3 attempts per game) and 76.9% from the foul line (6.5 attempts per game). The rookie has cemented himself as a key part of the 2026-27 Warriors' identity, playing 31.9 minutes per game and ranking second on the team in points and field goals attempted per game. He is very likely to continue with an impressive trajectory and will surely remain arguably the most important x-factor in his squad's success.

Curry is 38 years old and playing like a star in his prime, delivering a start to the season full of jaw-dropping highlights and historic feats. On October 26, against the Nuggets, he tallied 24 points on 8-for-8 shooting, including 3-for-3 from deep and 5-for-5 from the foul line, marking his second career "perfect" game, but his first with over 20 points. He also started the season shooting 39-for-39 from the foul line to set the record for most makes without a miss through a player's first four games. Additionally, he is on pace to rank as the highest-scoring 38-year-old point guard in history, as he is averaging 30.0 points per game, while the second-highest average belongs to Steve Nash, who averaged 12.7 points at the age of 38.

Curry was bothered by injury issues last season but played at least 70 games in each of the previous two campaigns. If he can stay healthy this season, he could be on pace for one of the most impressive campaigns of his career, as he is shooting 46.8% from the field, including 42.9% from deep, and 98.1% from the foul line through six games played. He is also taking care of the ball with 2.3 assists per turnover, which ties the second-best rate of his career. Curry's dominant play is not only impressive on an individual scale but is helping his team win games, as the Warriors are on a five-game heater, erasing their 0-3 start.

Wembanyama opened the season with a modest 12-point, 13-rebound performance, leading to a loss for his team and giving the most imaginative of skeptics reason to question his ability. However, the fourth-year phenom took no time to quiet the chatter, as he bounced back by averaging 31.2 points, 10.2 rebounds, 3.0 assists, 1.4 steals and 3.8 blocks over the next five games, including a 43-point outing in the game before last. In that game, he also became the first Spurs player to tally at least 40 points and five blocks in a game since David Robinson (1996), as well as the third player in history (behind Kristaps Porziņģis and Bob McAdoo) with at least 40 points and six blocks in a game before turning 23 years old.

On the other hand, Wembanyama put up another relatively modest stat line in his most recent outing, but he was also limited to just 24 minutes in a 22-point win over the Jazz. He is shooting 46.9% from the field on the season but is definitely showing some struggles from deep, where he is hitting just 26.2% on 6.0 three-point attempts per game. This is a notable dip from his 34.9% shooting from deep last season. Although the long-range shooting has been a highlight of the big man's game, as long as he continues to thrive closer to the basket, it should offset any other issues. The deep shooting will likely even out over a few more games, as he gets into the groove of the season.

Kessler is looking like a good fit with his new squad, averaging 14.4 points, 10.5 rebounds, 1.3 assists, 1.1 steals and 2.5 blocks per game through eight outings. He is also shooting a brilliant 71.4% from the field, as he stays true to his role and works almost exclusively in the restricted area. His free-throw shooting leaves something to be desired at just 53.6 percent effectiveness, but it is a small price to pay for the efficiency he brings in other areas, including giving up just 1.5 turnovers in his 30.8 minutes of action per game.

Kessler is coming off three straight double-doubles but has not yet recorded a 20-point double-double, despite opening the season with back-to-back 20-point games. He currently ranks sixth in the league in rebounds and third in blocks per game. He is likely to continue to excel in his current situation, with an offense led by top-tier scorers and playmakers Luka Dončić and Austin Reaves, and a defense bolstered by feisty forwards Ziaire Williams and Matisse Thybulle.

Johnson put the league on notice last season, averaging 22.5 points, 10.3 rebounds, 7.9 assists and 1.2 steals, while ranking second in the league in triple-doubles (13) and third in double-doubles (49) through his 72 games played. However, things have not panned out with the same luster so far this season, as he is shooting just 42.1% from the field and is averaging just 14.0 points per game over his last three outings. He has shot better than 48.5% from the field over his five seasons, and expectations were high leading into 2026-27, as the Hawks moved on from their previous star, Trae Young, seemingly in part to open the way for Johnson to take the reins.

Although it is early in the season, his struggles from the field bring reasonable cause for concern, as he is intended to lead the charge for a team that should have the highest of goals in mind. He continues to get to the rim and score from close range with efficiency, shooting 66.7 percent in/near the restricted area, but the mid-range and three-ball have not been working out the same, as he has hit just 3-for-25 from mid range and 8-for-31 from deep. On the other hand, concern should not equal panic, as Johnson is also averaging 9.0 rebounds and 7.9 assists per game and is likely to find his groove sooner rather than later.

#2910. Palantir sinks 2% after earnings as analysts point to slowdown in total contract value

Palantir shares fell on Tuesday after the software company reported third-quarter earnings and analysts pointed to signs of a slowdown in total contract value and other "forward indicators."

U.S. commercial revenue topped government for the first time, but the company closed $1.5 billion in TCV in the segment, up 11% from last year. In Q2, Palantir closed a record $2.1 billion worth of TCV in the segment, which was up 153% from the prior year.

Investors will "no doubt be looking for evidence in subsequent quarters that 11% growth in U.S. commercial TCV bookings is a fluke of duration and not something structural," a Piper Sandler note from Monday said. "We believe that is the case and demand for PLTR's solutions still very much outstrip supply."

In a Tuesday note, Goldman Sachs analysts noted that the company attributed some of this to shorter contract durations.

"The key will be converting shorter duration contracts into longer duration commitments, which Palantir has done successfully in the past," the analysts wrote.

Revenue nearly doubled from a year ago and blew past $2 billion for the first time, Palantir said in its earnings report.

The iPhone maker reported better-than-expected revenue in its fourth-quarter earnings, but fell short of estimates on iPad sales and issued a margin forecast that disappointed investors.

Apple's statement marked the first earnings report since John Ternus took over as CEO from Tim Cook in September. The stock fell more than 2% on Tuesday.

The company is reckoning with a global memory crunch and a scramble for computer components. Memory costs were higher this quarter than in the June period, the company said Monday. Despite the price surge, Ternus said Apple had just capped off its "best fiscal year ever."

He told CNBC's MacKenzie Sigalos that Apple's growth came despite supply constraints and foreign exchange headwinds.

"The concern is profitability," said Violeta Todorova, senior research analyst at Leverage Shares. "Management pointed to memory as the main pressure, together with the cost of rolling out Siri AI. Memory shortages are also expected to limit Mac and iPad supply, as demand has run ahead of plans."

Apple shares have risen 25% in 2026, ahead of other big tech names, but that premium leaves "little room for disappointment," Todorova added.

#2909. Patriots Power Rankings Week 9: Pats hold their ground

I understand the Patriots looked shaky in the first half against the Dolphins on Sunday. I understand that the team has been dealing with an overabundance of injuries, and the status of top players remains in question on when – or if – they will return this season. But New England also stormed back and won the game. What I don't understand is why the experts dropped the Pats down in the rankings this week.

The average of all these rankings turned out to be 10.5, which is reasonable. A fifth is too low, at least at the moment, and 14th is too high. Beating Green Bay on Sunday should give them a nice boost.

But while the Dolphins may have been playing for pride, the Packers will be fighting to stay alive in the NFC playoff race. Both the Patriots and Packers are coming into Week 9 hot. QB Drake Maye has posted MVP-type numbers over the last four games, (11 touchdowns, 1 INT) and crossing the 110 passer rating mark in each game. QB Jordan Love (8 TDs, 3 INT) has also been productive in this stretch, averaging over 300 yards and 2 TDs per game with a strong completion percentage.

The Packers are 2.5 point road underdogs but a solid matchup to New England, both offensively and defensively. If the Pats can play a clean game with regards to turnovers and penalties, they can win this one by a touchdown.

#2908. One Possession: Marcus Smart does Marcus Smart things against Celtics

One Possession: Marcus Smart does Marcus Smart things against Celtics originally appeared on NBC Sports Boston

Two 3s. Two steals. Two flops. Two middle fingers.

The Celtics got the full Marcus Smart experience on Monday night in Houston and — if we're being honest — it made us a bit nostalgic.

Sure, it was a lot more fun when the uniforms were flipped back in December 2017. That night, Smart busted out his entire bag of Smart tricks, including baiting James Harden into a pair of offensive foul calls as the Celtics improbably rallied to take down the Rockets at TD Garden.

Being on the other side of Smart's chaos-inducing playbook is decidedly less endearing.

Smart left his fingerprints all over Houston's late-game takeover. It started in the final minutes of the third quarter when Smart sold a shove from former teammate Jayson Tatum, earning Tatum his fourth foul and sending him to the bench with 1:39 to play in the frame.

The Celtics were down just five at that point, but Boston trailed by 16 by the time Tatum returned to the floor with 7:02 to play in the final quarter.

Smart hit 3-pointers on consecutive trips early in the fourth frame to help build that lead. Then, somewhat inexplicably, he threw his middle fingers in the air running back up the court. He would later suggest to reporters in Houston that it was "pent-up" emotions and some feedback to a Boston bench that had been previously vocal about his shot selection.

Before that, he 1) Tried to encourage referees to give Jayson Tatum a first-half technical as he vented about a whistle, 2) Nearly drew a Payton Pritchard charge before the play was overturned on video review, and 3) Did one of his familiar kip-ups to celebrate after nearly drawing that charge on Pritchard.

That's serious nostalgia for everyone who watched Smart in his nine seasons with Boston from 2014 to 2023. You might not always miss his shot selection, but no amount of analytics could quantify the Smart chaos quotient.

Every good team needs an agitator. The type of player that gets under your skin and never lets you get too comfortable. After tough stops in Memphis, Washington, and even with the Los Angeles Lakers, it's gotta be refreshing for Smart to be back in a situation where he can aid winning with his antics.

This was only the third game Smart had played against Boston, and the other two were otherwise forgettable, with Smart tallying three points in 41 minutes during games with Memphis and Los Angeles.

But the Celtics got a reminder of Smart and his penchant for winning plays on Monday night. To quote the great modern philosopher Ric Flair, "You don't have to like me, but you'll never forget me."

#2906. Las Vegas Raiders owner Mark Davis says he feels no pressure to rush backup QB Fernando Mendoza

Sign up today for the CNBC Sport Newsletter

Las Vegas Raiders owner Mark Davis says he feels no pressure to rush backup quarterback Fernando Mendoza onto the field. The No. 1 overall draft pick's popularity has fueled questions about when he will become the team's starting quarterback.

“From me, there’s absolutely no pressure whatsoever,” Davis told CNBC Sport’s Michael Ozanian in an interview Monday. "I'm really happy with the way it is with Fernando right now," he added.

Davis called Mendoza a hard worker and a "sponge." He said that the rookie has been working with Tom Brady, the Raiders' minority owner and a retired superstar quarterback. Davis also believes Mendoza can learn behind veteran quarterback Kirk Cousins.

“That’s why I think we are so fortunate to get Kirk Cousins as somebody that could bridge the gap and let this young man learn a lot about what football is in the pros, the speed of the game, the things that have to be done,” Davis said. “And again, he’s picking it up great.”

The Raiders have faced growing scrutiny over when to play Mendoza following a four-game losing streak. Las Vegas ended that streak on Sunday with a 38-33 win over the New York Jets, moving them to a 4-4 record. Despite the team's struggles, Cousins has the second-most passing touchdowns in the league this season.

Davis said he remembers a time in the 1960s when quarterbacks sometimes sat for several seasons before starting. He said he feels fortunate to be in a position where he doesn't have to rush his young quarterback.

“Today, there’s a lot of pressure put on teams when they draft a guy number one that, you know, you’ve got to play him right away,” Davis said. “I’ve seen quarterbacks get fast-tracked onto the field, and they may do well, but they don’t learn all the intricacies of footwork and everything else that it takes. So they’re limited in their development maybe throughout their career.”

Mendoza, who led the Indiana Hoosiers to a 2026 National Championship, has played in one regular-season game, completing six of eight passes. He's also taken a team-first stance, saying he just wants the Raiders to win.

Before the NFL draft in April, the 23-year-old Heisman Trophy winner told CNBC he was looking forward to learning from his childhood idol, Brady.

"To have that football intelligence and the way that he sees a game, not only from a physical but also from a mental and emotional standpoint. I'm excited to dive in, take everything I can to learn from him," Mendoza said.

#2904. $6.3 billion Binance hack is largest crypto theft on record

Binance said Friday that a security incident impacted about $6.3 billion in client assets, with around $3 billion frozen on-chain, marking the largest crypto hack on record.

The company later added it began recovering assets impacted by the breach after pledging to cover losses using its Secure Asset Fund for Users, which the company created in 2018 as a protection for clients in the event of a hack.

It wasn't clear how the attack took place. The world's largest crypto exchange initially said the hack may involve "a compromised DevOps engineer's laptop, which allowed an attacker to gain access to a service container and submit unauthorized withdrawal transactions." However, Co-CEO Richard Teng later said the attack involved a more complex process rather than the misuse of a single engineer's permissions.

The FBI said Friday it was aware of the incident and was in touch with "relevant public and private partners to understand the scope of the activity, evaluate ​potential impacts, ​and ⁠coordinate appropriate investigative steps."

It wasn't clear either who was responsible for the breach. But TRM Labs, a blockchain intelligence and AI investigations company, pointed to North Korean hackers as the potential culprits. "Several features of this attack match earlier North Korea-linked heists, including the Bybit hack and the KelpDAO hack in April 2026: small Tornado Cash withdrawals funding an operator wallet, staging that began well before the theft, and signatures obtained in advance then executed in one burst," the company said in a post.

Blockchain data platform Chainalysis also said this appears "be the work of North Korean cybercrime group TraderTraitor. This group has repeatedly targeted the cryptocurrency industry, exchanges, DeFi protocols, and investors, to raise funds for the regime. We base our intelligence assessment on on-chain activity observed in the hours following the hack."

Binance temporarily suspended withdrawals while it overhauls its withdrawal security systems and has revoked the affected address authorizations, Teng said. The exchange is also working with law enforcement and blockchain intelligence firms to trace and recover the stolen funds, while deposits remain unaffected.

Binance co-founder Yi He said deposits remained unaffected and continued to operate normally, while the exchange was working on enhancements to its withdrawal service and related address controls. It expects to begin resuming withdrawal services within the next 48 hours.

"We have found no evidence of any wallet private key leak. We will share a detailed technical post-mortem once the investigation is complete and the facts are fully verified," Binance said.

The announcement followed moves by several crypto firms, including Circle Internet Group, Tether and Ripple, to freeze funds tied to the hack.

Exploits are not uncommon in the crypto space. In 2025, Bybit suffered roughly $1.5 billion in losses due to a hack. More recently, Bitget lost more than $350 million worth of digital assets in a cyber theft incident. Over the past several years, bad actors have also drained billions of dollars in crypto from decentralized exchanges.

This week's breach "proves our exact point in proposing regulations for crypto markets earlier this month," Michael Selig, chairman of the Commodity Futures Trading Commission, said Friday in a CNBC "Squawk on the Street" interview. The U.S. derivatives regulator on Oct. 5 proposed a framework that would effectively put crypto exchanges under federal government oversight — after Congress failed to approve legislation to regulate the asset.

"The real issue here is that these are unregulated offshore exchanges, and they don't have the same sorts of safeguards that we have in our financial markets here in the United States. We want to offer a federal option to these exchanges to come and register with the CFTC, and they would be subject to the same sorts of cyber security safeguards that we have in our markets today," he said.

Bitcoin fell more than 4% to $77,187.60 on Friday as the hack weighed on crypto markets.

Circle CEO Jeremy Allaire said Thursday evening in a social media post that the company had frozen more than 800 million in USD Coin associated with the incident. USDT stablecoin issuer Tether CEO Paolo Ardoino also said in on X that his firm had frozen more than 1.5 billion of its stablecoin that may have been related to a potential breach.

Jack McDonald, Ripple's senior vice president of stablecoins, said it was actively freezing affected funds held in RLUSD, its U.S. dollar-backed stablecoin.

#2902. Ravens poach QB Kedon Slovis off Packers’ practice squad

The Baltimore Ravens are signing quarterback Kedon Slovis off the Green Bay Packers' practice squad, per The Ringer's Tom Pelissero. The second-year passer will add depth behind Joe Fagnano and Austin Reed following injuries to Lamar Jackson and Tyler Huntley.

Slovis, 25, has taken just five regular-season snaps since entering the NFL as an undrafted free agent in 2024 after five years(!) as a starter in college. He spent three years at USC and started as a true freshman before leading the Pac-12 in most volume passing statistics in 2020 and passing yards per game in 2020 and 2021. His numbers dropped sharply at Pitt and BYU over the next two years with a 58.0% completion rate, 216.5 yards per game, and a 3.6% touchdown rate after 68.4%, 280.6, and 6.1% marks, respectively, as a Trojan.

Slovis did not make the Colts' active roster as a rookie and spent the year on the Texans' practice squad. He was waived by Houston during final roster cuts in 2025 and signed with the Cardinals' practice squad, where he worked with now-Ravens quarterbacks coach Israel Woolfork. After Kyle Murray went down in October, Slovis was promoted to the active roster. He appeared in two games after Jacoby Brissett and Gardner Minshew also suffered injuries.

Arizona moved on from Slovis this past offseason. He took up the Packers' third-string quarterback job and posted prolific numbers in the preseason. His 434 passing yards ranked third in the league, as did his 83.6 grade from Pro Football Focus (min. 50 snaps). Unlike Fagnano in Baltimore, however, his performance did not earn him a 53-man roster spot. He stayed on Green Bay's practice squad, keeping him available to be poached by the Ravens amid their injury crisis.

Slovis only has a few days to learn Baltimore's offense – a tall task for a veteran, much less a third-year player. Since the emergency No. 3 quarterback must be on the 53-man roster, it would make sense to designate him such on Thursday night and elevate Austin Reed, who played for the Ravens in the preseason, to back up Fagnano – assuming he starts. The undrafted rookie is dealing with an ankle injury that limited him at Monday's walkthrough, though both player and club have expressed optimism about his availability.

The Ravens also worked out former 49ers quarterback Adrian Martinez on Tuesday morning, per The Athletic's Jeff Zrebiec, who adds that the team may not be done adding at the position. Martinez went undrafted out of Kansas State in 2023 and has spent time with the Lions, Jets, and 49ers in the last three years. He appeared in one gamesfor the 49ers in 2025 and played just one snap. He was also named the UFL MVP in 2024 after passing for 1,750 yards and 15 touchdowns with 530 rushing yards and three scores on 54 attempts across the Birmingham Stallions' 10 regular-season games.

#2899. Why are there no NBA games today? League promotes the midterm vote

In a push to get more people out to the ballot box, for the fourth time in five years, the entire NBA will go dark.

The league did not schedule any games Tuesday, Nov. 3, the day of the fiercely contested 2026 U.S. midterm elections. Because the first slate of polls close on the East Coast at 6 p.m. ET, with many more at 7 p.m. ET, holding games would severely interfere with the ability for people who choose to vote in person to do so.

It's one of the three days that the NBA typically avoids scheduling games, joining Thanksgiving and Christmas Eve.

The NBA has also published a website with several resources for fans interested in voting to learn more about their polling sites, ballots, and the league even has a link to check voter registration status.

Although the entire league is off Tuesday, each team played Monday, Nov. 2. And at each of those 15 games, players wore T-shirts that said "VOTE" across the chest.

"No games have been scheduled for Election Day (Tuesday, Nov. 3) as the league will encourage fans and the broader NBA community to make a plan to vote and to participate in the civic process," the NBA said in August when it issued its official release announcing the schedule.

Golden State Warriors coach Steve Kerr spoke on the importance of voting before their game against the Phoenix Suns on Monday, Nov. 2.

"I hope everybody votes," Kerr said. "I voted today, dropped my ballot off in a ballot box near my house, and, you know, I always encourage everybody to get out there and vote. And sometimes there's a tendency to stay away in the midterms and only vote in the presidential elections, but there's a lot of really important issues here in California.

"I hope everybody gets out there and cast their ballot.  That's what democracy's about."

The NBA first began the initiative of not scheduling games on Election Day in Nov. 2022. At the time, the league also encouraged its teams to promote voting initiatives. The NBA also declined to schedule games on Election Day 2023 and 2024.

And since Tuesday will be an off night, the league will return to action in full force Wednesday, Nov. 4, with a slate packed with 14 games.

#2896. NFL Power Rankings Week 9: Commanders’ win over Eagles worth less this year

Most Power Rankers moved Washington up after Jayden Daniels' first win in over a year, but the stink of a tank that's surrounding the Eagles actually lowered the Commanders rankings in the NFL.com rankings. Every other ranking stayed the same or improved.

The NFC East is still the worst division in the NFL with the New York Giants in the lead in Week 9. This week's results put the Washington Commanders within two games of the division lead with a rematch against the Giants set for Thursday Night Football next week. The win over the Eagles kept Washington alive in the division, with Philly getting kicked to the basement of the division.

The Power Rankers are higher on the Washington Commanders after getting their second win of the season, but there's only so much you can rise after a 17-point win over a team that's given up on the season. The Rams are coming to town on Sunday, and this is a "filter game". The Rams need a win to contend in the NFC West, and the Commanders need a win to maintain any hope for this season before next week's trade deadline.

You get the sense that the Washington Commanders could end up making a huge coaching move if they don't beat the Eagles on Sunday Night Football. Ever since that breakout season in 2024, things have fallen off the rails for Washington, as they have won just six regular season games since the start of the 2025 season, and QB Jayden Daniels has struggled to stay healthy.

With that said, Washington getting within one game of the Super Bowl two years ago could mean that more success is on the horizon if the necessary change is made. A non-playoff team trending toward making major changes, Washington should be able to beat Philly on SNF this week.

Best in-season trade acquisition ever: WR Calvin Muhammad in 1984

This really is based on one season, as Muhammad played only two years for Washington. The franchise needed help after Art Monk was injured and traded a fourth-round pick to Oakland for the speedy Muhammad. In his 10 games with Washington in 1984, Muhammad caught 42 passes for 729 yards and four touchdowns. He was a key reason why, even without Monk, Washington's offense remained among the best in the NFL, ranking third in points and ninth in yards. A year later, with a healthy receiving group, Muhammad caught only nine passes and was released before the 1986 season. — John Keim Last week: 29

Reality check: Jayden Daniels is getting beat up

The Commanders quarterback took a big hit from Nolan Smith on Sunday night that just added to what already has been a black-and-blue season. Daniels has only rushed for 33 yards (all on scrambles) in the last two weeks. #2892. Shopify falls 7% after Q3 revenue and merchandise volume miss estimates

Shares of e-commerce platform Shopify fell 7% on Tuesday after the company missed estimates on subscription gross profit and gross merchandise volume.

Shopify reported before the bell Tuesday and posted adjusted earnings of 49 cents per share, topping LSEG estimates of 46 cents per share.

Overall, quarterly revenue of $3.74 billion came in just below LSEG estimates of $3.75 billion. Shopify said it sees revenue growth in the "low thirties" range year over year for the fourth quarter, versus LSEG estimates of 32.1%.

Gross merchandise volume, which came in at $119.15 billion, missed analyst estimates of $120.03 billion for the quarter, according to StreetAccount. Subscription gross profit also missed estimates by nearly $20 million, coming in at $661 million compared to StreetAccount estimates of $680.3 million. Merchant gross profit beat estimates.

Shopify president Harley Finkelstein called the GMV results "consistent" and "durable" in comments to CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" on Tuesday, and noted it was the thirteenth consecutive quarter of GMV growth above 20%.

"We're really proud of the print, and we're going to keep building for the long term and ignore the noise day to day," Finkelstein said.

Subscription revenue of $836 million also missed analyst estimates of $848.1 million, according to FactSet. Merchant revenue beat estimates.

Meta released its consumer-facing artificial intelligence agent Muse during the third quarter, and Shopify was an early partner. The partnership allows Muse to browse and purchase items on Shopify's platform within the Muse app and allows customers to check out with Shopify's payment service, Shop Pay.

Finkelstein addressed concerns about potential pricing pressure as AI agents negotiate prices and cost compare on behalf of users.

"We're not seeing that prices are being lowered," he said.

Instead, Finkelstein said AI agents are driving new traffic to Shopify's business and are helping consumers find new brands and products."Brands like the Tin Can phone, a screenless phone for kids, are seeing incredible success because of agentic-based commerce, where a consumer is being very specific and using very merit-based results and using Shopify catalog. It is presenting these great products to consumers that there's no way they would have found otherwise."

Finkelstein acknowledged some economic pressure on consumers heading into the holiday season, but said businesses are optimistic and agentic shopping could have a breakthrough moment.

"I do think this is the first holiday season where AI-assisted shopping capabilities will become mainstream, especially with the rise of personal agents," he said.

#2890. Celtics’ shooting woes got the best of them — as did Marcus Smart

The Boston Celtics already had their hands full on Monday night. No Paul George. No reprieve on the second night of a back-to-back on the road. Then came Marcus Smart.

Former beloved friend of the program, Smart embraced his new Houston Rockets uniform. Like a gray wolf circling wounded prey, he watched his former team struggle through a miserable 38.6 percent shooting performance before sinking his teeth in at every opportunity. Whenever the Celtics tried to put out the fire, Smart poured kerosene on it, leading Houston to a 109-93 victory and bringing Boston's Eastern Conference-best five-game winning streak to an end.

"He woke up everybody's ass," Rockets teammate Alperen Şengün told Ryan Hollins postgame.

It didn't take long for Smart to make his presence felt.

HOUSTON, TX – NOVEMBER 2: Marcus Smart #36 of the Houston Rockets shoots a three point basket during the game against the Boston Celtics on November 2, 2026 at the Toyota Center in Houston, Texas. NOTE TO USER: User expressly acknowledges and agrees that, by downloading and or using this photograph, User is consenting to the terms and conditions of the Getty Images License Agreement. Mandatory Copyright Notice: Copyright 2026 NBAE (Photo by Logan Riely/NBAE via Getty Images) | NBAE via Getty Images

After noticing Jayson Tatum expressing his frustration with referee Matt Kallio with 3:11 left until halftime, Smart immediately fled Houston's bench and instigated, urging the official to issue Tatum a technical foul. He was all laughs, while Tatum — considerably less amused by the antics — remained visibly frustrated as he stared Smart down for a brief second and walked back to Boston's bench.

Smart was still all smiles, as he put his arm around Mike Conley and began plotting his scheme.

In the third quarter, Smart got Tatum again. He drew a charge on Tatum, handing him his fourth personal foul. Seconds later, Tatum was whistled for a second straight offensive foul, bringing his total to five and forcing Celtics coach Joe Mazzulla to remove him with 1:39 remaining in the frame. Tatum, visibly frustrated once again, made another unhappy trip to Boston's sideline.

Payton Pritchard also fell for Smart's bait, earning a third-quarter offensive foul himself. Smart did his signature Shawn Michaels-inspired kip-up to get back on his feet.

By then, it was official. Smart's plan was in full effect.

Much like the menace who wore No. 36 for nine seasons in Boston, Smart reminded the Celtics why it's great to play alongside him and a pain in the ass to compete against him. He went scoreless in the first half and missed his first seven shots before finally making Boston pay.

Rockets head coach Ime Udoka kept Smart on the floor to begin the fourth quarter, and the 13th-year veteran rewarded that decision with two timely shots.

Smart drained back-to-back 3-pointers, extending Houston's lead to 90-79 with over eight minutes remaining. He raised both middle fingers in celebration, later claiming the gesture was directed at a select few seated on Boston's bench.

"Just a lot of pent-up, I guess you can call it emotions," Smart told reporters. "Plus, certain guys on the (Boston) bench was talking. Didn't know who. I just heard them say something, so I let them hear it back."

Smart finished with eight points, four rebounds, four assists, and two steals in over 19 minutes.

"You always wanna do good against your former team," Smart added, "and show them that you still can go out there and do what you do, right? So it definitely felt good. Energy was there. They know what I can do, and I reminded them again tonight."

The energy Tatum once benefited from having alongside him had finally come back to haunt him. Smart's swagger was contagious, and everyone in Houston fed off it.

The Rockets shot 40 percent from three and 54.4 percent overall, having their way with Boston.

Meanwhile, the Celtics couldn't get anything to go.

Boston's top outside-shooting threats — Pritchard, Derrick White, Sam Hauser, and Baylor Scheierman — combined to go 4-for-21 from beyond the arc. Luka Garza, coming off the bench to play over 17 minutes, was the Celtics' leader in 3-point makes (four), although that contribution was largely overshadowed by the rest of the team's shooting woes.

They were a hair better from three (29 percent) than they were 24 hours earlier (27 percent) against the Orlando Magic on Sunday night.

Tatum went 1-for-7 from distance and 5-for-16 overall, finishing the night with 19 points and a season-high five turnovers in Houston.

It's difficult enough to play two games in two nights, especially on the road, but Tatum believes the Celtics didn't do themselves many favors either throughout the game.

"Everybody plays back-to-backs," Tatum told reporters, per NBC Sports Boston. "We're not the only team that's gotta travel and play a back-to-back. Are they tough? Yeah. But a lot of us been doing this for a long time. There were moments where we cut it to six or seven (points) and couldn't get a rebound, and they hit a three. It's moments like that where we gotta dig deeper a little bit."

It's been three years since the Celtics traded Smart, originally sending him to the Memphis Grizzlies. In the years since, the 32-year-old has struggled to make his former team pay, whether because of his on-court performance or availability. But Monday night in Houston, Smart finally got his get-back, and Boston felt it.

#2889. Ghosted after a job interview? There's a website for that—and thousands of frustrated job seekers have weighed in

Gary Meyer didn't intend to become a ghostbuster. But his website, Did They Ghost You, has become an outlet for thousands of frustrated job seekers who have been ghosted by employers.

Based on anonymous reports — 2,888 at last count — the site tracks and ranks the companies that are most often accused of abruptly ceasing contact with candidates, including some of the biggest names in the S&P 500.

Ghosting is a widespread issue. A recent report from hiring platform Greenhouse found that 45% of job seekers who made it to the interview stage of a hiring process said they were ghosted by an employer afterward, based on a survey of 1,200 U.S. candidates conducted in September 2026.

Meyer, 42, an artificial intelligence-focused marketing consultant who lives in Toronto, created Did They Ghost You in June after listening to his friends vent about interviewing with companies and then never hearing back.

He never intended for the website to be more than a "fun little side project," but when he shared a link to his site on LinkedIn, the reports started pouring in. He says that within the first few days, the site received more than two million visitors: "Clearly, it struck a bit of a nerve."

Meyer, who reads and approves each report himself, says he fielded five or six new submissions every minute at the height of the site's popularity over the summer. To protect people's identities, Meyer typically doesn't publish the contents of the report, and his site doesn't track personal information. Each report contributes to an overall tally of ghosting incidents.

"Was told that I was the preferred candidate by the Director of HR in a debrief immediately following the final interview and then just didn't hear back," one report featured on the site said.

"Ghosted, then reposted their ad a week later," said another.

Meyer's definition of being ghosted by an employer doesn't include applying for a job and never hearing back — unfortunately, "that's part and parcel" of the job market today, he says.

His website is for people who make it through several steps of the hiring process and then never hear from the company again.

"These are people who have gone through multiple rounds of interviews, met with people, flown to various states or cities for the interview and then right at the end, just nothing, no response, nothing," he says.

Part of the reason he created the site is to let those job seekers know they're not alone, he says.

"I think a huge part of what makes it hurt so much when you get ghosted is the assumption is that you've screwed up so badly that you're not worth responding to, that you're not even worth an email," he says.

It's easy for job seekers to blame themselves when they get ghosted, according to career coach Phoebe Gavin, but it's really a failure on the company's part to practice "basic etiquette" in the hiring process.

"The hiring process requires a great deal of unpaid labor from candidates, and it is a reasonable expectation that you will receive some sort of communication, that you will be treated with some sort of dignity," she says.

Although it doesn't excuse their actions, there are a few common reasons that a company might ghost a candidate, Gavin says.

Especially at small companies, managers are often tasked with supervising the hiring process on top of their regular duties. While juggling all those responsibilities, it's easy for a candidate to get "lost in the shuffle," she says.

Another possibility is that there could be some behind-the-scenes factors that the hiring manager can't share with an external candidate, like a budget cut, hiring freeze or leadership turnover, Gavin says.

Still, "candidates deserve to hear from hiring managers regardless," she says.

Meyer says he doesn't intend to turn Did They Ghost You into the next Glassdoor. The site's popularity "got a little bit overwhelming," he says, and he currently has a backlog of reports to read and approve.

He plans to keep running the website, but "the best case scenario is that this problem gets fixed, and there's no point in having the site anymore."

#2886. New national home listing database is taking on Compass in the battle for control of real estate information

A version of this article first appeared in the CNBC Property Play newsletter with Diana Olick. Property Play covers new and evolving opportunities for the real estate investor, from individuals to venture capitalists, private equity funds, family offices, institutional investors and large public companies. Sign up to receive future editions, straight to your inbox.

Some heavy hitters in residential real estate just joined the fight over who can see residential property listings. Their goal, they say, is to counter private listings, which are becoming increasingly popular in certain markets.

AGNT (which owns eXp Realty and NextHome), HomeServices of America, Keller Williams and Real REMAX Group, four of the nation's largest brokerages, announced that they are creating a largely open and shared database (API) of listings for brokers — and inviting all other brokerages to join it. Those who do must submit a new listing within one business day. There is, however, an option within the system for certain private listings.

Open Real Estate is designed, according to its founders, to counter the increasingly fragmented residential listing market, and specifically private, off-market listings, which are favored by Compass, a brokerage behemoth. Compass is embroiled in more than half a dozen lawsuits with state and local Multiple Listing Services (MLS) involving listing rules and antitrust claims.

"We want to ensure that for the American consumer, there's a complete data set that is capital T truth, that's reliable as to what is happening in the marketplace," said Leo Pareja, CEO of AGNT. "So whether it's MLSs fragmenting, pocket listings, walled gardens, whatever, the goal is to ensure that there's a complete data set for the American consumer, so buyers and sellers have the most amount of accurate information."

Those who want to join the platform can do so with no initial charge or fees. Pareja said the four founders are going to make the investment into the infrastructure and the technology. Brown Harris Stevens, Lamacchia Realty, Samson Properties and Windermere are listed as initial participants.

"It's clearly a clap back to Compass, firstly," said Jonathan Miller, a longtime housing market analyst and director of markets at StreetMatrix, a housing data and analytics company. "And it's absolutely better because Compass is literally hiding listings from competitors by design."

Just after the announcement, Compass CEO Robert Reffkin posted a congratulatory message on X, saying that Compass is "excited that there will be a national real estate listing service that will return the power back to the creator of the listing — the homeowner and their trusted agent. This is good news for all agents and their clients, and we look forward to helping build the next chapter of listing services."

When asked, Reffkin declined to comment further.

Pareja's response to that: "We saw that, and we're grateful for his appreciation, and he's very much invited. We'd love to have him participate."

While Reffkin's response may seem odd, given that Compass supports private listings, there is one part of the Open Real Estate platform that does allow for exclusives. It's called "participant view." A property that goes into that view is put into a part of the database, "where only agents that are associated with participating brokerages can see it, and it cannot be publicly marketed by anyone, including the listing agent and the listing brokerage," according to Open Real Estate's website.

Reffkin's response came at the same time another MLS, New York's OneKey MLS, joined the California Regional Multiple Listing Service's federal lawsuit against Compass. The organizations are asking the court to firmly establish that rules protecting the shared, public marketing of listings are lawful and pro-competitive, according to a release. It followed OneKey's rejection of Compass's demands to dismantle local cooperation policies.

"This case raises questions that extend far beyond any one company or market," said Richard Haggerty, CEO of OneKey MLS in the release. "It goes to the core of how homes for sale are shared, how everyday buyers access that information, and how we protect a fair, equal housing market for everyone. We believe these issues demand clarity from the court, and joining CRMLS gives OneKey a direct, active voice in securing it."

Separately, last month six regional MLSs representing more than 120,000 real estate professionals nationwide filed a joint federal lawsuit against Compass seeking to uphold rules that promote access to listings through publicly shared databases.

Pareja said the new database is not meant to replace MLSs and that it should not be seen as a national MLS.

"We are going to work with MLSs. We're going to work alongside them. We're not usurping. We're not replacing," he said.

CNBC's Property Play with Diana Olick covers new and evolving opportunities for the real estate investor, delivered weekly to your inbox.

#2885. Mark Davis: "Absolutely no pressure whatsoever" from me to start Fernando Mendoza

The Raiders have gotten good play out of quarterback Kirk Cousins through the first eight games of the season, with the team sitting at 4-4 after Sunday's victory over the Jets.

With Cousins keeping the team in contention, it's unclear when No. 1 overall pick Fernando Mendoza might make his first career start.

That's just fine with team owner Mark Davis, who talked about the young QB in a Monday interview with CNBC.

"From me, there's absolutely no pressure whatsoever," Davis said, via Adam Hill of the Las Vegas Review-Journal.

"Today, there's a lot of pressure put on teams when they draft a guy No. 1 that you have to play him right away," Davis added. "That's why I think we were so fortunate to get Kirk Cousins as someone who could bridge the gap and let this young man learn a lot about what football is in the pros, the speed of the game, and the things that have to be done. … And he's picking it up great, but I think we're fortunate he doesn't have to go out and play."

Davis doesn't want Mendoza to feel like he has to feel any kind of pressure to perform, because that could end up hindering his growth.

"I've seen quarterbacks get fast-tracked onto the field and they may do well, but they don't learn all the intricacies of footwork and everything else it takes," Davis said. "So they're limited in their development, maybe throughout their career.

"I'm really happy with the way it is with Fernando right now, and we'll see what happens."

Much of Davis' happiness with Mendoza has to do with how the QB has handled everything off the field.

"I tell people jokingly that if he can play football, he's going to be amazing," Davis said. "Because he's got every other intangible you could want in a person. He's a hard worker. He's a sponge.

"[And] he's working with Mr. [Tom] Brady on a lot of things."

Mendoza has made one appearance so far this season, completing 6-of-8 passes for 48 yards in mop-up duty during the Week 5 loss to the Patriots.

#2879. Russia confirms new Ukraine peace talks as Melania Trump prepares to host ceasefire meeting

Russia on Tuesday confirmed that peace talks with Ukraine and the U.S. will go ahead next week in Abu Dhabi, United Arab Emirates, while also signaling that a meeting between Presidents Vladimir Putin, Donald Trump and Xi Jinping to end the conflict was on the table.

Putin aide Vladimir ​Medinsky will serve ⁠as Moscow's chief negotiator ​at the talks in Abu Dhabi on Nov. 10-11, Kremlin spokesman ​Dmitry Peskov told reporters on a conference call, according to Reuters.

Separately, first lady Melania Trump will bring Russian and Ukrainian representatives to the White House to discuss the local ceasefire in Kherson, the White House said Tuesday.

Monday saw the end of a six-day ceasefire in the Russian-occupied city of Oleshky and surrounding areas in the Kherson region to allow for evacuation of residents, in a pause of hostilities that the White House said was brokered by the first lady.

Ukraine on Monday accused Russia of using the ceasefire to reinforce its military positions in the city.

The first lady's summit on Thursday will be "an extraordinary moment in American history," the White House said, but did not name the representatives who will attend. Melania Trump will deliver remarks on the Oleshky ceasefire at 11 a.m. ET on Thursday, it added.

Peskov also said Tuesday the Kremlin hopes to finalize plans for a trilateral meeting between the leaders of Russia, the U.S. and China in the coming days. The comments follow earlier reports that these countries were planning to negotiate an end to the war in Ukraine, without Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy at the table.

Zelenskyy has said that any negotiations over Ukraine's future must include Kyiv.

"It can be acceptable only when we are at the table of negotiations," he said on Oct. 20, adding that he had discussed the proposal of a three-way meeting between the U.S., China and Russia to negotiate an end to the nearly five-year full-scale war. "Nothing about Ukraine without Ukraine," he said.

Tensions between the U.S. and Ukraine have mounted over recent weeks amid continued strikes between Moscow and Kyiv, including on civilian and energy targets.

A series of moves by the Trump administration in October raised questions about Washington's reliability as Kyiv's most important military partner.

Tuesday's announcement follows U.S. special envoy Steve Witkoff saying late Monday that Washington will mediate Ukraine-Russia peace talks in Abu Dhabi on Nov. 10-11, focusing on security arrangements, de-escalation and humanitarian issues.

Witkoff also said that U.S. negotiators will meet European and NATO officials in Brussels this week to discuss postwar security guarantees and Ukraine's reconstruction.

#2877. Job openings posted big September gain in positive sign for labor market ahead of payrolls report

Job openings surged in September, as did the number of workers who quit their positions, the Bureau of Labor Statistics reported Tuesday, while hiring also rose and layoffs dropped.

In its monthly look at labor market slack and worker mobility, the BLS reported job openings at 7.52 million, up 333,000 from August and well above the Dow Jones consensus outlook for 7.15 million.

As a share of the total workforce, the rate of job openings increased to 4.5%, up 0.2 percentage point from the prior month, according to the Job Openings and Labor Turnover Survey. There are now some 414,000 more job openings than workers classified as unemployed, or about 1.05 openings to every available worker.

Openings rose in manufacturing (54,000) as well as state and local government (108,000) and professional and business services (106,000).

At the same time, the total of employees leaving their jobs spiked to 3.36 million, an increase of 321,000 from August and the highest level since March 2025. Quits are considered a barometer for worker confidence in finding new jobs after leaving their prior positions.

"There are more job openings than unemployed people in the United States right now. That doesn't solve all problems, but it's a big change from a year ago," said Heather Long, chief economist at Navy Federal Credit Union. "It's an encouraging sign that despite all the headwinds right now with high diesel and gas prices and ongoing inflation problems, companies are slowly increasing their hiring."

Hires rose on the month, up 131,000 to 5.4 million, while layoffs and discharges fell by 127,000 to 1.64 million, the lowest since March 2025.

The report comes ahead of the October nonfarm payrolls report, which the BLS will release Friday. The Wall Street consensus is for an increase of 59,000 after September eked out a gain of just 29,000, while the unemployment rate is figured to hold at 4.2%.

In other economic news Tuesday, September factory orders for manufactured goods jumped 1.8%, well above the estimate for 1.3% after increasing just 0.3% in August.

#2873. Ex-Amex CEO shares when business leaders need to speak up about politics: 'You should care about what happens in our society'

When a CEO talks politics, people tend to get nervous — especially when the issues don't seem to directly affect the company.

Yet "some fundamental issues" call for a public stance, even if the subject doesn't obviously or immediately affect a CEO's company, former American Express CEO Ken Chenault told CNBC's "Squawk Box" on Monday. Any threat to democratic institutions or processes in the U.S. can ultimately sow political and economic instability that could have a negative impact on any business, regardless of the sector or industry, he added.

"If you want to build an enduring company, you should care about what happens in our society," said Chenault, who stepped down as American Express CEO in 2018 after 37 years at the financial services giant, including nearly 17 as chief executive. He currently serves as chairman and managing director of venture capital firm General Catalyst.

CEOs shouldn't speak out on every political matter, Chenault said. But those seeking lasting success need "long-term perspective" about any risks and opportunities that could ultimately affect their companies, he suggested.

Business leaders often refrain from speaking out on political issues for fear of alienating customers or their own employees. Some companies have found success weaving politics into their public image — outdoor apparel company Patagonia is among the best known — but taking a publicly political stance is most often met with mixed opinions, according to a 2025 study from a group of Ivy League researchers. On the whole, the average person generally prefers CEOs to stay silent on divisive political issues, the study found.

Yet with American voters heading to the polls on Tuesday, Chenault specifically pointed to the issue of voting rights as one that corporate leaders like himself should publicly acknowledge. As the Republican Party braces to potentially lose national and state-level seats in the midterm elections, President Donald Trump has declined to say he would unconditionally accept the electoral results. Instead, the president has promised to accept the results "if they're honest elections," The Washington Post reported on Oct. 27.

"If, in fact, the upcoming election is threatened and the verdict of the election is not accepted, that is not an issue that I would be quiet [on]," Chenault said, adding: "Nothing is more fundamental than preserving and strengthening our democracy [and] the fundamental right to vote."

Political instability can hurt a country's economy, research suggests. One potential path for democratic erosion to harm business: A breakdown of the rule of law can scare off investors looking to deploy capital or build new companies, Princeton University political scientist Layna Mosley found in a July 2023 report published by nonpartisan organizations The Brookings Institution and States United Democracy Center.

As American Express CEO, Chenault weighed in publicly on some political issues that did have an immediate impact on his company. One of his more prominent stances was an acknowledgment of the financial industry's need to adapt and accept additional regulatory oversight following the 2008 financial crisis, for example.

More recently, Chenault has spoken somewhat frequently about the importance of robust voting rights. In March 2021, he and former Merck CEO Ken Frazier called on corporate leaders to push back on political efforts to restrict voting access in the U.S. In particular, Chenault — one of the first few Black CEOs of a Fortune 500 company, upon his appointment at American Express in 2001 — spoke out against policies that voting rights groups have criticized as disproportionately disenfranchising voters of color.

"Corporations have to stand up. There is no middle ground," Chenault told "Squawk Box" on March 31, 2021. "This is about all Americans having the right to vote, but we need to recognize the special history of the denial of the right to vote for Black Americans, and we will not be silent."

#2871. Takeaways from missing Marcus Smart in green

With Paul George sitting out the second night of a back-to-back and Boston playing their fifth game in eight nights, the Celtics snapped their five-game winning streak and lost to the Rockets 109-93. It was a demoralizing loss to a very good team, made only worse because of who was on the opposing team.

It's bittersweet to say, but it's all about Marcus Smart.

The former Celtic was everywhere on the floor. His stat line doesn't tell the story. He was 2-for-10 from the field; Alperen Segun paced all scorers with 26 on 12-of-15 shooting. He was just a +5 off the bench with the Rockets starters doing much of the damage in the game. With the ninth stingiest defense backing up the league's most efficient offense, the 6-1 Rockets have few weaknesses to take advantage of now that they have Fred Van Vleet and Smart at the point.

However, Boston was in this game. After Smart checked into the game at the 4:35 mark of the third quarter, the Celtics whittled down the lead to four. And then Smart happened.

During one stretch to start the fourth quarter, he was called for two consecutive dead ball defensive fouls that he followed up with back-to-back threes — the epitome of The Marcus Smart Experience that we know all so well. By the time he checked out midway through the final frame, Houston was up 100-82.

Since his departure in 2023, he's bounced around from four different teams, including a stint with Lakers last season. However, hooking up with Ime Udoka in Houston seems to be the right fit. For more insight to the former Celtics reunion with the Rockets, check out The Athletic's Will Guillory's profile on Smart and Udoka. Here's a snippet:

"There are some guys who have that defensive mentality, but it's rare to have a guy who comes into the game and impacts it the way he does on that side of the ball," Udoka said. "You saw it with Dillon, you saw it with Pat (Beverley). There are certain guys you have to prepare for because of what they do defensively. He's one of them."

"(Udoka's) mentality matched mine," Smart added. "He allowed me to be me without any persecution."

For the Celtics, they have several candidates that have filled in for Smart's Tommy Point contributions. Walsh's on-ball defense made him a starter earlier in the year. Scheierman chips in with the occasional hustle play or heady pass. Gonzalez often has the flair for the dramatic. But nobody has yet to solely take the mantle away from Marcus since his departure.

Last season, the Celtics were 7-11 when they shot under 30% from behind the arc. Before last night, they had endured a miserable game against the Nets in an overtime thriller and Sunday's brickfest over the Magic, but hitting just 12-of-42 in Houston just wasn't enough against an elite Rockets team.

Tatum made just one of his 7 triples. Pritchard and Hauser missed all five of their attempts each. The only bright spot was Garza's 4-for-7 clip.

Maybe it was a lack of legs in the second night of a back-to-back or missing Paul George's catch-and-shoot prowess on the wing, but stinkers like this are going to happen every sixth game or so. As long as they're figuring out other ways to win, it shouldn't be too big of an issue.

Once a Celtic, always a Celtic. Love and trust, Smarty.

"To be honest, I gotta figure it out soon. I don't know." — Mitchell Robinson on his impact on the offensive boards

After missing the previous game against the Magic, Robinson was replaced by Neemias Queta in the starting lineup. Soon after coming off the bench, he jammed his thumb and went to the locker room for a brief stint in the second quarter. He did make both of his looks from the field, but his one offensive board on the night was just his fifth in three games. That was supposed to be his calling card.

Head coach Joe Mazzulla said after the loss that Robinson's decreased impact is partly because they have him playmaking by setting screens further away from the rim, but after leading the league last season with 7.7 offensive rebounds per 36 minutes, it's been a disappointing start for the Celtics' key free agent signing of the summer.

#2870. Apple stock sinks as global memory crisis hurts profits, giving Ternus his first big test

John Ternus' first earnings report as Apple CEO was going fine until it was time to issue a forecast for the company's gross margin, one of the key metrics followed by investors.

Apple reported better-than-expected earnings and revenue on Monday, including in the company's core iPhone unit, and even projected sales in the current period that topped estimates. But just before Ternus opened the earnings call up to analysts' questions, CFO Kevan Parekh came forward with the first real piece of bad news.

Parekh said that for the December quarter, which goes through the holiday season, Apple's gross margin will be between 45% and 46%, trailing the 46.3% average analyst estimate, according to LSEG.

"This includes primarily the impact of higher memory costs, as well as some costs related to the ramp of Siri AI," Parekh said.

Apple shares slid 3% in morning trading on Tuesday.

It's part of an ongoing trend for Apple, which is absorbing some of the soaring costs from the global memory shortage. A margin drop in the fiscal first quarter would be a third straight decline for Apple, following a gross margin of 50.1% in the June period and 47.9% in the September quarter, boosted by 1 percentage point of tariff refunds.

At the midpoint of the forecast range, it would be Apple's lowest gross margin since the fiscal fourth quarter of 2023.

"What we're seeing with memory pricing right now is unprecedented," Ternus said on the call, adding that the company is paying more than it did earlier this year. "Looking beyond the December quarter, we see the pricing for memory purchases continuing to increase."

Ternus, who took the reins from Tim Cook on Sept. 1, is grappling with a problem that his predecessor spent his final months addressing. Cook told The Wall Street Journal in June that the memory situation was a "hundred-year flood." Apple followed by raising prices across the board on Macs, iPads and accessories.

In September, Apple increased the prices of its iPhones by $100, which Ternus said the company did "reluctantly."

Computer and smartphone makers everywhere are suffering. On Friday, Intel had its worst day on the market since the 1970s due to a weak forecast tied to the softening PC market. It's all because of insatiable artificial intelligence demand.

AI server giants like Nvidia and hyperscalers are buying up all the RAM they can find, and they're willing to pay a premium for it, leaving little supply for companies that make smartphones and tablets.

Despite its size and global clout, Apple said it expects supply constraints on iPhones and Macs in the holiday quarter, primarily driven by memory.

On the call, Ternus had some top lieutenants field component-related questions. Sabih Khan, chief operating officer, became the first Apple COO to appear on an earnings call since Cook in 2010, when Steve Jobs was CEO.

"As we have said before, we're seeing less flexibility in the supply chain than normal," Khan told analysts on the call.

In response, Apple has drastically increased its purchase obligations to secure supply, Khan said. Those obligations rose by $33 billion from the June quarter to the September period, Khan said, though he declined to answer questions about whether Apple had signed "long-term agreements" with memory makers.

"We're always working hard to improve supply," Khan said.

Even with the recent margin slide, Apple's gross margins are still well above where they sat earlier in the Cook era, when the number would predictably come in between 38% and 39%. As Apple's profitable services business grew, its gross margin expanded, hitting 40% in 2021 and growing from there.

Analysts see a potential silver lining for Apple in the current market turmoil. Because Apple is more capable of eating a portion of the increased costs, some experts predict the company will take market share from makers of Windows PC and Android phones that don't have such a cushion.

Citing data from a third-party researcher, Ternus said gains are already happening.

"According to IDC, we gained share globally for the quarter and for the full fiscal year," Ternus said. "And we have never sold more Macs in a fiscal year than we did this time, which is a truly remarkable testament to these incredible products."

#2869. The midterm election could reshape the muni bond market. Here’s what investors need to know

The outcome of Tuesday's election is expected to have broad implications across the market, including municipal bonds.

The spotlight has been on the battle for congressional control and whether Democrats will wrest the House of Representatives, the Senate or both from Republicans. But for the muni market, what happens at the state level could have bigger repercussions — at least in the near term.

There is an array of initiatives on ballots around the nation that could change tax laws and add to bond supply. Taxes matter to muni investors since the income received is free of federal tax, as well as state tax if the holder lives in the state where the bond is issued.

Depending on the location, ballot measures are up for approval that would either provide tax breaks, or raise taxes on the wealthy.

"It's a complete bifurcation between red and blue states," said Dan Close, head of municipals at Nuveen. "Blue states are trying to put in place graduated income tax structures, trying to capture more revenue from high-income earners. Red states are promoting affordability by trying to cut property taxes."

Red states are those that predominantly vote Republican, while blue states swing toward Democrats.

For instance, California has a proposition known as the "billionaire tax," which would impose a one-time levy equal to 5% of the wealth of certain taxpayers with at least $1 billion in assets. The state also has a measure on the ballot that would make a temporary tax hike permanent on individuals earning over $371,000, adjusted annually for inflation. The tax is currently set to expire in 2031.

"The impact to the muni bond market will be to make the exemption more valuable," Close said of the possible passage of tax increases. Investors will likely seek shelter in the muni market, which would send bond prices higher, he noted.

It is a different story in Florida, where voters are set to decide whether to increase the Sunshine State's homestead exemption for non-school district property taxes. Qualified homeowners currently have a $25,000 property tax exemption, as well as another for $25,000 that doesn't apply to school property taxes. The new proposal raises the latter exemption to $150,000 in 2027 and $250,000 by 2028. New residents have a five-year waiting period before receiving the benefit.

If passed, some local governments will have to find other revenue sources or increase the overall tax rate to replace what they've lost, said Tom Kozlik, head of public policy and municipal strategy at HilltopSecurities.

"It's not necessarily going to result in downgrades for sure, but there would have to be some balancing with regard to overall revenues," he said.

Not all municipalities will be hurt equally, which means credit research is important, said Close.

"Like everything in municipal finance, it is really on a case-by-case basis that you could have some credit deterioration from [a] rollback of property taxes," he said.

Other voters on Tuesday will be asked to approve more state or local government debt. That could weigh on a market that has already seen heightened supply.

Some $473.8 billion has already been issued year to date, through September, according to the Securities Industry and Financial Markets Association. That's a 7.8% increase over the same period a year ago. In 2025, total muni issuance reached $587.3 billion, 14.3% higher than 2024, Sifma said.

Cooper Howard, director of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research, believes supply is the biggest issue coming out of the election.

"That amount of issuance has been one of the reasons why you have to see that strong amount of demand continue to keep pace with it for total returns to keep up," Howard said.

Fortunately, new supply doesn't necessarily come quickly into the market. "There's a window over a couple years that they can issue," he noted.

The ultimate makeup of the next Congress could also have consequences for muni investors as lawmakers sort out their priorities. Most notable is what lawmakers will do to tackle the country's affordability problem, Kozlik at Hilltop said.

"There could be some tax policy initiatives and even affordability-related initiatives that manifest themselves in the first quarter of next year," he said. "That could impact housing. It could impact higher education. It could impact public power financing."

Investors considering moves before the election may want to look at those states with tax increases on the ballot, said Close at Nuveen.

For instance, Nuveen recently began offering a separately managed account dedicated to Washington after getting an enormous number of inquiries from financial advisors. The state recently enacted a 9.9% tax on individuals with taxable annual income above $1 million, set to go into effect in 2028. But the issue is now being put to voters, who can decide whether to repeal the new law.

"At the margin, if you do think that some of these measures are going to pass, knowing that bonds usually move up with an increase in taxes, there might be some opportunities to buy ahead of the election for blue states," Close said.

Howard believes munis are attractive right now, particularly for those who are in a tax bracket of 24% or above. Fundamentals are good and the bonds largely have good credit quality, he said. "About 70% of the broad muni index is either triple-A or double-A rated."

Kozlik is also bullish, calling yields "really attractive." He suggests investors concentrate on AA-rated or higher bonds.

"The muni space is part of the portfolio that most investors want," he said. "It helps them sleep well at night."
