#2867. Midterm elections could jolt stocks if Democrats sweep Congress

The stock market is not yet pricing in a Democratic sweep of Congress — despite some polls and most prediction markets pointing that way — so that scenario could weigh on share prices Wednesday, according to investors and analysts.

Follow CNBC's live coverage of the 2026 midterm elections

Republicans managing to keep control could cause a short-term relief rally, they said.

Short-term moves aside, the typical post-midterm pattern in the months ahead is bullish. But some wonder if the market could buck the positive historical trend this time because the S&P 500 is already up so much this year going into the election.

As far as certain stocks to watch, a big Democratic win on Tuesday night — especially in the Texas governor race — could add fuel to an artificial intelligence stock sell-off on the notion that more data center restrictions would gain momentum nationally. Defense stocks could come under pressure on fears of limits on spending.

Polls are pointing to a Democratic victory in the House and a toss-up in the Senate. Prediction markets have been more favorable to Democrats, calling for a blue sweep.

While investors are signaling "begrudging acceptance" to a base-case scenario that there will indeed be a Democratic sweep, that's not how they're positioning, said Tobin Marcus, head of U.S. policy and politics at Wolfe Research. The firm's recent survey data among more than 100 institutional investors suggests they are not strongly positioned for any particular result.

"Investors seem to have a significant dose of skepticism about whether the final outcome is going to be quite as favorable to Democrats as the polling suggests," Marcus told CNBC, noting that the firm shares the view that the "polling is likely overstating Democratic strength."

JPMorgan sees a blue wave knocking the S&P 500 by about a half percent on Wednesday.

On the other hand, if Republicans end up retaining both chambers of Congress, that could spur a rally, said Sameer Samana, head of global equities and real assets at the Wells Fargo Investment Institute.

"From a certainty standpoint, the market knows what to do with Republicans holding both chambers," he said.

Citigroup strategists said Monday that Republicans losing the House but managing to keep the Senate could be "a tailwind for equities into year-end." JPMorgan traders predict up to a 1% pop on Wednesday if Republicans hold on to that chamber.

Looking past any short-term move, stocks tend to rally after the midterms have concluded. In fact, the S&P 500 was up one year out after all midterm elections going back to the early 1980s.

But the S&P 500 has already recorded double-digit gains so far in 2026, so some on Wall Street worry that investors are being overly complacent heading into this year's elections.

During midterm election years, the S&P 500 has typically averaged a 14% drawdown at some point in the second half of the year found Jonathan Krinsky, chief market technician at BTIG.

"The reason stocks often perform well in November during midterms is because [the S&P 500] typically is weak INTO the elections. We have the opposite setup this year," Krinsky wrote last week. "Therefore, while consensus is that we see a post midterm rally, we think the opposite is actually more likely."

It's not helping that the broader market has recently come under significant pressure due to turbulence in the artificial intelligence trade, which led the Nasdaq Composite to post its worst day since June on Friday. AI shares led the market lower again on Monday.

A strong showing from Democrats in Tuesday's midterm elections could pressure the trade even more.

At the state level, increased Democratic control is probably a headwind for stocks associated with data centers and technological capital equipment, analysts said.

"It [would solidify] the early kind of cautionary signs in different states around the advancement of AI infrastructure," Rob Rains at Washington Analysis told CNBC.

Goldman Sachs strategists said this week that a Democratic Congress "might limit data center tax incentives, but a national moratorium looks unlikely in any election outcome."

One example would be Texas — a key hub of the data center buildout. A surprise loss there by Republican Gov. Greg Abbott has the "potential to spook" the market in the short term, Wolfe's Marcus said.

A blue wave might pressure defense shares, while boosting healthcare stocks, analysts and investors said.

"A substantial Democratic sweep could limit real spending growth unless a broader agreement increases both defense and health-care funding," said Alex Sagal, global equity analyst at Wells Fargo Investment Institute. However, he noted that the earnings outlook for the defense industry looks "stable to modestly positive."

Wolfe Research's Marcus said that healthcare is going to be "the biggest focus," as Democrats will push for restoring expired Affordable Care Act enhanced premium subsidies or unwinding some of the Medicaid reforms in the One Big Beautiful Bill Act.

"Healthcare stocks have been doing really well lately," Jed Ellerbroek, portfolio manager at Argent Capital Management, said to CNBC. "I think part of that is the anti-AI trade, but I also think part of it is just optimism that Democrats are taking over."

Ellerbroek is keeping his eye on hospital-related stocks such as HCA Healthcare and Tenet Healthcare as well as health insurers like UnitedHealth and Centene.

Financials was the worst S&P 500 sector in early 2026 on the heels of President Donald Trump calling on Congress to pass a law that would cap credit card interest rates at 10%. While the sector has been able to notch gains since, with its year-to-date rise now at around 3%, things could get more unstable for the group under Democratic control.

"Democrats will come in wanting to show tangible progress on the affordability issue, and that means targeting sectors that are front and center in consumer budgets," said Evercore ISI's Matthew Aks. "We know Trump has populist instincts, populist leanings, and could he pick a couple of issues and decide to team up with the Democrats on those issues and target those industries in a populist way? Absolutely."

Given that Democratic control is often associated with increased regulation, while Republican control is thought to promote deregulation, Democrats could be interested in passing credit card rate caps if they come out victorious.

"I think if Democrats have control of one or both chambers, they would try to pass it and see if they could get Trump to either sign it or force him to veto it, and that would let Democrats kind of score a political point," Aks said.

Trump could restart the war against Iran after holding off on any major strikes leading up to the midterms, a move that could upend the overall market and cause energy stocks to outperform. Crude oil prices jumped more than 10% in October, though they gave a lot of that gain back on Monday.

Gasoline prices stood at $4.17 a gallon on average nationwide Sunday, a nearly 40% increase compared with the same period last year, according to AAA.

"If there are escalations, that could obviously put upward pressure on prices just ahead of Thanksgiving," said Patrick De Haan, head of petroleum analysis at Gas Buddy, "which is also when Americans have really staunch traditions in terms of driving to friends and family."

If Trump decides to take forceful action, it could snuff out hope for a typical post-midterm year-end rally.

Evercore ISI said the S&P 500 could retreat by double digits to end the year at 6,800 as oil spikes if the president "moves to kinetic escalation in Iran ahead of seating the new Congress in January" and Democrats take Congress.

— With reporting by Sarah Min, Tobias Burns and Spencer Kimball.

#2866. The 'poison pill' ballot measure that could kill the California billionaire tax

The Inside Wealth newsletter by Robert Frank is your weekly guide to high-net-worth investors and the industries that serve them.

The California billionaire tax could face a stiff challenge from a competing ballot measure that would ban state taxes on retirement accounts, according to tax experts.

California voters will decide the fate today of Proposition 40, known as the Billionaire Tax Act, which would impose a one-time tax of 5% on the total net worth of Californians worth over $1 billion. The so-called "billionaire tax" is one of the most widely watched ballot measures this election season, since it would be the first state tax on net worth and taps popular anger over the rising power and wealth of tech billionaires.

Follow CNBC's live coverage of the 2026 midterm elections

The tax requires a simple majority to pass. Since it would only tax the state's roughly 250 billionaires, many political experts initially predicted easy passage. Yet a recent poll by the Public Policy Institute of California shows it falling short, with 46% supporting and 52% against.

Supporters of the tax blame a $300 million ad blitz funded by tech billionaires and business groups opposing the measure. Many of the ads claim the ballot measure would allow the Legislature, with a two-thirds vote, to expand the tax to everyday Californians.

Yet tax experts cite another potential roadblock to the tax: Proposition 42. One of two ballot measures that would act as poison pills against the billionaire tax if they get more votes, Prop 42 calls for a permanent ban on any statewide tax on property of all Californians.

The ballot measure "Prohibits any new state tax (1) imposed on the ownership of personal property (all things people own other than real estate), or (2) that applies retroactively based on taxpayer's past activities."

The official California Voter Information Guide explains the "pro" argument by saying that Prop 42 protects Californians from double taxation by "prohibiting new and retroactive taxes on personal property, including retirement and savings."

The "con" argument, according to the voter guide, is that Prop 42 is "just another billionaire-funded trick." Propositions 41 and 42 were created and submitted by Building a Better California, a political action committee funded by tech executives and several California billionaires opposed to the billionaire tax.

Political and tax experts say voters' fears about protecting retirement accounts will be a powerful motivation to vote "yes" on Prop 42, thus nullifying the billionaire tax. As much as they may want to tax billionaires, their retirement accounts may be more important.

"Both Proposition 41, which prohibits new taxes from disregarding voter-approved spending limits, and Proposition 42, which prohibits new taxes on retirement accounts and personal property, could win majority support," said Jared Walczak of Walczak Policy Consulting.

#2865. Ranking the Rockies, 2026: No. 29, Gabriel Hughes

Welcome to the 2026 edition of Ranking the Rockies, where we take a look back at every player to log playing time for the Rockies in 2026. The purpose of this list is to provide a snapshot of the player in context. The "Ranking" is an organizing principle that's drawn from Baseball Reference's WAR (rWAR). It's not something the staff debated. We'll begin with the player with the lowest rWAR and end up with the player with the highest.

When the Colorado Rockies selected Gabriel Hughes 10th overall out of Gonzaga in the 2022 MLB Draft, they knew they were taking on a power arm with at least a middle-of-the-rotation ceiling. However, after Tommy John surgery cut his 2023 season short and kept him out of the 2024 season, he had to start building his stock in 2025.

Hughes had a solid season last year, despite dealing with several injuries throughout the year. Heading into 2026, it seemed like it was inevitable that he would finally reach the big leagues. He finally earned his call-up to the big leagues in early July 2026, making his Major League debut on July 3 against the San Francisco Giants.

His inaugural taste of the majors was predictably turbulent. Across 14 games (13 starts) for Colorado, Hughes posted a 6.33 ERA and an 0-8 record. On paper, those surface-level traditional figures look grim. However, a closer look under the hood reveals a far more nuanced picture of a young arm navigating his initial trial by fire.

After a solid, albeit limited, spring training audition, Hughes was sent to Triple-A Albuquerque to start the 2026 campaign. He got off to a rough start, posting an 8.64 ERA in 25 innings across six starts. The strikeouts weren't an issue as he had 32 against just six walks, but he was giving up a lot of contact and getting hit hard. At the end of April, he was placed on the injured list and missed most of May.

After a pair of strong rehab starts with High-A Spokane, Hughes was sent back to the Isotopes where he looked like a man possessed. Over his next three starts in June, Hughes allowed just four hits across 15.2 innings with 16 strikeouts and five walks, limiting opponents to a .080/.179/.100 slash. His best outing of the year at any level came on June 25 when he fired six shutout innings at home against the Salt Lake Bees, giving up just two hits while striking out six.

It was hard to ignore that Hughes had tapped into something while recovering from injury, and he had suddenly tamed the Pacific Coast League. As the Rockies looked for pitching reinforcements, it was hard to ignore one of their top prospects banging on the door.

So, that's when he got an 11:30 pm call while in his hotel in Round Rock, Texas, from Isotopes manager Pedro Lopez telling him he was headed to the big leagues.

The initial plan for Hughes when he was called up was to wet his feet working out of the bullpen. If the opportunity were to arise he could work his way into the rotation, but the Rockies wanted to see what he could do in a multi-inning role first before taking that next step.

In that July 3 game against the Giants, Hughes entered with an 11-run lead in the seventh inning after Ryan Feltner had cruised through six. He breezed through three innings on 53 pitches to secure his first big-league save while giving up two hits with one strikeout.

Hughes's ability to hammer the strike zone was immediately evident, but his poise on the mound would shine in his next appearance, which came in his starting-pitching debut against the defending World Series champions, the Los Angeles Dodgers, on July 8.

The Dodgers jumped out to an early 3-0 lead in the first inning as Hughes struggled to find the zone and threw 39 pitches. However, he seemed to flip a switch in the second inning, retiring 15 Dodgers in a row before allowing a two-out double to Max Muncy in the sixth inning. Hughes threw just 55 pitches over those five innings after the first and had a first-pitch strike rate around 80% while becoming the first rookie since Peter Lambert in 2019 to record a quality start in their first big-league start.

He continued to look solid through his next three starts, allowing six runs on 16 hits over 15.1 innings, going at least five innings each time. However, Hughes hit a rough patch of inconsistency that would hinder him through the rest of the season.

Over his final nine starts, Hughes posted an 8.17 ERA over 39.2 innings of work. While he continued to grit his way through at least five innings in four of those starts, Hughes saw elevated pitch counts that knocked him out of games earlier than expected, including one start in Detroit where he threw 70 pitches and lasted just two innings.

Despite knowing that Hughes was going to grind as much as he could and throw strikes, teams became more selective in the box and were fouling off pitches. Hughes had a decent first-pitch strike rate of 59.7%, but nibbling became an issue.

Despite preventing hard-hit balls and limiting opponents to a .271 AVG, Hughes still struggled to limit baserunners as opponents had a .772 OPS and a .319 BAbip. He had a league-average 8.5% walk rate alongside a 19.8% strikeout rate, but the efficiency of at-bats didn't afford him chances to work deeper into games.

Through 13 starts, Hughes went 0-8 with a 6.33 ERA. The only other Rockies pitcher in franchise history to begin their career with zero wins through their first 13 starts was Jon Gray in 2015-16. Despite games where he pitched well enough to earn a win, the offense was often too lacking to give him any support.

Despite what the stat box says, there was a lot to like about Hughes during his first big league stint.

Immediately, his maturity and composure on the mound exhibited a player wise beyond his years. A studious individual, Hughes is meticulous about finding the right way to win a ballgame. He wasn't easily rattled most of the time, living up to his alma mater's Bulldog nickname.

What was most impressive was the potential of Hughes arsenal. Utilizing a four-seam fastball that is more akin to a cutter (fashioned after his childhood hero Mariano Rivera), Hughes complemented it with a deep mix that includes a changeup, a slider, a sweeper, a curveball, and a sinker.

He was able to command his pitches and utilize them as needed, sometimes having to rely on one more than the others because it was working. In that start against the Dodgers, catcher Brett Sullivan noticed that Los Angeles was not catching up to the fastball, so they just rode that pitch for as long as they could. Hughes was a poster child for the advantage of a deep arsenal, but you still need to have a bread-and-butter pitch, which was that cutter. Opponents hit .285/.245/.394 against it with an 18.4% whiff rate and a 15.7% putaway rate.

Out of all his starts, that one in Los Angeles and his start in New York against the Yankees are the beacons of what Hughes brings to the table at his best. Against the Yankees, he threw five innings, allowing two runs (one earned) and striking out six. He also induced 13 swings-and-misses and four groundball outs.

Hughes made a name for himself in the minors as a strike-throwing machine, and we saw that quite a bit this season. His ability to get strikeouts is also encouraging, as he had six starts with six or more strikeouts. The next step is to work on efficiency and refine both his control and command with his pitches.

Most encouraging was Hughes ability to tame Coors Field in his rookie campaign. Through seven appearances, he posted a 4.09 ERA compared to an 8.71 ERA in seven road starts. He limited opponents to a .234/.294/.363 slash at home, which is almost miraculous considering the state of the Rockies' rotation over the last few years.

Ever a thinker, Hughes was sure to take 2026 as a great opportunity to learn. From everything that he experienced this season, he was able to take something into the offseason to help him improve for next year.

"I think one of the things I've really learned is that I have to have different starting points for a lot of my pitches when I'm here versus on the road," Hughes said to Purple Row's Samantha Bradfield. "It's not something I've thought about too much in the past when I was in Albuquerque. But here, where the margin for error is so low, I think it's extremely important. So that's something I've continued to work on. But that's probably the biggest thing I've noticed because the margin for error is so low, so you have to be sharp here and on the road."

Having just turned 25 years old in August, Hughes has the opportunity to be a staple of the Rockies rotation for the next few years. The Rockies need Hughes to take that next step forward while they also need to prove they can continue to develop and improve players.

The future is bright for Gabriel Hughes; he just needs to continue to refine his methods.

#2864. What the 2026 midterm elections could mean for Trump's final two years

Tuesday's midterm elections will at their heart determine control of Congress for the remainder of Donald Trump's presidency. U.S. voters will decide whether Trump will maintain his Republican iron grip on Washington or recede into lame-duck status after a Democratic triumph.

Follow CNBC's live coverage of the 2026 midterm elections

If Republicans win, Trump could spend another two years facing little congressional resistance and pushing through major policy packages. If Democrats win one or both chambers, the president's remaining legislative priorities would be severely kneecapped, and even the basic functions of government could become gridlocked.

Oddsmakers and public polls placed Democrats in a favorable position ahead of Election Day, especially for flipping control of the Republican-held House. Still, the balance of power will not be decided until ballots are counted — a process that could take days or, in some cases, weeks to complete. The Senate is expected to be particularly close, and if the balance of power comes down to Alaska, the nation may have to wait while the state counts.

"The election results could trigger an immediate market reaction, particularly as the Senate outcome becomes clearer," the Washington Policy Strategy Group of Stifel wrote Sunday in an analyst report, where it predicted Democrats will win the House while the Senate remains too close to call. "However, the market's knee-jerk trade could reverse as investors recognize that gridlock remains the most likely policy outcome in either case."

Republicans also control the Senate and with a larger majority than in the House.

If Democrats capture one chamber of Congress, Washington policymaking would become significantly more gridlocked. Single-party budget reconciliation bills, the procedural vehicle for most major spending and policy changes in the current status quo of tight congressional margins, would become impossible. Bipartisanship would be required to advance legislation, making must-pass bills such as government funding packages, defense spending and the looming debt ceiling hike more important — and more contentious.

In a divided Congress, the party that doesn't control the White House typically uses moments including government funding and debt-ceiling hikes to extract concessions from the majority party, a practice that can lead to brinksmanship.

"If Republicans defy the conventional wisdom and maintain their Senate majority, then we would expect a relief rally in the sectors that might react negatively to a Democratic victory," Stifel wrote. "Democrats would still govern the House and gridlock would prevail. In this scenario, Senate Republicans would act as a check on both the Democrats and many of Trump's populist impulses."

Democrats are also widely expected to use any majority in Congress to launch investigations into the White House and businesses that have worked with the Trump administration. The majority party gains subpoena power in congressional committees.

Polling indicates a Democratic sweep of the House and Senate is possible. In that case, Democrats may more aggressively pursue party-line reconciliation measures that wouldn't become law but would give the party a megaphone ahead of the 2028 presidential election. Trump would still have the ability to veto legislation that Congress passed, severely limiting Democratic bills' prospects of becoming law.

Must-pass legislation would be slightly easier to get through Congress if Democrats controlled both chambers. But deals would still need to be brokered with the White House, and the Senate's 60-vote filibuster threshold could still prove a hurdle that requires bipartisanship. Whichever party gains Senate control is highly unlikely to have at least 60 seats.

Democratic lawmakers in both the House and Senate would jump at the chance to lead investigations of the White House and business if given the opportunity.

In either a divided Congress or unified Democratic Congress, analysts expect Trump's use of executive orders will increase due to congressional gridlock.

There is a possibility Republicans maintain control of both chambers of Congress. In that case, Trump's remaining legislative agenda could continue to be enacted, and the status quo would be maintained.

Democrats would still hold some leverage, largely due to the Senate's 60-vote filibuster threshold, but would be limited in investigating the White House and business.

#2863. Wizards defense cracks down on Magic in stifling third quarter

The Wizards entered last night's game at the Orlando Magic ranked 10th in defensive rating (points allowed per 100 possessions). They then put the Magic into a figurative chokehold — limiting Orlando to 28.2% three-point shooting, 94 points in the game, and just nine points in a stifling third quarter.

It's the second consecutive game that the Wizards opponent scored less than 100 points. Washington managed that feat just three times all of last season. And the Wizards did it without Alex Sarr (out with a sore knee) and Justin Champagnie (broken foot).

Kyshawn George played a strong all-around game in the Washington Wizards win over the Orlando Magic. | Getty Images

The Wizards' record is now 4-3. The last time they had a winning record this deep in the season was 2021-22.

Comparisons with last season are for illustrative purpose only, by the way. Washington was trying to lose last year. This is a different squad with a different purpose, and it shows. And it's fun to watch.

Against the Magic, Kyshawn George was excellent on both end all night. He took good shots except when the only shots were tough ones — and he shot well. He defended, rebounded, set up teammates while avoiding turnovers and fouls. He finished with 22 points, 4 rebounds, 5 assists, a steal, a block and a turnover. That's a helluva good game.

Anthony Davis dominated inside, scoring 27 points and grabbing 9 rebounds and 3 steals. He did get a bit sloppy at times, which showed in his five turnovers. Still, another strong game from the team's best player.

In what might well be a sign, I thought AJ Dybantsa had a relatively rough outing. A few times in the second half, he had some trouble finishing through contact and managing against Orlando's physicality. He shot just 7-17 from the floor. And yet, he finished with 21 points, 5 rebounds, 2 assists, and 3 steals. His PPA was 148, and the team was +10 in his minutes. Reminder: this is a 19-year-old rookie.

Bilal Coulibaly was disruptive on defense. Bub Carrington gave the team 19 productive minutes. Trae Young was kinda meh for him, and he still generated 9 assists and grifted free throws.

So why am I still at "want to believe" status? One big reason is the likelihood that they're benefitting from opponent shooting luck. One of the lessons of statistical analysis is that defenses don't have much effect on opponent three-point shooting. The fact that Washington's opponents are shooting below 30% on threes is great. But over a small sample, it's probably just luck. When opponent shooting regresses to the mean, so will Washington's defense.

I say "probably" because they're doing a lot right on that end. Their effort is NBA level, which is something the franchise hasn't had in a few years. They swarm, they pressure, they scrap, they chase and deflect and challenge shots. I see that and appreciate it while the analyst part of my brain keeps saying, "Tap the brakes. It's not that hard to look good when opponents are shooting 28% from three-point range."

The Magic targeted Young and had success, even on a bad offensive night. Young's defensive effort is as good as it's ever been across his entire career. My sense from watching him this season is that his (relative) defensive strength is as a help defender or jumping passing lanes. His point-of-attack defense is weak and will be a target as the Wizards play more meaningful games.

I'm not sold on Dybantsa's three-point shooting stroke. There's something about his arms and the location of the ball as he's shooting that's not sitting right with me. It's something like the ball spending too much time at eye level and a flat trajectory.

The Wizards are huge and have a couple of elite athletes — Dybantsa and Coulibaly.

Despite being tall, mobile, and athletic, Deandre Ayton has very little presence as a rim protector. In the second quarter, I jotted a note that Orlando guard Jase Richardson had found his ideal victims — Young at the point of attack and Ayton in the paint.

Once again, Washington's first offensive play was for Dybantsa. They set screens for him, and got him the ball on the move, which helped him drive to the room. He missed through contact that probably should have been called a foul.

Once again, Young's ability to threaten defenses with his passing set himself up for an open layup.

Head coach Brian Keefe used essentially a nine-man rotation. Sarr and Champagnie were out with injuries, but Khris Middleton got a DNP-CD, and Will Riley played just four minutes.

Below are the four factors that decide wins and losses in basketball — shooting (efg), rebounding (offensive rebounds), ball handling (turnovers), fouling (free throws made).

eFG% (effective field goal percentage, which accounts for the three-point shot)

TOV% (turnover percentage — turnovers divided by possessions)

FTM/FGA (free throws made divided by field goal attempts)

PPA is my overall production metric, which credits players for things they do that help a team win (scoring, rebounding, playmaking, defending) and dings them for things that hurt (missed shots, turnovers, bad defense, fouls).

PPA is a per possession metric designed for larger data sets. In small sample sizes, the numbers can get weird. In PPA, 100 is average, higher is better and replacement level is 45. For a single game, replacement level isn't much use, and I reiterate the caution about small samples sometimes producing weird results.

POSS is the number of possessions each player was on the floor in this game.

ORTG = offensive rating, which is points produced per individual possessions x 100. League average so far this season is listed in the Four Factors table above. Points produced is not the same as points scored. It includes the value of assists and offensive rebounds, as well as sharing credit when receiving an assist.

USG = offensive usage rate. Average is 20%. Median last season was 17.7%.

ORTG and USG are versions of stats created by former Wizards assistant coach Dean Oliver and modified by me. ORTG is an efficiency measure that accounts for the value of shooting, offensive rebounds, assists and turnovers. USG includes shooting from the floor and free throw line, offensive rebounds, assists and turnovers.

+PTS = "Plus Points" is a measure of the points gained or lost by each player based on their efficiency in this game compared to league average efficiency on the same number of possessions. A player with an offensive rating (points produced per possession x 100) of 100 who uses 20 possessions would produce 20 points. If the league average efficiency is 115, the league — on average — would produced 23.0 points in the same 20 possessions. So, the player in this hypothetical would have a +PTS score of -3.0.

Players are sorted by total production in the game.

#2862. Crude oil prices fall for second day as market hopes for diplomatic resolution to Iran war

Crude oil prices fell Tuesday for a second consecutive session, as the market hopes the U.S. and Iran will reach a diplomatic settlement to the conflict that has disrupted global energy supplies for eight months.

U.S. West Texas Intermediate futures were 63 cents lower to $95.83 a barrel after dipping below $95 earlier. Brent crude futures, the international benchmark, were down $1.59 at $103.18 a barrel. The move lower came after rumors circulated the U.S. and Iran were making progress toward a deal.

Oil sold off more than 7% on Monday after Washington and Tehran signaled they were both open to discussions to reach a settlement. President Donald Trump told reporters Tehran wants "to make a deal and that could happen."

Qatar is continuing to mediate talks between the U.S. and Iran, the Gulf state's foreign ministry said Tuesday. A delegation from Qatar held talks in Tehran in an effort to reach an agreement that prevents further escalation in the region and ends the U.S.-Iran conflict, the foreign ministry said.

However, Iran's Foreign Ministry spokesman Emsaeil Baqaei said Tuesday that Tehran will not accept diplomacy based on coercion, according to the state-owned media outlet Islamic Republic News Agency.

The market had been pricing in the risk of escalation after the U.S. midterm elections, as reports circulated that Washington was drafting plans for strikes on Iran. Crude prices gained more than 10% in October in response.

The situation in the Middle East remains volatile and there's still substantial risk that fighting could break out. Washington attacked Iran's Qeshm Island on Monday, U.S. officials told The Wall Street Journal, adding that the U.S. has been carrying out "limited strikes" against the country in recent weeks.

The military campaign against Iran has been kept deliberately low-profile to avoid signaling plans for a major escalation in fighting, the report said, quoting an unnamed official.

And commercial ships continue to come under attack in the Persian Gulf, Strait of Hormuz and Gulf of Oman. At least four ship attacks have been reported since the start of the month, according to the United Kingdom Maritime Trade Operations Centre.

Despite these attacks, the Trump administration has also been touting a possible diplomatic solution to the conflict, with Vice President JD Vance telling CNN, "The president and I both feel really good about the prospect of potentially coming to a negotiated settlement."

On Sunday, U.S. Secretary of State Marco Rubio said Trump would like to resolve the Iranian war "diplomatically," and prefers not to use military force.

High oil prices have contributed substantially to inflation and high gasoline prices in the U.S., which have become a cause of concern for Trump ahead of the midterm elections.

Last week, two Federal Reserve presidents said interest rates will need to move higher to rein in inflation. Higher rates generally crimp growth and raise costs for consumers, but help to bring down inflation.

— CNBC's Priyanka Salve contributed to this report.

#2860. Bill Ackman warns of 'casino' stock market as speculative trading grows

Bill Ackman told CNBC on Tuesday he worries that the stock market is broken, saying the rise of speculative trading is leading to wild price swings thanks to the amount of leverage in the system.

"Every number of days, I feel like markets are broken," Ackman said on CNBC's "Squawk Box." "Stocks trade at prices that make no sense to me. Yes, it's good if you're a long-term investor and you can buy at a stupid price. I don't think it's good for the country generally."

"We're back to the casino-type markets when there was 95% leverage," he added. "I don't think that's inherently good for the economy, for society, and it's leading to these massive moves in stocks."

The founder of Pershing Square Capital Management said those same dislocations in the market have given the hedge fund the opportunity to snag big bargains.

Pershing bought Microsoft, for example, when it was pummeled during the "SaaSpocalypse" earlier this year, and Amazon when it fell following President Donald Trump's tariffs, Ackman said. It also bought Alphabet when the stock moved "massively" following ChatGPT's launch, he said.

But the billionaire investor worries that the rise of one-day leveraged vehicles, perpetual futures and sports betting is dangerous for investors who are treating the stock market as a get-rich-quick scheme — an attitude that could have disastrous consequences.

"It's good for us long term — 'us' being Pershing Square," he said. "I don't think it's good for the country."

Among the stocks Ackman is especially bullish on are Visa and Mastercard, which he called among the world's "greatest" businesses, as well as streaming platform Netflix, in which he disclosed a position in August.

#2858. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on these 3 stocks for the long haul

The ongoing earnings season, Middle East tensions and buzz ahead of the U.S. midterm elections have been keeping investors busy. Further, worries about elevated artificial intelligence spending are weighing on investor sentiment.

Investors looking beyond the short-term noise and focusing on long-term returns can track top Wall Street analysts to pick the right stocks, backed by strong research.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

This week's first stock pick is Datadog, an AI-powered observability and security platform. The company will announce its results for the third quarter on Nov. 4. Datadog is seeing strong demand for its offerings, thanks to cloud migration and rapid AI adoption. TipRanks' KPIs tool for DDOG shows the growth in its customer base over the past few years amid increasing market penetration and growing adoption of its products.

Heading into Q3 FY26 earnings, Bank of America analyst Koji Ikeda reiterated a buy rating on Datadog stock and raised his price target to $335 from $305. The analyst noted that industry checks and data indicate that infrastructure software demand is strengthening, with AI increasingly driving usage and growth.

Ikeda called Datadog and Atlassian his top picks, saying that companies that can show that AI is a tailwind to growth could see their valuation rerate, even at premium multiples. He added that the total addressable market for observability is growing faster than anticipated, driven by accelerated growth of enterprise AI and the rapid ramp of consumer AI agents.

The five-star analyst believes that focus will be on revenue growth, which he estimates to be 28.7% for Q3 FY26 versus the Street's consensus of 29.1%. Ikeda thinks that the market is underestimating the growth prospects of DDOG's security products. Meanwhile, he cautioned investors about concentration risk related to large customers.

"We think results will be supportive of our long-term thesis that Datadog is a strong share gainer in the rapidly expanding observability opportunity," said Ikeda.

Ikeda ranks No. 673 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 55% of the time, delivering an average return of 10.9%. See Datadog Statistics on TipRanks.

The next pick, semiconductor giant Nvidia, is also benefiting from AI-driven demand for its products. After attending Nvidia's GTC Berlin conference, Morgan Stanley analyst Joseph Moore reaffirmed a buy rating on NVDA stock with a price target of $300, calling it a top pick in the semiconductor space.

The five-star analyst said that the conference supported his thesis that Nvidia continues to effectively navigate bottlenecks in the supply chain, land/power/shell, and funding. In fact, Moore is "convinced that all 3 aspects are opportunities for NVDA."

While Nvidia's GTC Berlin offered no major updates, Moore noted the conference reflected that the company continues to add to its significant advantages heading into 2027. The analyst said that Vera Rubin is gaining momentum, though details on next year's Rubin Ultra were lighter than expected.

With demand outpacing supply, Moore said that focus is on closing the gap. In this regard, he highlighted the supply chain deals on substrate, packaging and testing. While the analyst continues to view TSMC 3 nm wafer supply as the biggest challenge, he expects it to ease over time.

Moore ranks No. 142 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 59% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.4%. See Nvidia Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Aerospace company SpaceX recently announced solid third-quarter results, with its AI revenue surging to $7.56 billion from $813 million in the prior-year quarter.

Impressed by the results, Needham analyst Ryan Koontz reiterated a buy rating on SPCX stock with a price target of $250. Koontz highlighted that SpaceX's Q3 results significantly exceeded expectations, proving that the company's vertically integrated business model is driving massive scale.

Furthermore, Koontz emphasized the 195.2% sequential growth in SpaceX's AI revenue, fueled by wholesale spot compute deals. He noted management's increasing focus on its AI strategy, given that it is optimistic about adding 10 gigawatts of incremental capacity during 2027.

"With increasing confidence on Starship re-use, we meaningfully increase our estimates," said Koontz.

Koontz ranks No. 357 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 42% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.7%. See SpaceX Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2857. Suns’ fourth-quarter mistakes overshadow promising first half

You watch the Suns, and you feel like Bob Wiley following the guidance of Dr. Leo Marvin. Baby steps. Baby steps. If this team is going to get better, they have to start taking baby steps. And on Monday night against the Golden State Warriors, it felt like some of those steps were finally being taken.

After all, the Suns got off to their best start of the season, building a 17-point lead in the first half. It was easily the most impressive opening stretch we've seen from this team thus far. But as we've learned through the early portion of the season, Phoenix still has plenty of work to do. Nothing comes easy for this group. Everything has to be earned.

If they want to earn another victory this season, they're going to have to clean up multiple aspects of their game. One thing we've been waiting to see is whether this team can actually close out a basketball game. On Monday night, that question remained unanswered. They simply couldn't close.

Down one point with two minutes remaining, Devin Booker missed a wide-open 12-footer. On the next possession, Booker turned the ball over. The possession after that? A turnover by Jalen Green. And then, to complete the sequence, Booker was called for an offensive foul.

Was it a flop? Probably. When you have no room for error, it ultimately doesn't matter. What does matter is execution, and that continues to evade this team, particularly when it matters most. They've yet to put together a complete game, and every time it feels like they're ready to take a full stride forward, they stumble.

Those baby steps were certainly present on Monday night. At times, the defense looked locked in. At times, the offensive sets were functioning the way they're supposed to. There were stretches when you could see what this team is trying to become. But in the NBA, when you have a roster that feels like it's fighting against itself because of how poorly it's constructed, when you can't consistently rebound the basketball or prevent opposing teams from generating extra possessions seemingly at will, baby steps might be all you're capable of taking.

Perhaps that's the most frustrating part of watching this team right now. You can see the progress, recognize the improvements, and appreciate the effort, but none of it matters much when the same mistakes continue costing you games. The Suns are learning how to compete, yet they haven't figured out how to win. And while Dr. Marvin might applaud the baby steps, the NBA standings certainly won't. At some point, Phoenix has to learn how to walk.

#2856. Sullivan: What the 2026 midterms could mean for energy investors

Don't expect huge near-term moves in energy stocks off the results of the midterms. But there are some investing implications over the longer-term. Let me break it down.

Yes, it's entirely possible that one or both houses of Congress will shift to Democratic control. That's not my opinion. It's what many of the research reports, polls, and prediction markets are suggesting as of this writing.

For argument's sake, let's say that this time the polls are correct. The election ends with a "blue trickle," and Democrats win out with a slight edge in the House, Senate, or both. It won't be enough to meaningfully change, well, anything. If the election ends with just a slight lean to the Democratic side, it won't be enough for a veto-proof majority. Candidates can specify policies — like halting the growth of data centers — all they want, but there's a chasm between being elected and passing new laws.

Research I've read and conversations we've had on CNBC suggest that the next two years in Washington, D.C., will be flush with finger-pointing, investigations into the president and his family, and posturing ahead of the far-more-important 2028 presidential election.

S&P Global doesn't exactly agree with me, saying that Democratic control of either chamber would bring "tougher oversight of agency decisions" and could impact things such as tax incentives and "other policies underpinning long-term energy investments."

A Democratic Congress could be good for offshore wind, an energy source the president seems to hate with the white-hot passion of the sun. Speaking of the sun, a shift blue could also be positive for the continued growth of solar, perhaps via new tax incentives. Though both are growing rapidly.

If the Republicans maintain control over one or both houses, we keep doing more of the same, albeit with more urgency. S&P Global adds that "a continued Republican majority would give the administration more room to pursue expanded domestic production, faster permitting and environmental deregulation, though narrow margins could limit major legislation."

Any change in control of Congress is also unlikely to make a big difference around the Iran conflict. Once any new Congress begins in January, things will have either ramped up, stayed the same, or cooled down. A new Congress could try to use its authority under the War Powers Act. The team at Piper Sandler agrees, writing that "elections matter, but will likely not change the direction of things" as Trump focuses more on his own legacy. That's a polite way of saying that the President is going to do largely what he wants, Congress be darned.

On the state level, the midterms may matter a little bit more, particularly as they relate to energy-hungry data centers. In Texas, a change in the statehouse could bring a change in sentiment around data centers. Candidate Gina Hinojosa has a policy to "end data center madness" and supports a moratorium on new builds. Amy Acton also supports a "conditional moratorium" on new data center builds in Ohio. Vivek Ramaswamy is also calling for conditions on new data center construction.

The issue is a big deal in many states. Yet even if those candidates, or candidates with similar views on big new energy projects, win, they'd still have to enact meaningful change. Which could happen, of course. So let's say it does happen. New governors enter office and use their power to throttle or even stop new projects. Even if some of the big new projects are stalled or stopped, power demand, at a minimum, would stay at current levels. Which means demand for power production inputs such as natural gas, nuclear, and yes, even coal, would remain at current levels.

As far as power-hungry utility stocks go, Bank of America agrees with my election take above - mostly. Its team writes that it thinks the "2026 cycle matters for utility equities in far fewer states than the headline count suggests," but that committee appointments in each state will matter. The BofA team says that, regarding the election and utility stocks, five states matter most: Georgia, Ohio, Oregon, Pennsylvania, and Texas.

Texas is the "most consequential," as state environmental regulators have halted data center permits. That state's Commission on Environmental Quality (TCEQ) has actually stopped electronic permitting to make sure any permit is unrelated to data centers. To my point, that's already known information. It matters, but markets are already aware of it and have priced it in. Regardless, if you are investing in anything related to data centers, it wouldn't hurt to pay attention to Texas' election most of all.

There's a lot here. So take a breath, step back, and consider where we are right now.

The Strategic Petroleum Reserve is at a 40-year low.

We have two wars in some of the most important energy regions in the world.

The backlog for data centers - and thus the power needed to run them - is years long. Even if a number of them end up canceled, others will be built.

These days, it's hard to be certain of anything. But I am certain that overall energy demand isn't going down in America anytime soon. The only way to go is up. It's just a question of how much farther up.

That's my opinion. It could be wrong. And since I love hearing from you, I put out a poll on both X and LinkedIn. The results suggest you disagree with me.

Note → I am fully aware that the results of any poll on X or LinkedIn will be skewed by my type and makeup of followers on each platform - who lean heavily into both business, money and energy.

So if the elections won't matter in the near term for energy, what does?

Glad you asked.  Here's my ranking of what will matter for energy in the next year:

(Disagree?  Hit me up on X or LinkedIn and let me know why)

An end to either the Iran conflict or Russia's war on Ukraine would be massively positive for the world and send oil, natural gas, and refined products lower relatively quickly. Let's all hope for peace.

Lower interest rates would help many of the beaten-up stocks that rely on debt and consumer lending to prosper. Think residential solar panels.

The macro economy is going to do what it will, regardless of the U.S. elections, and American tax policy is largely unchangeable for the next couple of years.

Permitting reform is, of course, one factor that could be impacted by the election outcome, though it's unlikely to happen in the near term.

No, my friends, whatever the outcome of America's elections, it's safe to say the real political attention will be on Trump, more Trump, Trump's family, even more Trump, and then looking ahead to 2028's presidential vote.

If we could harness political infighting into some sort of power source, it may be better than nuclear!

I'm leaving for Europe this week to continue our five-year coverage of Europe's slow-rolling energy crisis. And while the word "crisis" is way overused in the media, I don't think it is here. Natural gas import prices are soaring. Storage levels are below recent averages. Interest rates are rising, and the public is already rage-filled about higher energy prices. It's a toxic combo. And I'll be in Europe later this week to talk about it.

First, from a macro markets perspective, I remain wary of tech and the stock market right now. As I posted on October 14, the markets "feel fragile."

Since writing that, the Nasdaq is down over 5%. A.I. isn't in a bubble, but not all companies are going to prosper. Some may go away. Watch the debt markets, particularly some A.I.-related high-yield (aka "junk") bonds. The State Street High Yield (JNK) ETF is prominently on my screens these days. Get to know the term "leveraged ETF," because those are in focus these days. The selling in many of these products can lead to selling in other products and stocks. If we get a slowdown in selling in those, some of the big tech stocks that have been selling off might stabilize.

Turning more specifically to why you are here: energy.

Earnings are (almost) behind us. ExxonMobil (XOM) and Chevron (CVX) posted big numbers last week. Marathon Petroleum (MPC) just reported, and those numbers were even bigger. The company earned $26.59 per share on $11.7 billion in EBITDA. That compares to EPS of $3.01 on earnings of $3.2 billion EBITDA one year ago.

ConocoPhillips (COP) caps things off later this week.

What now is that the world waits, watches, and focuses on Iran, the Strait of Hormuz, and Russia. What happens with those will determine the path of prices for oil and much more. Peace, and prices go down. New attacks, prices go higher. Higher prices mean many big-cap oil and gas stocks keep going higher. If Russia continues its brutal war against Ukraine, diesel fuel and jet fuel prices are going to remain volatile.

My take → Keep an eye on any headlines related to Russia selling diesel fuel to the United States. There is one ship on the way (see the RBI below), and let's see if more follow.

The continued risk around Iran and Russia pushed Goldman Sachs to raise its oil price forecast for the rest of this year. The firm is taking Brent crude to $105 and WTI crude to $94, and raising its average targets for next year to $88 for Brent (from $80) and $78 for WTI (from $73). Let's see if others follow.

While earnings have U.S. analysts swamped, Goldman's European team isn't too busy to update its "European Conviction List" of stocks. Some of the names it likes most are energy-related, including German power producer RWE (RWE-DE) and the better-known Siemens Energy (ENR-DE).

Concerns about AI, power, and rising interest rates have recently hit both stocks. RWE is down 4% this quarter, with Siemens Energy falling over 10%. Goldman remains bullish. The firm has a €212 price target on Siemens Energy and a €75 target on power producer RWE. That implies about a 30% return on RWE and a huge 70% upside on Siemens Energy.

Europe may have a world of energy problems, but that doesn't mean some of its stocks can't make you money. For more, tune in to my live CNBC coverage from Italy on Thursday, November 5.

We recently spoke with Toby Rice, CEO of natural gas giant EQT (EQT). His company just made a massive deal to buy Range Resources (RRC). We spoke with Toby about the deal, energy markets, and the importance of U.S. gas to Europe.

Another important interview on energy and AI this week was with Brian Nowak of Morgan Stanley. Brian focuses on tech firms such as Amazon, but his comments about some of the AI power players - the names we mention above - are worth a listen.

This week's Inside Line is with Christoph Halser, Gas & LNG analyst at Rystad Energy.

Could the one ship carrying Russian diesel fuel bound for America… actually skip America? It might.

The vessel we've had a close eye on for a week is moving slowly in the Atlantic Ocean. So slowly that some are now wondering if the ship will actually make it to New York. Could the destination change mid-voyage? It happens with ships all the time. A new buyer steps in to buy the cargo, and the ship changes course.

It does make you wonder… what American company would buy a load of Russian diesel right now? Putting money into Putin's pocket is not the PR that any company I know would want, but stranger things have happened.

The ship is supposed to dock in New York on November 7. Following the course of this ship right now is definitely random, but interesting.

#2855. Week 9 Fantasy Football Defense Rankings

We're almost officially halfway through the 2026 fantasy football season. There's still a long way to go, so don't count yourself out in you're struggling in your league. The Steelers and Titans are on bye in Week 9 — one useful defense, another not so useful one. With Pittsburgh out of the Week 9 picture, you may need a replacement option.

Below, Yahoo analysts Justin Boone, Matt Harmon, Scott Pianowski, Joel Smyth and Hayden Winks provide their defense rankings for Week 9.

QB | RB | WR | TE | FLEX | D/ST | Kickers
