#2854. College Basketball Rankings: Men’s Top-25 with insight from experts who know their teams best

There is a very obvious reason why Florida is a lot of people's choice to earn a No. 1 seed for a third consecutive season and a national title for the second time in three tries.

Put simply, college basketball has rarely seen a frontcourt as strong as the one the Gators are going to put on the floor throughout this winter. Thomas Haugh, Alex Condon, and Rueben Chinyelu are all potential All-Americans who give Florida an absurd combination of scoring, size, rebounding, facilitating and interior defense. Haugh returns as a preseason First Team All-American after averaging 17.1 points per game and flirting with the NBA. Condon was a preseason First Team All-American last season, while Chinyelu is likely the best defensive big man in the country.

What Todd Golden has that he didn't have last season is experience and continuity in the backboard. Point guard Boogie Fland really came on in the second half of last season, and is back to run the show again in Gainesville. He'll be aided by Denzel Aberdeen, a key reserve on the 2024-25 national championship squad who spent last season at Kentucky, and sharp-shooting reserve Urban Klavzar.

As long as this group stays healthy (and Aberdeen stays eligible), there just aren't many weaknesses here for opponents to exploit. — Mike Rutherford

Duke loses Cameron Boozer, but might still be better, because Jon Scheyer has ridiculous depth and versatility at every position. Some analysts think Pat Ngongba might be the best player in the country; some think he might be second best on this team behind JoJo Boumtje Boumtje.

Duke has perhaps the deepest guard rotation in the country with Caleb Foster, John Blackwell, Cayden Boozer, and Deron Rippey.

There's depth at the wings, too, with Dame Sarr, Bryson Howard, and exhibition season breakout star Sebastian Wilkins.

All things considered, this might be the deepest team in college since the 2014-15 Kentucky Wildcats. Duke is primed for a major run this year. — JD King, Duke Basketball Report

The Illinois Fighting Illini are expected to be one of the best teams in the country again this season following their historic Final Four run last year. The Illini lose three key pieces in Keaton Wagler, Kylan Boswell and Ben Humrichous but will return the other five rotation players from a season ago. David Mirkovic, Tomislav Ivisic, Zvonimir Ivisic, Andrej Stojakovic and Jake Davis are all back, giving Illinois the third-most returning minutes in the Big Ten behind only Minnesota and Michigan State, according to Bart Torvik.Illinois also added one of the best guards in the transfer portal in former Providence guard Stefan Vaaks, along with five-star freshman Quentin Coleman. Both players should help the Illini form a prolific backcourt to complement their already established frontcourt. While Illinois lost No. 5 overall NBA Draft pick Keaton Wagler, its veteran returners should provide a high floor, while its six-man freshman class, headlined by Coleman and Ethan Brown, should give the team the depth it lacked last season. Health remains the biggest question mark for the Illini, as preseason All-American David Mirkovic was spotted back in a boot ahead of Illinois' exhibition game against Baylor. His absence would significantly lower the team's ceiling. — Robby Nardini, The Champaign Room, @RobbyNardini

UConn's national championship game loss marked the end of a historic run for Tarris Reed Jr. and the end to Alex Karaban's legendary collegiate career. Fortunately for the Huskies, being a top program in the age of NIL and the transfer portal allowed Dan Hurley to reload instead of rebuild. UConn has seven transfers and three freshmen on the roster this season, indicating that a new era is underway in Storrs.

Duke transfer Nik Khamenia will have big shoes to fill to try to replicate Karaban's production, and Seton Hall transfer Na'jai Hines will attempt to follow in the footsteps of Reed, Donovan Clingan and Adama Sanogo and become the next great UConn big man.

Braylon Mullins, who hit one of the most iconic shots in tournament history to lift UConn over Duke and send the Huskies to the Final Four, elected to return to UConn to cement himself as a lottery pick. Silas Demary Jr. also returns and figures to be one of the best point guards in the country.

Jayden Ross will reprise his role as an excellent 3-and-D guy. Solo Ball, a starter all of last year, was set to miss this year due to a wrist injury but now will return at some point this season. Freshmen Colben Landrew and Junior County already look comfortable and will be key pieces in the rotation.

Originally, the expectation was that it would take time for this team to get its bearings, but after impressive exhibition wins at Michigan State and against Purdue, the Huskies seem to be well ahead of schedule. Momentum seems to be building for a real shot at a fourth Final Four in the last five seasons for Hurley and the Huskies. — Dan Madigan, The UConn Blog

Fresh off their first Final Four appearance in 25 years, Arizona brings back two key starters from that 36-win team in center Motiejus Krivas and wing Ivan Kharchenkov. Both passed up opportunities to enter the NBA Draft and were rewarded, both financially and by getting named to the Big 12 preseason all-conference team, where Krivas was tabbed as Preseason Player of the Year. Krivas is also a preseason AP All-American.

The Wildcats added a pair of top-tier guards from the high school ranks, led by projected lottery pick Caleb Holt. They also signed two transfer guards with major upside in Derek Dixon (North Carolina) and JJ Mandaquit (Washington). Two European forwards and a pair of transfer frontcourt players round out the additions.

There's also guard Bryce James, son of NBA superstar LeBron James, who redshirted in 2025-26 but is expected to have a specific role for the Wildcats this season. James started all three preseason games as Arizona went with a 3-guard lineup.

The big question mark is at the power forward position, where the UA was expected to have Clemson transfer RJ Godfrey. The 6-foot-8 Godfrey has been seeking a fifth year of eligibility but has run into numerous legal hurdles, only briefly gaining eligibility through a lawsuit in Georgia before the NCAA appealed and had that temporary restraining order stayed. If he can't go the Wildcats will either move Kharchenkov to the 4 and go small or turn to someone like freshman Maksim Brnovic.

With or without Godfrey, Arizona will contend for another Big 12 title and should be considered among the top contenders for another trip to the Final Four and possibly the program's first national title since 1997. — Brian Pedersen, AZ Desert Swarm

Michigan State fans were certainly drinking the Green Kool-Aid this offseason, especially after PG Jeremy Fears Jr. announced he was withdrawing from the NBA Draft and returning to East Lansing. With the player who garnered the most votes for the preseason All-American team, the Spartans have, arguably, the nation's top point guard running the show. In addition to Fears, the Spartans also feature the most electric player in the game, and that is not up for debate. Coen Carr is a human highlight reel who jumps higher than should be physically possible and provides jaw-dropping dunks in every game. Here is a sample from last year: Coen Carr Top Dunks Compilation, Michigan State Spartans Basketball Highlights | The Only Colors.

However, those offseason vibes quickly turned into preseason jitters when MSU lost a pair of exhibition games to UConn and Marquette. Suddenly, the glass is only about 70% full and there are questions about what MSU's starting lineup is going to look like. Will Coach Tom Izzo go with a small-ball lineup with Coen Carr playing the 4? And who would start at center in that scenario? There are three possibilities at this point, which is to say that none of the options have been good enough to claim that role. Or MSU could go with a traditional lineup where Carr is the small forward, his natural position, but that only creates more questions for the frontcourt.

This year's team's strength is its backcourt and wings. In addition to Fears, MSU brings back Jordan Scott and Kur Teng, both of whom are great outside shooters. Scott especially should be a big part of this year's lineup as he goes into his sophomore year. He was a starter by the end of his freshman season, is a great defender with a long wingspan, and has an incredible motor in getting to loose balls and long rebounds. In addition to those two, MSU has one of the nation's top incoming classes this year, and the jewel in that class is SG Jasiah Jervis. Jervis earned a spot in the starting lineup in the Spartans' final exhibition game and was the high scorer that day with 19. Whether he continues to be a starter in the regular season or one of the first guys off the bench, he is certain to provide a lot of points for this team.

While there is some concern right now among State fans, one thing we can find comfort in is that MSU teams usually tend to get better as the season goes on. So while MSU will probably lose some early games as they once again face one of the country's hardest non-conference schedules (Duke, Tennessee, Arkansas, and Gonzaga all appear on the November/December slate), we can expect Tom Izzo to figure out the rotation by the time we are in Big Ten play and for him to have this team peaking come March. The Final Four is at Ford Field in Detroit this year, just 88 miles from the Spartans home court. Last time the Final Four was in this venue, 2009, MSU made it to the championship game where they lost to North Carolina. There is certainly a world where MSU gets back to that game this year, and maybe even gets that final win to give Tom Izzo his 2nd natty. — O Basse, The Only Colors

After a surprise run to the Sweet 16 last season that ended in a last-second loss to Purdue, Texas faces heightened expectations in Sean Miller's second season on the Forty Acres. The Longhorns will tip off ranked No. 7 in the preseason AP Top 25 thanks to Miller and his staff retaining center Matas Vokietaitis and landing the nation's top incoming class. Colorado transfer guard Isaiah Johnson and TCU transfer forward David Punch headline the five transfers.

Former 2027 No. 1 prospect Marcus Spears Jr. was a late high school addition when he reclassified in July, joining five-star guard Austin Goosby. The Longhorns have substantially more talent this season and higher defensive upside. If simply making the NCAA Tournament was enough to call the 2025-26 season successful, making the Sweet 16 again is now the standard for a team with Final Four potential. — Wescott Eberts, Burnt Orange Nation

Expectations are high on Rocky Top once again, as Rick Barnes has attacked the transfer portal and completely remade his roster for this season. Led by Wake Forest transfer Juke Harris, former VCU standout Terrence Hill Jr. and Cal transfer Dai Dai Ames, Barnes might just have his most capable offensive team that we've seen in Knoxville. However, a couple a shaky exhibition games in October against Duke and Xavier have raised some early concerns. Barnes famously demands a lot from his team defensively, and the buy-in from an entirely group doesn't seem to be there just yet. Tennessee will need to get things together quickly, but the talent is certainly there. Rick Barnes will be looking for his fourth straight Elite Eight appearance this year, and of course, the Volunteers will be trying to make that always-elusive Final Four. — Terry Lambert, Rocky Top Talk

Ryan Odom enters his second season as the head coach of the Cavaliers. Virginia returns a decent group of players from last year and star Thijs De Ridder, who was named the ACC preseason Player of the Year. De Ridder started in all 36 games last season, led Virginia in scoring and rebounding and became the first Cavalier to make the All-ACC first team since Sam Hauser in 2021. The media voted him ACC Preseason Player of the Year with 30 of 48 votes, and he was the only unanimous pick for the preseason All-ACC first team, according to Streaking the Lawn. Chance Mallory, who was named Second Team Preseason All-ACC, has potential for a major breakout season.

Until Kelvin Sampson gives the basketball world a reason to doubt his ability to reload and continue to produce squads that win 30 games and finish in the top two of the Big 12, we should all probably just assume it's going to continue to happen.

Joseph Tugler is back after establishing himself as one of the Big 12's best defensive players, while Mercy Miller and Chase McCarty return as experienced rotation pieces poised to take a step up in production. LSU transfer Dedan Thomas Jr. gives Houston another creator capable of taking on a larger offensive role.

Houston has made seven consecutive Sweet 16s, the longest active streak in the sport and tied for the fourth-longest streak in the history of the expanded tournament. Until Sampson produces a season that is a clear step in the wrong direction for the program, the safe bet is on the Cougars once again being a second weekend squad. — Mike Rutherford

Pat Kelsey has once again made a push for the title of "portal king" after landing a loaded class highlighted by Flory Bidunga (Kansas), Jackson Shelstad (Oregon), Karter Knox (Arkansas), Alvoro Folgueiras (Iowa), and De'Shayne Montgomery (Dayton). The return of guard Adrian Wooley, who came on late last season when star freshman Mikel Brown Jr. was sidelined with a back injury, hasn't received as much national attention but could prove to be just as crucial when it comes to Louisville's chances of making its first NCAA Tournament second weekend since 2015.

The Cardinals will also hope to rely on the services of North Carolina transfer Seth Trimble, who earned a fifth year of eligibility through the courts system. If Trimble is declared ineligible at any point, it'll put immense pressure on Shelstad to carry the load at the point guard position, in addition to taking on the assignment of the other team's best scoring guard defensively.

Also worth keeping an eye on is five-star freshman big man Obinna Ekezie, who had a coming out party of sorts in Louisville's exhibition win at Kansas last month. The 7'2 center has the potential to be an NBA lottery pick. — Mike Rutherford, Card Chronicle

John Calipari reloaded the Arkansas roster with length, explosiveness, and lottery-bound NBA talent once again, and it makes the Hogs a legitimate threat in the SEC if they can find answers to their biggest concern. Jordan Smith is Cal's next future top-five pick. He's a 6'2 guard who plays like he's 6'9, wrecking the game with elite defensive playmaking and rebounding from the guard position while attacking the basket with straight line drives and acrobatic finishes offensively.

Smith is joined by fellow top-20 overall freshman JJ Andrews and Abdou Toure, who should help make this team an absolute terror in the open floor. Calipari also brought back a key returner in Swiss Army Knife Billy Richmond III, and added a high-volume shooter in the transfer portal in Jeremiah Wilkinson. The big question for Arkansas are the big men. It feels like the best teams in college basketball all have great bigs, and Arkansas does not. Still, this team has tons of firepower and should be thrilling to watch all season. — Ricky O'Donnell

There's no question that the St. John's brand is back. Rick Pitino has led the program to back-to-back Big East titles and most recently took the Red Storm to their first Sweet Sixteen since 1999. What's left to wonder is if the Red Storm can reach another gear or two under their Hall of Fame head coach, especially after program icon and team captain Zuby Ejiofor graduated.

No team will be more impacted by eligibility drama than St. John's. While the Red Storm got good news when the NCAA cleared composed floor general Quinn Ellis, who should become one of the country's top point guards, they still await word on fifth-year Auburn transfer Keyshawn Hall and ex-G-Leaguer Babacar Sane. Each forward brings an exciting scoring touch, physicality, and rebounding presence.

They will have two returning players expected to start every night. Former McDonald's All-American Ian Jackson is poised for a post-hype breakout as he returns to his natural shooting guard position, and fan favorite Ruben Prey hopes to show he can fill Ejiofor's gargantuan shoes in the post.

If the Red Storm get their full complement of players, they are serious contenders to win their third consecutive Big East crown and more than just dark horses to reach their first Final Four in 42 years. — Thomas Cavanagh, Rumble in the Garden

The Miami Hurricanes enter the 2026-27 season with a lot of optimism, as they look to see how big the next step is that they can take after a massive leap last season. The 2025-26 team had a 19-win improvement (which tied the NCAA record) from the previous season in Jai Lucas' first year, going 26-9 and making it to the second round of the NCAA Tournament.

Some of the most important pieces of the 2025-26 team that pleasantly surprised return. Star forward Malik Reneau was able to gain a final year of eligibility this offseason through the legal process, and he is back to spearhead a talented roster. Reneau earned 18.9 points and 6.5 rebounds per game to go with 72 assists last year, earning first-team All-ACC honors. He was a do-it-all player in the frontcourt, and the importance of his return cannot be overstated. Guard Shelton Henderson (13.7 ppg) is another big piece back. Transfers Acaden Lewis (Villanova), Somto Cyril (Georgia), DeSean Goode (Robert Morris) and Brent Bland (St. Peters) are new faces who will all play varying but important roles this year. True freshman forward Caleb Gaskins is another player to watch. Lewis and Cyril led the Hurricanes in scoring with 24 and 13 points, respectively, in Miami's season-opening 77-67 loss to No. 1 Florida on Monday night.

Make no mistake though: Miami has the makeup of a Final Four team this season. They were fourth in the ACC preseason rankings behind Duke, Virginia, and Louisville, and that might be too low. With what he did last season, you have to give Lucas the benefit of the doubt that he'll mold this group into a true contender by March. — Craig Smith, State of the U

No college basketball program during this anarchic offseason had to deal with more dragging lawsuits against the NCAA than the Gonzaga Bulldogs. They're, in fact, still trying to gain 5-in-5 eligibility for Florida State Seminoles transfer/forward Chauncey Wiggins.On top of all that nonsense, the Zags were in search of a new starting point guard throughout the summer after Mario Saint-Supery abruptly returned to his home country of Spain to play with Valencia Basket in the Liga ACB and the EuroLeague. Luckily, fifth-year Florida Gators transfer Xaivian Lee, fifth-year Dayton Flyers transfer Javon Bennett, as well as French lead guard Nathan De Sousa were able to find their way to the Pacific Northwest. That's a major lift to the backcourt, something that was desperately needed. Now, can these additions help in the different three-point shooting categories? Last season, Gonzaga finished No. 218 in the nation at 33.3 percent from beyond the arc. That simply won't cut it. Even with all these obstacles thrown their way, Hall of Fame coach Mark Few and his staff were still able to put together a talented 2026-27 roster, especially in the loaded frontcourt. The trio of sophomore wing Davis Fogle, redshirt senior power forward Braden Huff, and Arizona State Sun Devils transfer/sophomore center Massamba Diop have brought back Final Four aspirations to the Spokane, Washington area as they prepare for their first season in the Pac-12 Conference. Another name to remember? Spanish forward/center Izan Almansa, coming in from Real Madrid of the Liga ACB and EuroLeague. The 6-10 talent has all the tools to be the most impactful bench piece in the game. Now, can these additions help in the different three-point shooting categories? Last season, Gonzaga finished No. 218 in the nation at 33.3 percent from beyond the arc. That simply won't cut it. Houston Cougars transfer/shooting guard Isiah Harwell should bump that number up. — Arden Cravalho, The Slipper Still Fits

You always worry about championship hangovers in college sports, and with Dusty May out and Mike Boynton Jr. in, I'd say the expectations for Michigan have lessened a bit. They will not have three lottery picks in the front court this year, but they have a really good back court in experienced pick-and-roll maestro Elliot Cadeau and breakout candidate Trey McKenney. Roddy Gayle Jr. returning for a fifth year (pending his eligibility case) helps Michigan on the wing, Cincinnati transfer Moustapha Thiam is a rim protector with an intriguing offensive skillset, and Tennessee transfer JP Estrella was a top-10 offensive rebounder in the country last season.

The biggest question for this team will be its depth; as intriguing as Ricky Liburd and Quinn Costello are off the bench, they are both inexperienced. Michigan probably won't be a historically great team like last year, but Boynton has a stellar defensive scheme and the team has enough talent at the top of the roster to finish in the top-four of the Big Ten and make another run in the NCAA Tournament with a favorable draw. — Kellen Voss, Maize N Brew

There is a lot up in the air coming into this season for Kansas basketball. Perhaps the biggest unknown is whether Bill Self, who publicly acknowledged that he considered retirement last spring, is coaching his final season. But beyond that, two expected returning starters in Melvin Council, Jr and Tre White are only eligible due to injunctions, and we haven't actually seen newcomer Trey Kaufman-Renn, who was a massive pickup from Purdue, in a Kansas uniform yet. Add in that the Kaufman-Renn eligibility case is a bit different from the other two, and nothing feels set in stone at the moment.

With that said, we know that freshman Tyran Stokes has a real shot at being next year's 1st pick in the NBA draft, and through two exhibitions, you can see why he has that kind of hype. 5 star freshman point guard Taylen Kinney has shown flashes, but seems like he'll need time to settle in and play the position at this level. Utah transfer Keanu Dawes was a huge acquisition and will be a great, versatile big man even if Kaufman-Renn weren't able to suit up, and the Jayhawks have a pair of 7 footers they can use for rim protection. If Toledo transfer Leroy Blyden provides the shooting he showed last year, it could round out a very competitive roster, along with pure scorer Dennis Parker.

Self has struggled to generate effective offense in recent years, but this year's pieces seem to fit a bit better than what he's worked with recently, and the defense is pretty much always there under his watch. If things click with all the new pieces in place, this team can win a title. We'll probably have a better idea of how likely that is by the end of the non-conference slate. — David Potter, Rock Chalk Talk

No. 18 Iowa State heads into the 2026-27 mens college basketball season with a mix of challenges and opportunities.

The team faces roster turnover and injury recovery after a 29-8 season that ended in the Sweet 16. Head coach T.J. Otzelberger, who has a 124-53 record entering this year, began the season on Nov. 2 against Memphis in Sioux Falls, South Dakota.

The Cyclones must replace their two scorers from last season: forwards Milan Momcilovic, who averaged 16.9 points per game and Joshua Jefferson, who scored 16.4 points per game. There is a bright spot in the return of senior guard Tamin Lipsey. Lipsey, a native of Ames is staying for a year of eligibility. He finished his career with 1,507 points, 602 assists and a school-record 314 steals.

To rebuild the rotation Iowa State added five Division I transfers. The incoming players include guard Jaquan "Bully" Johnson, the 2026 Missouri Valley Conference Defensive Player of the Year from Bradley. They also brought in forward Tre Singleton from Northwestern and guard Leon Bond III from Northern Iowa.

Senior Blake Buchanan returns as a key interior starter. The team will be without sophomore guard Killyan Toure until January after he underwent shoulder surgery in August. Freshman guard Christian Wiggins will miss the season after tearing his Achilles tendon over the summer.

Statistically, Iowa State's identity under Otzelberger continues to focus on defense and forcing turnovers. Season the Cyclones ranked fifth nationally in adjusted defensive efficiency and fourth, in forced turnover rate at 22.6%. Coach Otzelberger says playing time will be based on production as the team adjusts to the Big 12 schedule. — Jared Larson, Wide Right Natty Lite

The Red Raiders are fielding a team with multiple players who should be out of eligibility, but look to get back on the floor because of court rulings. Darrion Williams and Donovan Atwell both graduated high school in 2022, with Atwell playing NBA Summer League with the Chicago Bulls, but they returned to college because the money is better and no one stopped them. Williams' all-around scoring and creation and Atwell's knockdown shooting should make this team very good when combined with head coach Grant McCasland's other pieces. Dra Gibbs-Lawhorn is another experienced player who can score in bunches out of the backcourt, while Hofstra transfer Cruz Davis can rip shots from three and run some offense as the lead guard. The center position is an open question mark, but if anyone steps up, Texas Tech will be loaded with this much veteran talent. — Ricky O'Donnell

Alabama enters the 2026–27 season with the ingredients to make another deep NCAA Tournament run. The Tide has an established backcourt in Amari Allen and Aden Holloway, a veteran sharp-shooting big in Drew Fielder, and substantially more frontcourt size than in previous seasons. Add in a freshman class that appears to be sorely underrated and a potential game-changing marksman in Cole Cloer, and the ceiling is quite high.

On paper, this looks like a team capable of competing for a SEC title and making a deep run in March. Alabama is a projected top 12 squad according to both KenPom and Evan Miya.

But, as always, you never know how a team with this amount of turnover will come together until it happens. With 10 teams projected in the KenPom top 30, the SEC is projected to be as brutal as ever this season. — Joshua Chatham, Roll Bama Roll

USC missed the NCAA tournament in the first two seasons of the Eric Musselman era, and the head coach acted with a level of desperation over the offseason to make sure that streak doesn't extend to a third year. USC should be highly competitive in the Big Ten with the firepower to out-score most of the league. Rodney Rice returns for his senior year, and should be one of the better scoring guards in the country. Forward Jacob Cofie is also back after testing the NBA Draft process, and he gives Musselman a long and athletic 6'10 forward who consistently makes a positive impact on both ends of the floor. Alijah Arenas feels like the wildcard who can take this team to the next level with his microwave scoring ability. UConn transfer Eric Reibe is currently recovering from foot surgery, and his return looms large to make the Trojans a formidable defense. USC at least has the depth to whether his absence with a star-studded freshman class led by five-stars Christian Collins, Adonis Ratliff, and Darius Ratliff. The Big Ten is loaded again, but USC should be able to play with anyone. — Ricky O'Donnell

The UCLA Bruins enter the 2026–27 season with a combination of optimism and still feeling like they have something to prove. After finishing 24-12 last season and reaching the NCAA Tournament's second round, the Bruins seem capable of taking another step forward and challenging to be the best of the best in the Big 10.

The Bruins will be led by returning standouts Trent Perry and Eric Dailey Jr., who provide scoring, experience, and leadership. Perry averaged 12.6 points per game last season and will be counted on to take another step forward as the team's primary backcourt leader. Dailey averaged 11.6 points and brings valuable versatility.

Texas Tech transfer Jaylen Petty adds perimeter shooting, while Auburn transfer Filip Jovic brings physicality and some rebounding inside. Freshman Nikola Kusturica, a 17-year-old from Serbia, is likely the top NBA prospect on the team and one of the more intriguing and exciting players on the West Coast.

If the pieces can gel and find a rhythm, Mick Cronin's team has the potential to be a second weekend (and perhaps beyond) team come NCAA Tournament time. — Mike Rutherford

Kentucky Basketball probably had the most turbulent offseason in terms of recruiting. Between their early high school and portal misses, landing Milan Momcolivic, and having Mark Mitchell commit before backing out, Kentucky did manage to build what could be Mark Pope's best roster since being hired in 2024.

Now, whether that's a team capable of Final Four contention or one that's destined to go down in the first weekend remains to be seen, but having someone with superstar potential like Momcilovic should at least keep this team afloat in the regular season.

And just like last year, point guard is a question mark. While Zoom Diallo was signed to be the guy, he struggled in exhibition play, while freshman Mason Williams consistently looked like the better option to run the offense. While the real games have yet to be played, Williams looks like someone ready to make a major impact for this team, despite not being a top-100 recruit in the class of 2026.

Then there's Malachi Moreno, one of the more promising young centers last season, who should be poised for a major leap in Year 2. But outside of Moreno, the frontcourt is a big question mark. The 4 spot will be a heated battle between James Madison transfer Justin McBride and international recruit Ousmane N'Diaye. Both offer unique skill sets, but the 6-foot-11 N'Diaye clearly has the higher ceiling with his 7-foot-3 wingspan and ability to knock down 3s. He may very well become one of the better international big men this season and could be a sneaky candidate to hear his name called in the 2027 NBA Draft.

The biggest x-factor for this team is sophomore shooting guard Alex Wilkins, one of the top mid-major players last year who had a fantastic showing in Furman's Round of 64 loss to national runner-up UConn. If Wilkins can become that player on a consistent basis, then this team could really take off come March.

Kentucky won't start the year ranked highly, and they'll likely take some early-season bumps with a loaded non-conference schedule. But when it comes time for the postseason, this could become a dangerous team that's capable of making a second-weekend run, potentially even advancing to the program's first Elite Eight since 2019. — Jason Marcum, A Sea of Blue

Only three players return from the 27-9 team that lost to Nebraska in the second round of the NCAA Tournament, but one of the three is Tyler Tanner, who withdrew his name from the NBA Draft at the last possible minute and returned to Vanderbilt. Tanner, who averaged 19.5 ppg as a sophomore, begins the season on the short list for National Player of the Year. Surrounding him will be a solid if unspectacular group of transfer portal additions, including Ace Glass (Washington State), Berke Buyuktuncel (Nebraska), and Bangot Dak (Colorado.) Mark Byington also signed a promising freshman class.

The biggest question mark for the Commodores is whether the talent around Tanner this season is what it was last season. The departures of Tyler Nickel, Duke Miles, AK Okereke, Devin McGlockton, and Jalen Washington are significant ones — and not ones that Vanderbilt fans just assume will be replaced by the incoming talent. The newcomers will help, but another place to look is one of the other returnees: Chandler Bing (yes, that's his real name), who only averaged 3.6 ppg a year ago but saw his minutes pick up from late January until the end of the season. The 6'6″ wing could give Byington the positional flexibility to make his system work.

Like Byington's first two seasons, Vanderbilt has a pretty light non-conference schedule — a November 24 trip to Ohio State is Vanderbilt's only projected loss before SEC play per KenPom, and the Commodores have a 45% chance of winning that one. A top-4 seed in the NCAA Tournament and a trip to the second weekend, neither of which have happened in almost 20 years, are realistic goals, but Vanderbilt fans have spent an offseason dreaming beyond that. — Thomas Stephenson, Anchor of Gold

Spacing and shooting. Those are the two words you should know about this BYU team. BYU might have the best shooting team in college basketball. With Michigan State transfer Jaxon Kohler eligible, BYU has four players who shot 39%+ from three at the Power Conference level last year. The fifth, Bruce Branch, is a 5-star freshman and projected top 10 pick in next year's NBA Draft. With Rob Wright returning to run the show and Jaxon Kohler giving BYU a legit inside presence, this team is much more well-rounded than last year's with AJ Dybantsa. The question is defense and rebounding — can BYU defend and rebound at a competent enough level to warrant a second weekend trip in the NCAA Tournament? If BYU can be decent in those areas, they will be among the top teams in the Big 12. — Robby McCombs, Vanquish the Foe

#2852. AI-generated 'slopaganda' in political ads puts internet platforms on alert

The midterm election cycle has been dominated by "slopaganda," or campaign ads featuring AI-generated clips and images, creating another challenge for Instagram, YouTube and X as they attempt to monitor their platforms.

The term slopaganda dates back to early last year, when a group of AI experts used it in a paper to describe a new phenomenon combining generative AI slop with propaganda and misinformation.

There's no comprehensive federal law governing the use or disclosure of AI in political ads. And while there are some 30 state laws with different requirements about AI use and disclosure, candidates don't always abide by them. On the leading internet sites run by Meta, Google and Elon Musk's SpaceX, users run into AI-generated ads and AI-generated posts shared by politicians, alongside authentic videos and clips.

More than $187 million has been spent on nearly 300 ads generated or enhanced by AI, according to an analysis released last week by the Wesleyan Media Project. That's almost certainly an undercount, the organization says, because so many ads don't contain disclaimers. Over 90% of the spending came from Republican candidates, a Republican party committee or a Republican-leaning group, the report found, and most don't include disclosures.

CNBC found a number of AI-generated ads running online without disclosures, including a fake video of New York Democratic Governor Kathy Hochul that was posted by her opponent, Republican challenger Bruce Blakeman.

In Texas, Republican senatorial candidate Ken Paxton created an AI-generated video showing Democratic opponent James Talarico making controversial statements, with a three-second disclosure flashing in the middle. It's being shared as organic content on YouTube and X by Paxton's campaign.

The Blakeman and Paxton campaigns didn't immediately respond to requests for comment.

YouTube told CNBC that the company applies "prominent labels to realistic AI content."

"For organic content on YouTube, political candidates can request removal where their face appears to be altered or generated by AI," the company said. "For advertisers, we require disclosure when an election ad contains synthetic or digitally altered people or events to provide viewers with transparency."

YouTube parent Google also requires political ads to complete its election ads verification process and include "paid for by" disclosures.

X says advertisers must comply with applicable laws, putting the responsibility on the advertiser, not the platform. The company didn't respond to CNBC's request for comment.

Meta referred to its guidelines on political ads, including restrictions on their use in the week prior to the election. The company also mandates "paid for by" labels and maintains an ad library of political ads. In June, the company ramped up its labeling, adding a new "about this ad" tool for ads that Meta detects have been created with third-party AI tools.

One risk the tech platforms face with generative AI is being asked to create misleading images and videos. The Brennan Center for Justice tested ChatGPT, Gemini, Grok, Claude, Perplexity and DeepSeek for their willingness to facilitate deceptive content. The study from August found that many models may avoid generating content around familiar conspiracies, but can generally be manipulated into creating misleading material.

A retest of those platforms in October found that ChatGPT had made progress. It had started redirecting prompts for harmful election images into illustrations so they look like comics, or refusing to take action on them altogether.

A number of national politicians are trying to address the issue.

The Protect Elections from Deceptive AI Act, backed by senators from both parties, would prohibit knowingly distributing materially deceptive AI-generated video or audio about federal candidates. Other bills are being led by Democrats in Congress, including the AI Ads Act, which is looking to expand current bans on campaign misrepresentation to include generative AI content.

None of the proposed bills have advanced out of committee.

#2850. Santoli: AI bull market almost surely needs tech stocks to recover to keep advancing

The road to bull-market glory does not run through Walmart and Berkshire Hathaway and Eli Lilly.

There's been a virtual buying panic in traditionally defensive, high-quality stocks over the past few weeks, a near-equal-and-opposite offset to the selling pressure in semiconductors and AI-build-out spenders. The three stocks above — the only members of the $1 trillion market-cap club not in Big Tech – have collectively added $550 billion in market value in the past month, almost making up for what Alphabet has lost.

The Nasdaq-100 and the S&P 500 Low Volatility ETF (SPLV) are rushing toward one another in a satisfying picture of symmetry that nonetheless reflects an almost comical level of tactical rotation executed by an investment community accustomed to playing one factor against another.

While this to-and-fro has so far protected the S&P 500 as a whole from an appreciable setback for months, it has left it stymied around levels that date back to May — before we got two earnings-reporting seasons showing 30% annual profit growth.

A week ago here, I suggested the AI theme was nearing a make-or-break moment to stage a recovery or risk surrendering its leadership status and destabilizing the broader market. Thus far, this trade has failed to respond to moderate oversold conditions or a run of cosmetically strong hyperscaler quarterly results, further loosening its grip on the benefit of the doubt.

In stressed markets undergoing soul-searching about the long-term bullish thesis, prices often need to travel further to find buyers with conviction while the market action must disappoint dip-buyers to extinguish complacency. Hard to know if we're there, but presumably we're now closer.

The Nasdaq-100 closed Monday right at its 200-day moving average, off almost 11% from its record high. After an enervating failed Monday-morning bounce attempt, the Philadelphia Semiconductor Index finished down another 3.8% and settled under the late-July low that was set as the Situational Awareness hedge fund was finished liquidating.

"Markets did not get the memo that bearish seasonal pressure typically subsides in mid-October and instead ended the month with some of the most acute selling pressure that has been realized during the late-summer/early-fall period," notes JPMorgan technical strategist Jason Hunter. He's advising clients to remain on the defensive "until we see clear evidence of a bottoming process on the charts or until there are clear signs of capitulation."

This is a time-tested playbook, though many a sharp market pullback has ended without what would qualify as unqualified capitulation.

The market action is winning some plaudits for the way non-tech stocks have held up better, many groups benefiting from the "quality" rotation while abandoned consumer cyclicals get a reprieve from easing oil prices and the slide in the 10-year Treasury yield back below 5%.

This gives active managers plenty to do in playing against a highly concentrated and tech-dependent S&P 500. But I'll continue to argue that the bull market built on the AI story will almost surely need the AI story to get back on track to keep advancing. A prolonged period when the equal-weight S&P 500 outperforms the market-cap-weighted version is otherwise known – usually – as a bear market.

The AI-related stocks' weighting in the S&P 500 is off the highs but remains near 45%, according to Morgan Stanley.

As the chart shows, AI has wobbled relative to the rest of the market during this uptrend before reasserting itself.  But there's a trickier element to the current gut check: In prior phases of the buildout, the key limiting bottleneck gave investors something to buy in order to exploit it.

The initial scarcity of GPUs meant to own Nvidia above all else from 2023 through 2024. When cloud capacity, power and cooling were found to be the crucial scarce resources, hyperscaler stocks worked and the AI-adjacent industrials were drastically revalued. Then memory emerged as the chokepoint and Micron and Sandisk went stratospheric.

Now, as Nvidia's Jensen Huang has said, capital is the constraint and that not only leaves no asset to buy, but it raises the cost of capital broadly as the mega-caps go free-cash-flow-negative.

Overlapping feedback loops and scrambled correlations across the markets and the real economy have further sapped investor conviction. Treasury yields no longer move with oil but largely with AI-capex sentiment, with both in retreat lately. AI-stock setbacks also mute inflation momentum and the equity wealth effect, meaning fewer Federal Reserve rate hikes are anticipated now versus a month ago.

This all probably sounds like an elaborate trap, and it has become one for the previously crowded trades. Could this reflect the end game for the AI-exuberance market chapter, which as I keep pointing out peaked around the SpaceX IPO in June? Impossible to say for sure, but if it were, I'm not sure things would look much different to this point.

But I don't want to dismiss the possibility that a lot of the known challenges are largely reflected in prices.

I argued in August that we should assume the valuation peak for this cycle was in. We've now retrenched to a forward P/E of 18.5 on the S&P 500 from 23 a year ago and the Nasdaq-100 at 20x is at the slimmest premium to the S&P 500 valuation in nine years. I'm on board with the idea that the market is telling us companies are now "over-earning," but if nothing else there is less valuation risk now than in recent months.

Sentiment toward AI stocks is in the dumps. Funds' positioning in tech has retreated below neutral. Important trading lows this year have happened near the turn of the month on March 30 and July 29, and the Nasdaq-100's relative-strength reading is exactly as oversold as it was at those turns.

So, stay alert for possible relief bounces that could evolve into something more consequential if indeed the midterm elections pass in a way that allows traders to unclench a bit into the year's home stretch.

John Kolovos of Macro Risk Advisors on the latest reading: "Similar to valuation, sentiment is not a timing tool, but it is getting harder and harder to find any bulls, as a near-perfect storm of tight financial conditions is being met with tech earnings that have gone from bad to worse in the blink of an eye."

-Another good big-picture conversation on the practical business and investment implications of the next phase of AI proliferation on Patrick O'Shaughnessy's Invest Like the Best podcast, this one with investor Cliff Sosin. His comparison of AI infrastructure economics to internet connectivity providers and his argument for how most of the productivity boost will go toward "consumer surplus" rather than corporate profits are particularly interesting.

-For my birthday Monday, my colleague and friend Sara Eisen wondered how I would write a birthday message to myself. I immediately regretted suggesting she ask ChatGPT, which generated this credible and somewhat embarrassing result:

"Another year older, though the longer-term trend remains constructive. Some modest deterioration in the underlying internals, perhaps, but nothing that suggests a decisive breakdown.

"The candles on the cake are approaching historically elevated levels, though valuation concerns have persisted for years without derailing the advance. At this stage, the key question isn't whether I've gotten older — that's largely priced in — but whether the wisdom gains can continue to offset the inevitable compression in energy multiples.

"For now, I'm maintaining a cautiously optimistic outlook, with upside optionality, downside support from family and friends, and no immediate need for Fed intervention. Happy birthday to me. The secular bull market remains intact."

The stampede into perceived defensive stocks has been a crucial driver of Apple shares' massive outperformance over the Nasdaq-100 since the AI-capex agita flared up months ago. Apple heading into Monday's earnings report had outperformed the Nasdaq-100 by nearly 20 percentage points over the prior six months.

Its singular refusal to join the aggressive spending binge to build AI capacity preserved its free cash flow and made it the perfect counterweight to the capex trade, while inflating Apple's valuation to a 15-year high.

But its advantage in free-cash-flow yield has now narrowed, thanks to its stock-price gains and the collapse of peer shares.

#2849. Meta seeks to raise up to $25 billion in debt sale as AI spending jumps

Meta is seeking to raise between $20 billion and $25 billion in a bond sale on Tuesday as the company ramps up its spending on artificial intelligence infrastructure along with AI models and services.

The debt sale is expected to be two-thirds in U.S. dollars and one-third in euros, according to people familiar with the matter who asked not to be named because the details are private. Final pricing for the bond sale should come Tuesday afternoon, the sources said.

Meta declined to comment. Bloomberg News first reported on Meta's plans.

Meta's bond sale comes days after S&P Global Ratings revised the company's outlook from AA- stable to AA- negative, citing "uncertainty" about its hefty capex spending and negative free cash flow.

In its third-quarter earnings report last month, the Facebook parent revealed that its full-year capex will come in between $135 billion to $145 billion, representing a $5 billion increase to the low end of its previous guidance. Meta posted negative free cash flow of $2.12 billion for its latest quarter, joining Alphabet and Amazon in reporting a negative figure.

Meta previously raised $25 billion in an April bond sale, and said last week that it plans to raise $95 billion in an effort to fund its AI initiatives. Of that, Meta intends to first raise $35 billion in underwritten public equity offerings, and an additional $60 billion through an at-the-market program.

Meta's stock rose 2.7% on Tuesday after dropping in 12 of the prior 14 trading sessions and closing out October with its worst month in four years.

#2848. Knicks 103, Nets 90: “Shoot 3s better, win games”

January 28, 2023, was a day when Yuta Watanabe scored 9 points, Joe Harris was playing basketball and Nic Claxton still felt like a somewhat promising prospect. It was a day when Julius Randle, RJ Barrett and Immanuel Quickley were still donning the orange and blue Knicks jersey, Jericho Sims practiced for the dunk contest before the game and Evan Fournier, for some incomprehensible reason, got a DNP.

The day also marked the last time the Brooklyn Nets beat the New York Knickerbockers.

Obviously, a lot of things have happened between the game almost four years ago and today. Kyrie Irving's career path has considerably flattened since falsely inspiring the 27 fans of his former team, and the Knicks have changed almost their entire roster (luckily not the guy wearing number 11) and now have a shiny little ring to show for it. It's hard to put the distance between the two franchises into words, but if you needed a reminder of it, last night's game was a good illustration.

For long stretches, New York did not really deserve to win this game. They settled for three after three, sleepwalked through one pick and roll after another and were constantly late to the orange leather ball. They didn't think it was necessary to feature Karl-Anthony Towns against a team that had no answer for him in the past; they didn't think the opponent was dangerous enough to punish the experiments with their own lineup. And you know what? It all didn't matter because the New York Knicks are a good basketball team and the Nets are not.

What they are, though, is a scrappy young bunch (actually the youngest there is in the league) with some decent players and an inspiring coach, a team that is hellbent on trying to get the most out of their limited resources—be it on the court or later at the trade deadline. For three quarters, that was plenty enough to give the Knicks some headaches, as Brooklyn actually led the contest going into the fourth quarter. But then the New York bench happened.

Just like a few nights ago in Chicago, Mike Brown found the right mix of guys from the pine, and Tyler Kolek, Landry Shamet and Jordan Clarkson completely turned around a game that never felt like it was in danger yet still needed to be taken care of late. The bench boys did that and then some. Even Andre Drummond got into the mix and played some smart, efficient basketball while in there. For all the might New York's starting lineup supposedly has, you could argue that they would have to answer a bunch of uncomfortable questions if it weren't for their reserves early in the year.

No matter who you take, they all have shown the ability to completely change the dynamic of a game in an instant, which truly is one of the most important qualities a reserve has to possess. The Knicks' offense is flowing visibly faster with Tyler Kolek at the point right now, Landry Shamet's motor seems to be running longer and more reliably than Haile Gebrselassie ever did, and Clarkson is playing every game like an Olympic berth for the Philippines is on the line.

Now, make no mistake about it: they all need serious talent next to them to actually thrive, but that, in the end, is the epitome of a team—a group of individuals that all bring different strengths to the table. For the early part of the season, most of the Knicks' reserves, more often than not, have held up their part of the equation and reminded us all that depth was a key component in New York's championship run last year. The same can be said for the uncanny ability to have someone else step up on any given night.

The bench was also a big reason why TomRogerAstro's seemingly simple headline from the Postgame Thread rang true. The Knicks have been a bit up and mostly down with their three-point shooting so far this season, and it was a welcome sight to see them find their range, especially late in the game. Brooklyn had no answer once the shots fell, the crowd got ignited and the already pretty solid defense completely locked in to secure the victory.

One Knick who is still waiting for things to turn around, though, is Mikal Bridges. Once again, he only "played" 16 minutes, in which he actually drove all the way to the rim once. If anything can tell you that he feels a little desperate right now, it should be that he actually did not settle for a midrange shot but tried to finish inside. That's what 30 strong minutes from Landry Shamet time and again can do to you. It's not a controversy yet; we've been there before. But as more and more people stream about it, this can get pretty dicey soon.

If games against the Nets will ever get dicey again, you ask? Well, they might, but not without a lot of things happening. And don't worry, we haven't heard about the Knicks giving Evan Fournier a call recently.

#2847. Binance co-CEO Richard Teng says hackers used fake job interviews, AI tool to steal $6.3 billion in crypto

Binance said hackers used fake job interviews, malicious software and compromised internal AI tools to infiltrate its wallet infrastructure, leading to the record-breaking Oct. 29 cyberattack that resulted in more than $6 billion in assets being stolen from the world's largest crypto exchange.

The attackers "spent months building their way in," Binance co-CEO Richard Teng told CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" Tuesday, rejecting suggestions that there was a single point of failure through one employee's laptop.

"In June, [the attackers] set up a fake version of a developer tool and lured one of our engineers to install that," he added. "In September they posed as recruiters and ran a series of job interviews with another engineer, and that ended up in a coding exercise that contained hidden malware designed to evade security ... from there they moved violently through our internal AI tools and obtain[ed] access that breach[ed] the system that authorized our wallet transaction."

He also said Binance has replenished 98% of $6.34 billion in affected assets, and that it is still in the process of purchasing outstanding, more illiquid assets.

Teng himself did not attribute the attack to North Korea but acknowledged that the FBI has linked it to North Korean hackers tied to the TraderTraitor group.

Preparations for the attack began in June, when a Binance Wallet DevOps engineer downloaded a tampered Homebrew installation "to install a popular AI coding tool after searching for it on a search engine," according to a security report published by Binance Tuesday. It said it detected the suspicious activity, replaced the engineer's device and rotated credentials as a precaution, but that by then the employee's "role and his related colleagues on our wallet infrastructure team was exposed to the hacker."

As Binance works to recover from the incident, it has temporarily restricted certain withdrawals, pledged to cover all customer losses and tapped its own reserves to replenish affected assets. Binance and industry partners including Tether, Circle and Ripple have reportedly frozen about $3 billion in assets, though some stolen funds continue to move through laundered channels. Coinbase blocked transfers to compromised wallets and offered Binance direct assistance.

Although the incident represents a significant security breach, Binance maintains that it remains financially sound and focused on identifying the vulnerability, recovering remaining funds and preventing another attack.

The hack sparked a sharp but short-lived hit to confidence across crypto markets, especially compared with previous failures at major exchanges. Investors are now watching whether Binance can fully restore confidence in centralized exchanges.

As of Tuesday Binance has rebuilt the affected wallet system and internal AI tool, replaced compromised devices, decommissioned credentials and tokens associated with the affected engineers and expanded security scans across employee devices, Teng told "Squawk on the Street."

"We're also getting an independent expert to review everything that we have done, and we'll keep strengthening our defenses as the investigation continues," Teng said. "We're working around the clock with law enforcement agencies and with industry partners to trace and freeze the stolen funds for that front."

So, a good day stopping the run, but a very rough day disrupting the passer.

He triggered late to a receiver at the sticks on another third down, allowing a first. Just wasn't his day after also having a solid day against USC.

#2843. Do Miami Heat have a fatal flaw? Giannis, Bam Adebayo issue warning

LOS ANGELES – Off to the side, beyond the end of the bench, Heat players Bam Adebayo and Davion Mitchell were standing on the baseline, talking with their hands on their hips.

The sober looks on their faces, however, gave the impression that it was something else. It looked like more of a debrief. As in: how did it get like this?

Just to their right, one seat from the end of the bench, sat Giannis Antetokounmpo, who draped a towel over his shoulders. He was staring blankly ahead, his head resting on his hand.

Once again, an opposing team had bombarded the Miami Heat. This time, it was Luka Dončić and the Los Angeles Lakers on Monday, Nov. 2 in a 138-117 torching. Miami jumped out to a 4-0 lead in the opening minutes. It was the last time the Heat would be ahead.

And as these remade Heat seek to build a contender around Antetokounmpo and Adebayo, it's not the offense or perimeter shooting that has become the problem. It is – surprisingly – the team's defense. All of which raises a question, and a significant one, even this early in the season.

Is this a fatal flaw that will prevent Miami from making the playoffs?

Realistically, it's far too early for such sweeping pronouncements. The lack of cohesion, however, is significant enough to be a serious concern.

"We're not playing hard enough," Antetokounmpo told reporters after the game. "We've got to play hard, have some pride. Individual pride, first of all, and then we've got to keep one another accountable and trust one another defensively. Right now we don't trust one another. We're not giving that extra effort.

"Effort, individual pride and trust. We've got to have those three things."

More: Watch: LeBron James takes a sip of 76ers fan's wine mid-game

The Heat have a collection of stellar individual defenders. Adebayo is an elite stopper, a six-time All-Defensive selection and a player who can guard any position. Antetokounmpo is six years removed from his Defensive Player of the Year award but remains a force in the paint. Mitchell, the starting point guard, has the nickname "Off Night" because convention goes that when he's guarding a prominent offensive player, said player will have an off night. Andrew Wiggins is a defensive-minded wing with plenty of length.

Yet, the sum has not been greater than the parts, and it shows.

"A lot of times I feel like when fatigue sets in, we stop communicating, and that's when mistakes happen," Adebayo said. "That's when bad habits set in. The lack of effort is there. We've got to figure it out."

The Lakers made eight of their first nine shots. They dropped 79 points in the first half. Entering the night, NBA teams had been averaging 113.7 points per game; Los Angeles had already reached 111 by the end of the third quarter.

Dončić flamed the Heat to the tune of 42 points and fellow guard Austin Reaves dropped 27.

And after the Lakers converted on 19-of-43 (44.2%) attempts from beyond the arc, no team in the NBA is allowing more made 3-pointers per game (17.3) than the Heat. Making matters worse, Miami also ranks second-to-last in opposing 3-point percentage at 41%; by comparison, the last-place team, the Suns, are giving up 3s at a 41.1% clip.

It's not just perimeter defense, either. Time after time Monday night, Lakers players blew past Heat defenders on closeouts, as L.A. outscored Miami in the paint by a margin of 60-40.

To a certain extent, this lack of trust does make some sense. The Heat traded four players to land Antetokounmpo and the group is still figuring out how to play together. So, in practical terms, what does this lack of trust actually look like on the floor?

"Like, when a guy beats you off the dribble, sometimes you don't close out all the way to Luka, and that is because you don't feel like somebody has your back," Antetokounmpo said. "So then you give him air space to hit a stepback 3 or pull up for a 3. Same thing with Austin Reaves: you have to close out all the way, knowing that somebody has your back in the paint.

"Sometimes, when you don't feel that trust, you kind of hesitate and you go half way. You don't put yourself out there. That's the trust I'm talking about; you've got to do your job. I got to do my job. When they shot goes up, I've got to box out and get the rebound. They've got to trust that I'm going to get them the possession. That's the kind of trust that I'm talking about."

More: Why are there no NBA games today? League promotes the midterm vote

The Heat still have Erik Spoelstra roaming the sidelines. He's a basketball lifer who is widely considered to be the best active coach in the NBA, a coach who does not shy away from in-season adjustments. After the game, he expressed optimism that his group would figure it out, though he acknowledged it would take time.

Losses like these, in their own way, help by showing Miami what it cannot afford to do. Lessons can come from victories, too. The paradox about trust is that, in a sense, you have to let go of individual insecurities for a greater good. You have to yield. That is, of course, easier said than done.

"If you don't want to play defense as a team, you can't do it by yourself," Mitchell said after the game. "It's not surprising. We've got to have a better sense of urgency. We've got to be intentional about our situation and our game plan, and we've got to stick to it."

This article originally appeared on USA TODAY: Do Miami Heat have a fatal flaw? Giannis, Bam Adebayo issue warning

#2842. Green Bay Packers receive encouraging Micah Parsons update ahead of Patriots matchup

The Green Bay Packers found their footing again in Week 8, earning a 27-19 win over the Carolina Panthers. Their defense led the way, and the unit could receive another boost following the team's latest injury update.

Micah Parsons could soon take another step toward returning to the field. ESPN's Adam Schefter reported that Packers head coach Matt LaFleur said Monday the team is preparing for the star pass rusher to resume practicing this week. Parsons has not played since Green Bay's December 14, 2025, matchup with Denver, when he suffered a torn ACL. He has been focused on recovering from the injury ever since.

Having Parsons back for Sunday's matchup with the Patriots would give Green Bay a major lift. Still, his expected return to practice does not necessarily mean he will be cleared for game action right away. Reports have pointed to the Packers' Thanksgiving Eve meeting with the Rams as a more realistic target for his return.

Green Bay Packers defensive end Parsons (1), linebacker Edgerrin Cooper (56) and cornerback Keisean Nixon (25) team up to stop Detroit Lions running back Jahmyr Gibbs (0) on Sunday, September 7, 2025, at Lambeau Field in Green Bay, Wis. The Packers won the game, 27-13.Tork Mason/USA TODAY NETWORK-Wisconsin

The timing would work in Green Bay's favor, as the Packers have their bye week immediately before that matchup. A return to practice this week would give Parsons several weeks to build up before potentially being activated for Week 12. Although he would miss a significant portion of the season, he could still appear in seven regular season games. Even with that limited window, Parsons could provide a major boost as Green Bay pushes for a playoff spot.

Green Bay Packers defensive end Parsons (1) interacts with fans before a game against the Chicago Bears on Sunday, Oct. 11, 2026, at Lambeau Field in Green Bay, Wisconsin. The Packers won the game, 31-7.Tork Mason/Green Bay Press-Gazette

Green Bay enters the stretch run with a 5-3 record and remains in contention for the NFC North title, despite sitting fourth in the division. The Packers' Week 10 clash with the Minnesota Vikings could prove crucial as they look to strengthen their position in the NFC playoff race.

The Packers will be looking to secure an important win this weekend, regardless of whether Parsons is available.

Parsons and the Packers visit the Patriots on November 8, with kickoff set for 4:25 p.m. ET at Gillette Stadium.

#2838. The CW reportedly interested in licensing College Football Playoff games from ESPN

Over the last few years, The CW has grown to include a solid portfolio of sports rights. The network has ACC, Pac-12, and Mountain West football, ACC basketball, NASCAR's O'Reilly Auto Parts Series, WWE NXT, and a few other minor properties.

Now, the network is becoming more ambitious. As other media companies prepare to "rebalance" their sports rights portfolios to afford NFL rights when they reopen at the end of the decade, networks like The CW, Ion, and Versant's USA Sports division figure to become major players for the inventory that bigger networks leave behind. But few would expect these networks to land properties that are considered major.

The CW could be looking to change that perception. During an interview with Michael McCarthy in Front Office Sports, CW senior vice president of sports, Michael Perman, said the network is interested in licensing College Football Playoff games from ESPN when the field expands to 24 teams next season.

"We are interested. Obviously, we have ACC, Mountain West, Pac-12, which we launched in August. So we're excited about how that's going," Perman told FOS. "Yes, the CFP is interesting. There's a lot of games, it's a lot of money. …But we'd definitely be interested in doing more, or talking to ESPN, potentially about that."

Last month, reports surfaced that ESPN and the CFP were "nearing" a deal to expand the playoff to 24 teams beginning next season. Subsequent reports indicated that as part of its deal, ESPN could sublicense half of the newly created playoff inventory to another network if it chooses to do so. That potentially puts up to six new games on the market from the batch of additional first- and second-round games expansion creates.

The CW would seemingly like to kick the tires. The Nexstar-owned broadcast network and ESPN already have a partnership that sees the ESPN app serve as a streaming platform for all CW Sports content. The CW's ACC deal is also a sublicense from ESPN.

"That relationship is great. We talk to them all the time. So we're definitely open to adding more," Perman said of the network's ESPN partnership.

Whether ESPN would be open to sublicensing CFP games to The CW is a different story. The network would assuredly like to get top dollar for any CFP games it opts to license out, and there figures to be other interested parties.

TNT, now owned by Skydance, has a sublicense agreement for five games under the 12-team format in a deal that extends through the 2028 season. That agreement includes two first-round games (which will be second-round games in the expanded format), two quarterfinal games, and a semifinal game. Perhaps the network would like to add some of the new inventory to compliment its ongoing deal. Versant could also get involved, especially as the company's CEO said it will look to be opportunistic about adding sports rights during an earnings call on Tuesday. Or maybe ESPN will simply choose to keep the games for itself if it doesn't find any offers particularly attractive.

In addition to the CFP, Perman indicated that The CW will look towards sports rights that Skydance might shed as a result of the recent merger of CBS Sports and TNT Sports. Analysts expect Skydance to streamline its sports rights portfolio by letting ongoing deals expire without a renewal, or even selling ongoing rights to other broadcasters before those contracts end. The CW could be the beneficiary of those rights hitting the market.

"[T]hey definitely have some properties that are interesting. They have the French Open. Their NHL rights are up in a couple of years, which is something we're taking a look at," Perman said, later adding that Apple's MLS deal that expires in 2029 could also be of interest.

Perman's mention of the French Open is particularly notable given that TNT Sports' deal with the grand slam tennis tournament runs through 2034. This could be the first indication that Skydance is considering exiting that contract and is looking for a partner to offload the rights to.

Regardless of specifics, The CW is clearly positioning itself as an active buyer of sports rights in the coming years.

The post The CW reportedly interested in licensing College Football Playoff games from ESPN appeared first on Awful Announcing.

#2835. Warriors' Charles Bassey plays big role in win over Phoenix

SAN FRANCISCO – The Golden State Warriors' biggest concern has been the center position. Charles Bassey showed on Monday, Nov. 2 that he could be a solution for the Warriors with his 'next man up' mentality.

After losing Kristaps Porzingis, the Warriors have had to pivot with different lineups. Amid their five-game winning streak, they've thrown a few small ball lineups out with Draymond Green as the center.

But when Green was ejected from the Warriors' 116-113 win against the Phoenix Suns on Monday, Bassey stepped up and impacted the game with his high-energy and efficiency around the rim, just days after his 26th birthday.

Bassey finished the game with a career-high 19 points and shot 7-of-10 on field goals. He had 8 rebounds and finished with a plus-minus of +10.

"Next man mentality, to be honest. We knew we could get a win," Bassey said. "We started to the game slow.  We got to get better at doing that, you know, last couple games we started slow. We just gotta get rid of doing that. There was a mentality, we felt we could win. Like I said, next man, up, just go in and do your job. That's why I think every player on this team has confidence doing that and as long as you're on this team, you know, like I said, coach Steve (Kerr) gives you all that confidence."

The Warriors got off to a 31-19 deficit after the first quarter. Golden State came out shooting 33% on field goals and went 2-of-15 on 3-point attempts in the first quarter. However, Bassey's hustle stood out to everyone, especially his teammates.

"Bass like... I'm sure he didn't think he was going to play 23 minutes and he was ready for it," Warriors guard Stephen Curry, who scored a game-high 34 points, said about Bassey. "So those are the patterns of what a good team does when night after night, no matter what the combination is you're finding production up and down the bench, and you love it."

"Everybody feeds off the energy when a guy comes in like that with what Bass did tonight, changed the game," Curry added. "Everybody notices. It lifts everybody's spirits and keeps momentum on our side, so it was great."

More: Spike Lee hopes Knicks skip Trump White House visit despite Dolan's wishes

Brandin Podziemski scored 20 points in the win and had high praise for Bassey.

"I thought Charles was great," Podziemski said. "Super efficient, gave us a lob threat, gave us a defensive presence when Dray was out."

Warriors head coach Steve Kerr raved about Bassey after the Warriors' win. One of the things that stood out was the chemistry between Bassey and rookie forward Yaxel Lendeborg.

"Bass was great. What a big night for him," Kerr said. "The rebounding, the scoring. He and Yaxel have a really good connection. The pick and roll game. He gives him a lob threat and Yaxel sees the floor so well and he's so big that he finds him when he's behind the defense."

Bassey feeds off of playmakers like that. His efficiency around the rim makes teammates trust that he'll complete the play. Lendeborg finished the game with 16 points and five assists, four of them went to Bassey for a layup or dunk.

"Since training camp, I'm telling you, man, every time," Bassey said on his continuity with Lendeborg. "We talk about our game, every time. After we played on Sunday, he tells me, you are wide open. When he sees the pass he makes the right play. I love playing with him and I think the coaches, they figured that out too. I mean, everybody knew that."

They have a connection they've established as they've learned each other's game. When Bassey gives Lendeborg a look, he knows whether to set a ghost screen or clear the lane so he can get downhill.

Bassey can be reliable energy guy for the Warriors. A workhorse who provides an offensive spark through his efficiency and an anchor on defense. If comparing to Warriors teams of old, think Festus Ezeli, Kevon Looney, Andrew Bogut or Zaza Pachulia. He's going to scrap, claw and fight for possessions.

Bassey plays physical and the Suns didn't appreciate it. Further pushing and shoving ensued with Bassey after Green's ejection. Suns guard Jalen Green drove to the basket and got tied up with Bassey before Suns players came to Green's aide, including forward Miles Bridges, who was assessed a technical.

"Being physical doesn't mean you have to scuffle with guys, that doesn't mean you're tough" Bassey told USA TODAY Sports.

"Being physical is playing hard every possession, going out there, fighting for rebounds, and doing what I'm doing," he added. "Just being physical, you know, challenging every shot, and going out there you know, two or three guys trying to box me out, me on the offensive end. And that's being physical, still getting the rebound.  But I mean, what they're doing is so fake… That's a fake toughness."

Bassey, Nigerian-born, is no newcomer in the league. He was drafted with the 53rd overall pick of the 2021 NBA Draft by the Philadelphia 76ers out of Western Kentucky. He then bounced around the league, spending time in the G-League, including the Santa Cruz Warriors.

Bassey signed a one-year contract with the Warriors in July. He's looking to make the most of it.
